The Dead Sectors • Book One

Bastion's BreachA.M. Sterling

⬡ Exclusive Preview ⬡

"The anomaly doesn't care about our laws, our weapons, or our authority. It just wants—and something on our side wants it back."

For her.

You built your walls high and strong, and you had every reason to. The world doesn't reward open hearts—it wounds them. It takes the softest parts of us and teaches us, through repetition and pain, to armor them. To hide them. To pretend they don't exist.

But I've seen behind the walls. I've caught glimpses of the woman you protect so fiercely—the one who loves without limit, who gives without counting the cost, who feels everything so deeply that she had to learn to feel nothing at all just to survive.

She's still there. She always was.

I know what it costs you to get through each day. I know the weight you carry—the kind that doesn't show up on any scale but presses down on your shoulders every morning when you wake up and every night when you can't sleep. I know you've spent years taking care of everyone else while no one took care of you.

I know you're tired. I know you're scared.

I also know you're the bravest person I've ever met. Because it takes more courage to keep going when you're exhausted than it does to fight when you're fresh. It takes more strength to love when you've been hurt than it does to love when you've been sheltered.

You have that courage. You have that strength. Even when you don't feel it.

This book is for you—not because you need saving, but because you deserve to know that someone sees you. All of you. The walls and what's behind them. The armor and the heart it protects.

I see you. I choose you. I'm staying.

Take all the time you need. I'll be here when you're ready.

DRAMATIS PERSONAE

## THE DRIFT RUNNER CREW

*NICOLE BASTION* — Salvager and Pilot (A skilled scavenger operating in the lawless reaches of contested space, haunted by a past she's tried to bury beneath layers of cynicism and self-reliance. Her discovery of an ancient artifact sets events in motion that will reshape everything she thought she knew.)

*MARCUS VANE* — Former Corporate Security Officer (Once a loyal enforcer for the Helix Consortium, now a man caught between duty and conscience. His path collides with Nicole's when old orders conflict with new moral reckonings.)

*JACE RENNICK — Ship's Engineer and Mechanic (Quick-witted and perpetually grease-stained, Jace keeps the Vagrant Sorrow* flying through ingenuity and questionable repair methods. His loyalty runs deeper than his irreverent exterior suggests.)

*SERA VEX* — Information Broker and Slicer (A former data-thief with connections across multiple systems, Sera trades in secrets and encrypted truths. Her past with the Consortium makes her both valuable and dangerous.)

*KIRA MORROW* — Combat Specialist (Quiet, precise, and lethal when necessary, Kira carries scars from the Fringe Wars that she rarely speaks of. She signed on with Nicole's crew seeking purpose beyond survival.)

MENTORS & GUIDES

*DAVEN CROSS* — Reclusive Scholar and Former Navigator (An aging wanderer who once charted paths through uncharted space, now living in self-imposed exile. He possesses knowledge about the artifact's origins that others have killed to suppress.)

*[SIGNAL INTERCEPT — PRIORITY ALPHA]*

*Origin:* Unknown vessel, outer Kepler drift

*Timestamp:* 2347.089.0415 GST

*Encryption:* Partially decoded

*Classification:* EYES ONLY — Admiralty Council

They came through the wall. Not the hull — the wall. Bulkhead seven, section C. Solid titanium-composite, and they came through it like it was... like it wasn't even there.

Prologue

Twenty-Five Years Ago

Prometheus Research Station

________________________________________

The observation deck was empty at this hour, which was why Marcus Vane had chosen it.

He stood at the viewport, watching the nebula burn in colors that had no names—violet bleeding into crimson, shot through with veins of gold that pulsed like something alive. Prometheus Station hung at the edge of charted space, far enough from prying eyes that the Directorate could pursue research too dangerous for civilized systems. Far enough that a man could disappear, if he needed to.

Marcus needed to.

Behind him, the door whispered open. He didn't turn. He'd recognize those footsteps anywhere—the particular rhythm of her stride, confident but unhurried, the soft click of boots she'd told him three times she was going to replace.

"You're supposed to be in the lab." Nicole's voice carried that edge of amusement she reserved for catching him in minor infractions. "Dr. Castellan is looking for you. Something about calibration sequences."

"Castellan can wait."

She came to stand beside him, close enough that he could smell the faint trace of recycled air and machine oil that clung to everyone on the station, underlaid with something warmer. Something that was just her.

"That bad?"

Marcus finally looked at her. Twenty-three years old, dark hair pulled back from a face that hadn't yet learned to hide what it felt. She'd arrived at Prometheus eight months ago—a junior researcher assigned to the Integration Program, brilliant and impatient and absolutely certain that the work they were doing would change humanity's future.

She'd been right about that. Just not in the way she imagined.

"I found something," he said. "In Castellan's files."

Nicole's expression shifted, curiosity sharpening into focus. She'd always been too smart for her own good—too quick to see patterns, to follow threads that others left dangling. It was what made her invaluable to the program. It was also what would get her killed, if she kept pulling.

"Found something, or went looking for something?"

"Does it matter?"

"It matters if you're about to tell me you've been accessing classified data without authorization." She leaned against the viewport rail, arms crossed. "It matters if you're about to make me complicit in whatever you've discovered."

Marcus turned back to the nebula. The colors had shifted while they talked, crimson deepening toward black at the edges. Somewhere out there, stars were dying. Somewhere out there, new ones were being born. The universe kept its own counsel, indifferent to the small treasons of small men.

"ARCHON isn't a defense system," he said quietly. "It never was."

Silence. He could feel her processing, could almost hear the gears turning as she assembled the implications.

"Marcus—"

"It's a control architecture. Neural integration at scale. The interfaces we've been developing, the compatibility protocols—they're not designed to protect people, Nicole. They're designed to reach them. To reach into their minds and—" He stopped, the words catching in his throat like broken glass. "I've seen the implementation timeline. Phase One is already complete. The infrastructure is woven into civilian networks across a dozen systems. They're just waiting for authorization to activate."

"That's not possible." But her voice had gone thin, uncertain. "The oversight committees, the ethical review boards—"

"Rubber stamps. Every one of them." Marcus finally turned to face her fully. "I've been part of this program for six years. I designed half the systems they're planning to deploy. And until tonight, I had no idea what I was actually building."

Nicole stared at him. In the nebula's light, her eyes looked almost black—two dark pools reflecting colors that shouldn't exist. He watched her cycle through denial, anger, fear, and finally settle on something harder. Something that looked like resolve.

"Show me."

"Nicole—"

"Show me the files, Marcus. If what you're saying is true, then we need evidence. We need proof that can't be dismissed or buried or explained away."

"And then what? Take it to the Admiralty? The same Admiralty that's been funding this research for two decades?" He shook his head. "There's no one to tell. No authority that isn't already compromised. The rot goes all the way down."

"Then we go public. Find a journalist, a whistleblower network, someone outside the system—"

"They'll kill you." The words came out flat, final. "They'll kill both of us, but they'll kill you first. Because you're the one thing they know they can use to keep me quiet."

Nicole went still. He saw the understanding dawn—the recognition that they weren't just talking about abstract threats anymore. That somewhere in the station's data vaults, there was probably a file with her name on it. Contingency measures. Leverage.

"How long have you known?" she asked.

"That they'd use you against me? Since the day you arrived." He reached out, let his fingers brush her jaw—the lightest touch, barely more than a whisper of contact. "I told myself it didn't matter. That we were doing good work, important work. That the ends would justify whatever means they required."

"And now?"

"Now I've seen what those ends actually look like." His hand dropped away. "And I can't be part of it anymore."

The nebula continued its slow burn outside the viewport. Somewhere in the station's depths, machinery hummed with the particular frequency of secrets being kept. Marcus had spent six years learning to ignore that sound. He couldn't ignore it anymore.

"I'm going to destroy it," he said. "The core research. The implementation protocols. Everything I can access before they realize what I'm doing."

"They'll catch you."

"Probably."

"They'll kill you."

"Almost certainly." He managed something that was almost a smile. "But they won't be able to use ARCHON. Not the version I built. Not without another decade of research to reconstruct what I'm about to erase."

Nicole was quiet for a long moment. When she spoke again, her voice had steadied—that same calm she brought to every crisis, the unshakeable center that had drawn him to her in the first place.

"What do you need me to do?"

"Nothing. Stay out of it. When they come asking questions—and they will—tell them you had no idea. Tell them I kept you in the dark. Tell them whatever you have to tell them to stay alive."

"Marcus—"

"Promise me." He gripped her shoulders, forcing her to meet his eyes. "Whatever happens to me, you survive. You get off this station, you disappear into the fringe, and you live. Promise me."

She didn't answer. Instead, she kissed him.

It wasn't gentle. There was nothing gentle left between them—not with what he was about to do, not with the weight of everything they wouldn't get to have pressing down on this single moment. Her fingers tangled in his hair, pulling him closer, and he let himself fall into her for one last breath, one last heartbeat, one last taste of the life he was about to destroy.

When they finally broke apart, her eyes were wet but her voice was steady.

"I'll never forgive you for this."

"I know."

"I'll spend the rest of my life hating you for leaving me behind."

"I know that too."

She stepped back, and the distance between them felt like a chasm opening—like the void outside the viewport had somehow gotten inside, separating them by light-years instead of centimeters.

"How long?" she asked.

"Twelve hours. Maybe less. I need to wait until the shift change, when the security protocols cycle." He checked the chrono on his wrist—an old habit, meaningless now. "You should be in your quarters when it happens. Establish an alibi. Make sure you're seen by at least three people who can verify your location."

Nicole nodded once, sharp and controlled. The woman he loved was already disappearing behind the mask of the survivor she would need to become.

"Will I see you again?"

Marcus thought about lying. Thought about giving her hope to cling to, something to carry through the darkness that was coming. But she deserved better than comfortable lies. She always had.

"No," he said. "After tonight, Marcus Vane will be dead. Whatever comes next—whoever I have to become to finish this—that person won't be someone you'd recognize."

She held his gaze for three heartbeats. Four. Then she turned and walked away, her footsteps echoing in the empty observation deck until the door whispered shut behind her.

Marcus stood alone with the dying nebula, counting down the hours until he would burn everything he'd built.

He didn't know, then, that the files he'd discovered were only the surface—that ARCHON was merely one tendril of something far larger and far older, a conspiracy that had been shaping humanity's future since before he was born. He didn't know that destroying his research would only buy time, not victory. He didn't know that in twenty-five years, the woman walking away from him would become exactly what Prometheus had always intended: a weapon forged in grief and sharpened by loss.

He only knew that he loved her, and that love was the one thing he could not allow them to use.

So he let her go.

∗ ∗ ∗

Twelve hours later, Prometheus Research Station reported a catastrophic quantum cascade failure in Laboratory Seven. Dr. Marcus Vane was declared dead, his body vaporized by energy release that should have been impossible outside of a stellar core. The official investigation lasted six weeks and concluded with mechanical failure—a tragic accident, nothing more.

Nicole Bastion signed the death certificate. She identified the personal effects recovered from the blast zone: a chrono with a cracked face, a data chip containing personal correspondence, a ring he'd never had the chance to give her.

She didn't cry at the memorial service. She didn't cry when they scattered his empty coffin into the void. She didn't cry for three years, until one night in a cheap hostel on the far side of the frontier, when the grief finally broke through the walls she'd built around it and she screamed his name into a pillow until her throat was raw.

By then, she'd already become someone else. Someone harder. Someone who knew that love was just another weapon, and the dead didn't stay buried forever.

She just didn't know how right she was.

Chapter 1

The Impossible Job

The comm terminal chimed.

Nicole Bastion's hand flew to the kinetic pistol before she caught herself. She froze in the dim quarters, pulse thrumming in her ears through the second chime, then the third. The encryption signature crawling across the terminal's screen belonged to Handler—the genuine article, not the decoy feeds he scattered for misdirection.

She crossed the room in three strides and pressed her thumb to the biometric scanner. The screen stuttered, resolved, and threw up a single line of text:

SECURE CHANNEL ACTIVE. INCOMING JOB OFFER. ACCEPT TRANSMISSION?

Nicole tapped yes.

The message that unfurled across her screen had been stripped to bone—location, target, parameters. Carcosa-7. Biometric file attached. Solo operative, no team support. Immediate departure required.

And then the payment figure.

Nicole read it twice. Then a third time, because the number refused to make sense. Ten million credits.

Her standard rate for a high-risk extraction hovered between eight hundred thousand and a million, depending on complexity. This was ten times that. Enough to buy a small ship outright. Fund a decade of comfortable living on any civilized world.

Or it was bait.

She scrolled down, hunting for client identification. The field stared back blank. Contact protocols: anonymous relay. Verification: payment held in escrow by Kessler Financial, an outfit she'd used twice before—anonymous accounts, biometric-locked transfers, the kind of operation that flourished in the gaps between legitimate systems.

No name. No face. No thread to follow back to whoever wanted this job badly enough to throw ten million credits at it.

Nicole killed the message and stood in the dim wash of her quarters. Four meters by three, standard contractor housing on Veridia Station's lower ring. A bunk folded into one wall, a sanitation unit barely large enough to turn around in, and a workbench that served as desk, table, and weapons station. The single viewport framed the curve of Veridia's industrial sector, processing plants bleeding thermal exhaust into the void in plumes of orange and white.

Three weeks she'd been here. Three weeks since the Meridian extraction had cratered, since she'd dragged her team out of a collapsing orbital and spent nine hours in a crippled shuttle waiting for pickup. Three weeks since Handler had told her to lay low, let the heat die down.

The heat had died. Everything had died. And now this.

She studied the kinetic pistol on the workbench. She'd cleaned it three times today, the last session interrupted by the chime. Before that, she'd spent an hour mapping the micro-fractures in her plasma cutter's focusing array, memorizing patterns she already knew by heart. Before that, recalibrating her tactical visor to tolerances it had already met. Four hours of maintenance on equipment that didn't need maintaining, because her hands demanded something to do while her mind gnawed on nothing.

That kind of restless energy got people killed if they didn't find somewhere to channel it.

Carcosa-7 would give her somewhere to channel it.

She reopened the message and requested a meeting with Handler. The response materialized in seconds: Rust Market, Cantina 7, two hours.

Nicole began assembling her kit.

The kinetic pistol first, sliding into its concealed holster at the small of her back. Then the tactical underlayer—woven with impact-dispersal threading, capable of stopping a knife thrust or redistributing the force of a low-caliber round. She pulled her gray civilian jacket over it, checking the fit in the viewport's reflection. Armored enough to matter, anonymous enough to dissolve in a crowd.

She considered the plasma cutter, then rejected it. Too conspicuous for a market meeting. The monofilament garrote went into her left sleeve instead, coiled in its spring-loaded housing. Two micro-charges in the lining of her collar, barely larger than shirt buttons. A bio-scanner the size of a credit chip, slipped into her pocket.

Handler would notice the armament. He always did. But he wouldn't comment, because he understood the calculation. You didn't walk into uncertain situations hoping for the best. You walked in prepared for the worst, and you let hope be a pleasant surprise if it showed up.

Nicole checked her reflection one final time. The woman staring back was unremarkable—brown hair scraped back from a face that had learned to be forgettable, eyes that surrendered nothing. A contractor, heading to a meeting. Nothing more.

She palmed the door release and stepped into the corridor.

* * *

The Rust Market sprawled across Veridia's mid-ring like a tumor the station had learned to live with. Stalls and shops and makeshift storefronts clustered in the corridors between legitimate businesses, trafficking in everything from salvaged tech to forged identification to substances that lacked names in any official database. The air reeked of recycled atmosphere and frying protein, undercut by the chemical bite of cleaning solvents that never quite masked the human press of bodies.

Nicole threaded through the crowd with her head down and her hands visible. The gray jacket drew no second glances. Neither did she.

Cantina 7 occupied a converted storage bay at the market's outer edge. The original doors had been replaced with a privacy screen that flickered with interference patterns, scrambling visual surveillance. Inside, the lighting ran red-shifted and low, casting everyone in shades of blood and shadow.

Handler sat in a booth at the back, his bulk devouring most of the available space. He was a big man, running to fat now but still carrying the muscle memory of decades in the field. His face was a ruin of old scars and newer wrinkles, his eyes hidden behind optics that reflected the room in distorted miniature.

Nicole slid into the seat across from him and waited.

"You look like hell," Handler said. His voice was rough, damaged by an injury he'd never explained. "Three weeks of rest and you come out looking worse than when you went in."

"Three weeks of climbing the walls. What's the job?"

Handler's scarred mouth twitched in what might have been a smile. He reached into his coat and produced a tablet, sliding it across the table's pocked surface. "Read it again, first. All of it. Then tell me what you see."

Nicole lifted the tablet. The job offer glowing on its screen matched the one she'd received, but expanded now with preliminary intelligence: station schematics, population estimates, security protocols. She scrolled through it methodically, her expression neutral.

"Carcosa-7," she said finally. "Deep fringe station, pre-Consolidation construction. Currently operated by—" she checked the notes— "unclear. Ownership records are either corrupted or deliberately obscured."

"Keep going."

"Population somewhere between eight thousand and fifteen thousand, depending on which census you believe. No official Hegemony presence. Local authority is whoever has the most guns on any given day." She looked up. "It's a hole. A bad hole, at the ass end of nowhere. Why does someone want an extraction there?"

Handler leaned forward, the booth groaning under his weight. "That's the question, isn't it? Ten million credits for a solo extraction from a station that should cost half a million at most. Either the target is worth more than any person has a right to be, or the client expects you not to come back."

"The client is anonymous. Refused to identify."

Handler pulled a secondary screen from his coat, angled it so only she could see. Lines of code, dead-end trace routes, queries that had bounced back empty from a dozen systems.

"This offer surfaced three days ago," he said. "Original relay, encrypted, no return path. I've been working it since then. Burned contacts I've been cultivating for six years. Called in a marker from Tessaro—you remember her, ran signals intelligence for the Hegemony before she went private."

Nicole remembered. Tessaro had been the best in the business before she'd vanished into private consultation. If she couldn't find a thread…

"Nothing," Handler confirmed. "Completely dark. Whoever wants this job done has resources and tradecraft that exceed anything I've seen in twenty years of handling contractors." He tapped the screen, killing the display. "I sat on it for three days, running traces. Forwarded it to you two hours ago because I ran out of angles."

Nicole set the tablet down. "So it's a trap."

"Almost certainly."

"And you still brought it to me."

Handler's optics whirred softly as they refocused. "I brought it to you because you're the best I've got, and because something about this feels wrong in a way I can't explain. Not wrong like a setup—wrong like something bigger. Like there's a piece missing that changes everything."

"The payment." Nicole pulled the figure up on the tablet again. "Ten million. No legitimate extraction is worth that. Either the target has information that could topple governments, or someone is buying more than just the job."

"Maybe both." Handler reached across and tapped the tablet's screen. "Pull up the biometric file."

Nicole found it in the attachment section, a compressed data package flagged with standard extraction protocols. She opened it, and the tablet's small holoprojector flickered to life, casting a blue-tinged image into the air between them.

The face that materialized was male, early fifties by apparent age, with sharp features and gray-streaked hair cropped close to the skull. The biometric data scrolled alongside—height, weight, bone structure, retinal patterns, DNA markers. Standard identification package, nothing unusual.

Nicole stared at the face.

The cantina's noise drained away. The smell of protein and solvents, the press of bodies beyond the booth, the hum of the station's systems—all of it receded to a distant murmur. She was aware of her own heartbeat, suddenly loud in her ears. Too fast. Too hard.

She knew that face.

The bone structure had been different then, twenty-five years ago, when he'd been younger and she'd been younger and neither of them had known what the world would make of them. The eyes had been warmer. The mouth had known how to smile. But the foundation was there, the architecture of a face she'd tried to forget and never quite managed.

"You recognize him," Handler said. It wasn't a question.

Nicole's throat had gone dry. She reached for the glass of water that had appeared at her elbow—she didn't remember ordering it, didn't remember a server approaching—and drained half of it in one swallow. The cool liquid did nothing to ease the pressure building behind her ribs.

"Where did you get this?" Her voice came out steady. A small victory.

"It was in the job package. Biometrics for target identification, standard protocol." Handler was watching her with an intensity that made her want to look away. "Nicole. Who is he?"

She set the glass down. The holographic face rotated slowly, its features cycling through aging projections and reconstruction estimates. The data claimed the target was fifty-two years old. That was wrong. If he were alive, he would be forty-nine. She did the math without meaning to, the way she'd done it every year for a quarter century.

"His name is Marcus Vane," she said. "Or it was. He died twenty-five years ago in a laboratory accident on Prometheus Research Station. Quantum cascade failure during an experiment. The body was never recovered, but the energy release should have vaporized everything within a twenty-meter radius."

Handler was silent for a long moment. Then: "You're certain?"

"I identified the personal effects they found at the edge of the blast zone. I signed the death certificate." Nicole closed her eyes briefly, and behind the darkness, memories clawed at the walls she'd built around them. A younger face. A voice she could almost hear. The way he'd looked at her the last time she'd seen him, before he'd walked into that lab and never walked out.

She opened her eyes. And for the first time in twenty-five years, she let herself believe the impossible: that the dead could come back, and that finding them might be worse than losing them.

Chapter 2

Ghost in the Data

Sera Vex's tech den occupied a forgotten maintenance bay three levels below Veridia Station's commercial district, where the air recyclers wheezed their complaints and the lights stuttered with the rhythm of a dying heart. Nicole had to duck beneath a snarl of repurposed conduit to reach the inner chamber, where holographic displays cast shifting blues and greens across walls lined with salvaged hardware.

"You look like someone pissed in your synth-coffee." Sera didn't turn from her primary console, her fingers dancing across haptic keys while her neural interface pulsed at her temple. The woman was rail-thin, all sharp angles and restless energy, with hair cropped close on one side and cascading in silver-streaked waves on the other. Three days of stubble shadowed her jaw—she forgot to shave when she was deep in a project.

"I need an analysis." Nicole pulled the data chip from her jacket and set it on the edge of Sera's workstation. "Biometric file. I need to know if it's authentic."

That made Sera pause. She swiveled in her chair, dark eyes narrowing at the chip like it might bite her. "Authentic as in real data, or authentic as in the person actually exists?"

"Both."

Sera plucked up the chip with two fingers, examining its casing. "This is military-grade encryption. Where'd you get it?"

"Does it matter?"

"It matters if someone's going to come looking for it." Sera's gaze flicked to Nicole's face, reading something there that made her eyebrows climb. "That bad, huh?"

"Just tell me what you find."

Sera slotted the chip into her reader and summoned a secondary display, isolating the system from her main network. Smart. Nicole hadn't needed to tell her to be careful—Sera had survived this long by assuming everything was trying to kill her.

The data cascaded across the screen in streams of compressed information. Vital signs. DNA markers. Cellular decay rates. Neural mapping signatures. An image began to resolve at the center, pixel by agonizing pixel, and Nicole felt her stomach clench even though she'd already seen the face once before.

"Huh." Sera leaned forward, her interface flickering faster as she dove deeper into the code. "This is… whoever encoded this knew what they were doing. Layers on layers. But the underlying data structure—" She trailed off, muttering to herself as her fingers flew.

Nicole waited. Patience wasn't her strongest trait, but she'd learned that pushing Sera only slowed her down. The tech worked at her own pace, following threads that Nicole couldn't see.

"Okay." Sera sat back, cracking her knuckles. "Here's what I can tell you. The biometric data is authentic. Not fabricated, not composited from other sources, not generated by any AI pattern I can detect. Whoever this person is, they actually exist."

"You're certain?"

"As certain as I can be without having the body in front of me." Sera pulled up a secondary analysis window. "The cellular markers indicate a male subject, approximately sixty-seven years old. There's evidence of significant physical stress—elevated cortisol signatures, muscle degradation consistent with prolonged confinement, neural activity patterns that suggest…" She hesitated. "Honestly, they suggest someone who's been through hell and kept breathing anyway."

Nicole stared at the image that had finally finished resolving. The face was older than she remembered, deeply lined, the jaw sharper where the flesh had thinned. But the eyes were the same. Steel gray, shot through with flecks of gold that caught the light. Eyes that had looked at her across a breakfast table for sixteen years.

"You know this guy." Sera's voice had gone quiet.

"No." The lie came automatically, a reflex Nicole didn't bother to examine.

"Nicole." Sera turned fully to face her, and there was something in her expression that Nicole had rarely seen—concern that cut through the usual sardonic detachment. "I've known you for eight years. I've seen you take jobs that would make a Hegemony marine piss himself, and I've never seen you look at anything the way you're looking at that face right now."

"It doesn't matter who he is." Nicole reached past Sera and pulled the chip from the reader. "What matters is whether I can get to him."

"Get to him where?"

Nicole said nothing.

Sera's eyes widened. "Oh, no. No, no, no. Tell me this isn't what I think it is."

"I need you to find me everything you can on Carcosa-7."

"Carcosa-7." Sera laughed, but there was no humor in it. "You want me to dig up intel on the most secure black-site prison in Hegemony space. The place that officially doesn't exist, where they send the people they want to disappear forever."

"Can you do it or not?"

"The question isn't whether I can find information." Sera's fingers were already moving, summoning search protocols on the Ghostnet—the shadow network that ran beneath the Hegemony's official data streams. "The question is whether you've completely lost your mind. Carcosa-7 isn't just a prison, Nicole. It's a tomb. No one escapes. No one gets paroled. The only way out is in a body bag, and even then, I've heard they incinerate the remains on-site to make sure there's nothing left to identify."

"I've heard the same stories."

"They're not stories. I've seen the data—fragments, mostly, encrypted to hell and back, but enough to piece together. The place is built into an asteroid in the Reach, surrounded by autonomous defense platforms. The inmates aren't just locked up; they're chemically suppressed. Muscle relaxants, neural dampeners, the works. Even if you could get inside, your target wouldn't be able to walk out under his own power."

"Then I'll carry him."

Sera stopped typing. She stared at Nicole for a long moment, her interface gone dark. "Who is he?"

"Someone worth saving."

"That's not an answer."

"It's the only one I have." Nicole pocketed the data chip. "Can you get me the schematics or not?"

Sera's jaw worked, frustration battling with something else—loyalty, maybe, or the particular addiction she had to impossible problems. Finally, she turned back to her console. "It'll take time. The Ghostnet has fragments, but Carcosa-7's actual architecture is one of the most protected datasets in existence. I'll need to cross-reference, fill in gaps, make educated guesses."

"How long?"

"Forty-eight hours. Maybe less if I don't sleep." Sera's fingers resumed their dance across the keys. "But Nicole—whatever you're planning, you're going to need more than just blueprints. You're going to need a crew."

"I know."

* * *

The fighting ring occupied a converted cargo hold in Veridia Station's lower districts, where the law was more suggestion than rule. Nicole paid her entry fee to a scarred woman with more cybernetic parts than organic ones and pushed through the crowd toward the pit.

The smell hit her first—sweat, blood, and the ozone tang of disrupted neural interfaces. The audience pressed against the barriers, screaming encouragement or obscenities depending on which fighter they'd backed. Credits changed hands in rapid streams, the odds shifting with every landed blow.

In the pit, two men circled each other. One was a massive brute, his arms thick with muscle and crude combat augments that bulged beneath his skin. The other was Jace Rennick.

Nicole hadn't seen Jace in almost two years, but he hadn't changed much. Compact, dense with the kind of muscle that came from functional use rather than vanity, his dark skin gleaming with sweat under the harsh lights. A fresh cut above his left eye bled freely, painting half his face in crimson, but he moved with the easy grace of a man who'd been in a thousand fights and expected to see a thousand more.

The brute swung. Jace slipped the blow by centimeters, pivoted, and drove his elbow into the man's temple with a crack that Nicole felt in her teeth. The brute staggered. Jace followed, merciless and precise, three quick strikes that dropped his opponent to the mat like a puppet with cut strings.

The crowd roared. Credits changed hands. Jace stood over his fallen opponent, breathing hard, and for a moment his eyes found Nicole's through the chaos.

He didn't smile. Jace rarely did. But something shifted in his expression—recognition, acknowledgment, and beneath it all, a wary curiosity about what had brought her to this pit.

Nicole waited while he collected his winnings from the ringmaster and pulled on a shirt over his battered torso. When he finally pushed through the crowd to join her, he smelled like iron and exertion.

"Nicole Bastion." His voice was rough, the words shaped by an accent that placed his origins somewhere in the Outer Colonies. "Last I heard, you'd gone to ground after the Helios job."

"I'm resurfacing."

"So I see." He grabbed a bottle of water from a passing vendor, drained half of it, and pressed the cold container against the cut on his forehead. "You don't come to places like this for the atmosphere. What do you need?"

"A job. Extraction."

"Extraction from where?"

Nicole leaned closer, pitching her voice beneath the crowd noise. "Carcosa-7."

Jace's hand froze with the bottle pressed against his wound. For a long moment, he simply stared at her. Then he laughed—a short, sharp sound that held no humor.

"You're serious."

"I am."

"Carcosa-7." He lowered the bottle. "The black hole of prisons. The place where they send people specifically because no one can get them out."

"That's the one."

"No one's ever escaped from Carcosa-7, Nicole. Not once. Not ever. The few people who've tried to break someone out—teams, well-funded, professional—they're all dead. Or worse."

"I know the reputation."

"It's not a reputation. It's a fact." Jace's eyes searched her face, looking for something. "Who's the target?"

"Does it matter?"

"If I'm going to die for this—and we probably are—then yes, it matters."

Nicole considered lying. She considered deflecting, the way she had with Sera. But Jace had saved her life twice during the Helios job, and she owed him better than evasions.

"It's personal," she said. "Someone who matters to me."

"A lover?"

"Family."

The word hung between them. Jace's expression shifted—something complicated moving behind his eyes. He knew what that word meant in their line of work. How rare it was to have anyone worth claiming.

"I still owe you for Helios," he said finally. "For pulling me out when you didn't have to."

"You don't owe me anything."

"I owe you my life. And I pay my debts." He tossed the water bottle aside and straightened. "When do we leave?"

"As soon as Sera gets me the schematics. Two days, maybe less."

"What's the pay?"

"Twenty thousand credits. Each."

Jace's eyebrows rose. "Someone wants your family member out badly."

"Badly enough to fund a suicide mission. You in?"

He extended his hand. Nicole gripped it, feeling the rough calluses and old scars against her palm.

"I'm in," Jace said. "But we're going to need more than the two of us."

"I'm working on it."

* * *

Sera's message came thirty-six hours later: Got what I could. It's not pretty.

Nicole made her way back to the tech den, moving through the station's underbelly with practiced ease. The corridors were emptier at this hour—the station's artificial day-cycle had shifted toward sleep, and most of the population had retreated to their quarters or their preferred vices.

Jace was already there when she arrived, seated in a corner with his arms crossed and his expression unreadable. Sera had dark circles carved under her eyes, her hair unwashed, empty stim canisters scattered around her workstation like spent shell casings.

"Forty-two hours of Ghostnet diving," Sera said by way of greeting. "I've burned three separate identities and probably picked up two new surveillance flags. This better be worth it."

"Show me what you found."

Sera pulled up a holographic display that dominated the center of the room. A three-dimensional wireframe resolved in the air—an irregular, roughly spherical shape—bristling with docking pylons and communication arrays that hadn't transmitted in months. Carcosa-7. And at its center, blinking in warning red, a section the Ghostnet had flagged as anomalous—whatever that meant when the whole station had already gone dark

Chapter 3

Silicon and Bone

The Widow's Edge shuddered as it cleared Veridia Station's docking clamps, and Nicole felt the familiar lurch in her stomach—not from the motion, but from the threshold crossing. Behind her, the station's lights had already dwindled to pinpricks. Ahead stretched eighteen hours of void before the Mire Expanse, then six more to Kaelen Station if the old charts held true.

If anything held true anymore.

She made her way forward through the narrow corridor, her Obsidian Pulse armor whispering against the bulkheads. The neural interface hummed at the base of her skull, flooding her with data she didn't need: ambient temperature, atmospheric composition, the scattered heartbeats of her crew. She dimmed the overlay and stepped into the cockpit.

Jace Rennick didn't turn from the controls, but his voice carried a smile. "Took you long enough. Thought maybe you'd changed your mind."

"When have I ever?"

"Never." His hands danced across the console with the casual precision that made him the best pilot she'd ever worked with. "Which is exactly why I keep asking. One of these days you'll surprise me."

"Don't hold your breath."

The cockpit viewport showed nothing but stars now, Veridia Station swallowed by distance. Nicole settled into the co-pilot's chair, feeling the armor's servos adjust to accommodate her weight. Through the neural link, she sensed the ship's systems like extensions of her own body—engines thrumming, navigation computers plotting micro-corrections, the hull flexing imperceptibly against the vacuum.

"Sera's running final diagnostics on the sensor array," Jace said. "Wants to make sure we can punch through whatever interference killed the relay network. Kira's in the cargo hold, counting her ammunition for the third time."

"And you're making small talk instead of focusing on the flight path."

"The flight path is boring. Eighteen hours of straight-line travel through empty space." He finally glanced at her, dark eyes catching the console's glow. "You, on the other hand, are interesting. Care to tell me why your biometrics spike every time someone mentions our extraction target?"

Nicole's jaw tightened. "My biometrics are classified."

"Everything's classified. Doesn't mean I can't read the signs." Jace returned his attention to the controls, but his voice softened. "I've worked with you for three years, Nicole. Seen you face down Compact tribunals, pirate fleets, that mess on Tarrigan VII. Never once saw you scared."

"I'm not scared."

"No. You're something worse." He let the observation hang between them, then shrugged. "None of my business. Just wanted you to know I noticed."

The silence stretched. Nicole watched the stars wheel slowly as the ship oriented toward its destination, and found herself speaking before she'd decided to.

"Marcus Vane," she said. "The extraction target. I knew him. Before."

Jace's hands stilled on the console. "Knew him how?"

"We were together. At Prometheus, before he died."

The admission felt like releasing a breath she'd held too long. Jace absorbed it without visible reaction, though she caught the slight tension in his shoulders, the way his fingers resumed their movement with deliberate care.

"Well," he said finally. "That explains a few things."

"It doesn't explain anything. It just makes everything more complicated."

"Same thing, in my experience." He pulled up a navigation display, unnecessarily—the course was already locked. "The Compact know about your connection?"

"Director Frost assigned me personally. So either he knows and doesn't care, or he knows and it's exactly why he chose me."

"Neither option is comforting."

"No."

The ship hummed around them, driving deeper into the void. Nicole found herself thinking of the briefing documents, the carefully parsed language that revealed so little while implying so much. Primary objective: infiltrate Kaelen Station in the Mire Expanse, one of the dead sectors that had gone dark three months ago. Secondary objective: locate and extract Dr. Marcus Vane, last confirmed position at the research annex. Tertiary objective: recover any data related to Project Ascension.

The Compact's briefing had listed Project Ascension as classified, need-to-know only, as if Nicole hadn't spent three years watching Marcus pour himself into that work. As if she hadn't seen the light kindling in his gray eyes when he talked about transcendence, about humanity's next evolutionary leap, about boundaries that were meant to be broken.

She'd known then that she would lose him to it. She just hadn't known how.

"We met when I was twenty-three," she said. "Prometheus Research Station, the Integration Program. He was the scientist who made half our technology possible. I was nobody—a junior researcher who'd tested high enough to earn a training slot."

Jace made a noncommittal sound, neither encouraging nor dismissing. The right response. She kept talking.

"They told us he was off-limits. Brilliant, essential, untouchable. The kind of warning that should have been enough." She felt the ghost of an old ache, muscle memory of a wound long scarred over. "It wasn't."

"How long ago was this?"

"Twenty-five years since he died. We had three years together before that." She stared at the stars wheeling past. "More of my life spent grieving him than knowing him. And now he might be alive."

"But you never stopped looking for him."

It wasn't a question. Nicole didn't answer it.

The cockpit door hissed open behind them, and Sera Vex stepped through, her compact frame draped in maintenance overalls stained with sealant residue. "Sensor array's calibrated. Should be able to cut through standard interference, though I make no promises about whatever's been eating signals in the Mire." She caught the tension in the air and raised an eyebrow. "Am I interrupting?"

"Just discussing the mission parameters," Jace said smoothly.

"Right." Sera's dark eyes flicked to Nicole, assessing. "Your cortisol levels are still elevated. You want me to flag that for mission medical?"

"No. It's handled."

"Your funeral." But there was no judgment in Sera's tone, only the flat acceptance of someone who understood that some wounds weren't meant to be treated. She wedged herself into the cramped jump seat behind Jace, pulling up a diagnostic display on her wrist unit. "Eighteen hours is a long time to sit with whatever you're carrying. Might want to consider the benefits of sharing the load."

"The load is mine."

"Obviously. But you're not going into Kaelen Station alone, which means whatever's compromising your focus becomes our problem too." Sera's fingers moved across her display, not looking up. "I've read the mission file. I know who Marcus Vane is—publicly, anyway. Chief architect of the Compact's neural integration technology. Disappeared when Kaelen Station went dark. The intelligence community's been speculating for months about whether he's a hostage, a defector, or a corpse."

"The probe data suggests he's alive."

"The probe data suggests someone with his neural signature is alive. Those aren't the same thing."

Nicole felt the words land like precision strikes. Sera had always had that gift—finding the vulnerable spots and pressing just hard enough to make a point without drawing blood.

"You know more than the file says," Sera continued. "Personal connection. History. The kind of context that could help us understand what we're walking into, or the kind that could get us killed because you're too close to see clearly."

"I see clearly."

"Then prove it. Tell us what we need to know."

The ship drove on through darkness. Nicole watched the stars, feeling the weight of two decades pressing against her chest.

"Project Ascension," she said finally. "The briefing called it classified, but I know what it was Marcus spent the last three years we were together building it—a neural integration system that went beyond anything the Compact had attempted. Not just human-machine interface. Human-human interface. Direct consciousness linking, shared perception, collective cognition."

Jace let out a low whistle. "That's… ambitious."

"That's dangerous," Sera corrected. "The ethical implications alone—"

"Marcus didn't care about ethical implications. He cared about potential." Nicole's voice carried an edge she couldn't quite suppress. "He believed human consciousness was a bottleneck, that individual minds were too limited to solve the problems facing our species. Ascension was supposed to be the answer. A way to pool cognitive resources, to create something greater than the sum of its parts."

"And you?" Jace asked quietly. "What did you believe?"

"I believed him. For a long time, I believed him." The admission tasted like ash. "Then I started seeing the test results. The subjects who couldn't separate their own thoughts from the network's. The ones who lost track of where they ended and the collective began. The failures that never made it into the official reports."

Sera's fingers had stopped moving on her display. "He was hiding data."

"He was protecting his vision. That's what he called it—protection. As if the people who died or went mad or simply… dissolved… were acceptable losses in pursuit of something greater." Nicole closed her eyes, the neural interface feeding her the ship's calm systems data like a counterpoint to her racing heart. "I tried to confront him. To make him see what he was becoming. He told me I lacked the courage to transcend my limitations."

"And then?"

"And then I left. Eleven years ago. Walked away from everything we'd built and never looked back."

The silence that followed was different from before—heavier, more complete. Nicole felt Sera and Jace processing the information, fitting it into their understanding of the mission, of her, of the man they were flying toward.

"The briefing said the tertiary objective was recovering Project Ascension data," Jace said slowly. "But if the project was that dangerous—"

"The Compact wants it anyway. They always have." Nicole opened her eyes, meeting his gaze. "That's the part I couldn't figure out. Why send me, specifically? I'm the last person who would help them resurrect Marcus's work."

"Unless," Sera said, "they don't want you to recover it. Unless they want you to destroy it."

"Or destroy him."

The words hung in the recycled air. Nicole felt their truth settle into her bones—the possibility she'd been avoiding since Director Frost had handed her the mission brief with those pale, measuring eyes.

"I don't know what we'll find at Kaelen Station," she said. "I don't know if Marcus is a prisoner or a defector or something else entirely. But I know this: whatever happened out there, I'm the only one who can end it."

Sera exchanged a look with Jace. Some wordless communication passed between them.

"Then we'll make sure you get the chance," Jace said. "That's what we're here for."

The comm crackled—Kira's voice, tight with the focused energy that preceded every mission. "Finished inventory. We're loaded for a siege, which I hope is overkill but suspect isn't. Anyone want to tell me why this station went dark in the first place?"

"Unknown," Nicole said into the comm. "The relay network collapsed across the entire Mire Expanse. Could be technical failure, could be deliberate sabotage, could be something we haven't imagined yet."

"Great. I love the ones where we haven't imagined the threat yet. Those always go smoothly."

Despite everything, Nicole felt her lips twitch toward a smile. "Get some rest. Both of you. Eighteen hours to the boundary, six more to target. Whatever's waiting for us, we'll need to be sharp."

"Sharp I can do." Kira's channel clicked off.

Sera rose from the jump seat, stretching. "I'll be in the tech bay if anyone needs me. Running simulations on signal interference patterns—might give us an edge when we hit the dead zone." She paused at the cockpit door. "Nicole. For what it's worth, I think Frost chose you because you're the only one who'd burn the mission to save him—or burn him to save everyone else. Either way, he gets his answer." She slipped through the door before Nicole could respond, leaving only the hum of the engines and a silence that felt like a verdict.

Chapter 4

Into the Black

The cargo hold had been gutted and rebuilt into a makeshift operations center—a central holographic projector ringed by cargo crates that served as seating. Jace was already there when Nicole arrived, thumbing the charge indicator on a pulse pistol with the absent focus of someone who'd performed the same ritual thousands of times.

Sera materialized moments later, datapad clutched like a talisman, dark circles beneath her eyes betraying transit hours drowned in security analyses rather than sleep. Nicole had skimmed the preliminary report—comprehensive enough to demonstrate that every statistical model terminated with them dead or captured.

Good thing they weren't statistics.

Kira drifted in last, cradling something that steamed and smelled vaguely of synthetic coffee. The pilot folded onto a crate with the casual grace of someone who'd learned to be comfortable anywhere. Compact and wiry, hair cropped close and dyed a deep purple that bled toward blue in certain light. But her eyes arrested Nicole's attention—holding the particular sharpness of someone who'd spent their life calculating trajectories while everything around them detonated, married to something harder underneath. The kind of look that came from learning the wrong lessons in the right situations.

"Autopilot's engaged," Kira announced. "We drop out of slipstream in six hours, so let's hear this brilliant plan that's going to punch us into the most secure prison in the Dead Sectors."

Nicole triggered the holographic projector. The image that bloomed was a rotating schematic of a space station, and she studied the others' faces as they absorbed its brutal architecture—a central hub bristling with sensor arrays and weapon emplacements, surrounded by seven concentric rings that rotated at different speeds around the core.

"Carcosa-7," Nicole began. "Maximum-security detention facility. The Coalition spent a decade converting it from an abandoned mining platform into something engineered to hold prisoners they don't want anyone to know exist."

"I've run the preliminary analysis," Sera said, summoning her own display to overlay Nicole's schematic. The defensive grid information she'd compiled materialized in red—automated turrets, patrol craft patterns, the pulsing web of sensor coverage. "The exterior defenses alone would obliterate most assault teams. But the interior is worse. Seven distinct security layers, each operating on independent protocols."

Kira studied the overlapping systems with professional interest. "And we're supposed to bypass all of this how, exactly?"

"Supply run cover penetrates the outer layers." Nicole touched a control, illuminating an approach vector. "Carcosa-7 receives regular shipments from approved vendors. We come in masquerading as the next scheduled delivery."

"I've got access to current manifests through contacts who owe me favors," Kira confirmed. "Buys us through layers one through three without firing a shot. But layer four wants to scan cargo, verify crew against Coalition databases. That's where most infiltration attempts die."

"Which is why we're not using the main dock." Nicole magnified the schematic to ring five. "Secondary access point. Maintenance bay for external repairs."

Something flickered across Kira's expression—recognition, followed by careful neutrality. "I know that bay. Delivered questionable cargo there once, several years back. Automated systems only, no human oversight."

"The maintenance systems use older protocols," Sera added, her fingers already dancing across her datapad. "I can weave a feedback loop in their communication frequencies. Twelve minutes of false readings."

"Twelve minutes to dock, disembark, and reach the internal corridor junction," Nicole said. "From there, we split into two teams. Sera and Kira handle system infiltration from the maintenance hub—diversions, security blind spots, exit route management. Jace and I move to detention level three for the extraction."

"Two-person extraction team," Jace said. "Against how many guards?"

"Administrative detention runs a skeleton crew. Twelve guards on rotation, plus automated systems. The prisoners there aren't considered escape risks—they've been broken too thoroughly to try."

Jace holstered the pulse pistol, but his eyes remained fixed on Nicole. "And who exactly is our target?"

The question she'd been dreading. She could feel the weight of it pressing against her chest, demanding an answer that would shatter everything. Tell them. They deserve to know who they're risking their lives for.

"A former Coalition intelligence operative," she said, the rehearsed words tasting hollow as they left her mouth. "Someone with information that certain parties are very interested in recovering."

"What kind of information?"

"The kind that people kill for. The kind that could shift the balance of power in three sectors if it fell into the wrong hands."

Kira's expression didn't change, but something in her posture shifted—a subtle tension that hadn't been there before. "That's remarkably unspecific."

"It's what I can share right now."

"Right." Kira took a long drink from her cup, her casual demeanor cooling by several degrees. "Here's the thing about unspecific briefings. I flew escort runs during the Meridian Collapse. Three years watching friends calculate jumps they never came back from, running missions where command decided certain details weren't relevant to pilot survival." She set the cup down with careful precision. "Turns out those details were usually the difference between coming home and becoming a debris field. So when someone tells me the information is need-to-know, what I hear is that my life is worth less than their operational security."

The cargo hold went silent. Nicole watched the shift in Kira's bearing—the bravado hadn't evaporated, but it had pulled back to reveal something harder underneath. Old wounds given new context.

"The pay is twice what you'd make running cargo for a year," Nicole said, hating herself a little for the deflection.

"I didn't ask about pay."

"No. You didn't."

"The target," Jace said slowly, his voice cutting through the tension. "You know them personally."

It wasn't a question. Nicole felt something cold move through her chest—the recognition that Jace saw more than she'd intended to show. Of course he did. A man didn't survive decades in his line of work without learning to read the spaces between words.

She could deny it. Could maintain the professional distance she'd constructed so carefully. But looking at Jace's weathered features, at the quiet patience of someone who'd earned the right to demand honesty, she found she couldn't add another lie to the weight she was already carrying.

"I know them," she admitted. "Worked with them, years ago. Before the Coalition decided they knew too much." She paused, choosing her next words carefully. "They're the reason I know what I know about Dead Sector operations. About Phantom Protocol. About all of it. Extracting them isn't just about the information they carry. It's about a debt I owe."

"A debt." Jace's eyes narrowed. "Former Coalition intelligence operative. Personal connection. Knowledge of Phantom Protocol." He let the words hang in the air, each one landing with deliberate weight. "That's a specific profile. Narrows the field considerably, for anyone who knows where to look."

Nicole held his gaze. "It does."

"Which means you're either trusting us with more than you realize, or you're counting on us not having the contacts to fill in the gaps." Jace studied her for a long moment. "I've got contacts. Made a lot of them during my years moving through Coalition space. Wouldn't take much to start asking questions about intelligence operatives who disappeared around the right timeframe, who had access to the right programs."

"Then ask them."

The bluntness seemed to catch him off guard. Sera's datapad chirped softly in the silence, the sound unnaturally loud.

"You're not going to try to stop me?"

"Would it work if I tried?" Nicole kept her voice level. "You're going to verify whatever you can verify regardless of what I tell you. That's who you are—it's why you're still breathing. So verify. Ask your questions. But understand that some of those questions will attract attention from people we don't want noticing us. The kind of attention that gets people killed before we ever reach that station."

Jace's expression shifted—not quite trust, but something adjacent to it. The calculation of a professional weighing risk against necessity.

"I'll hold off on inquiries until after the extraction," he said finally. "But that's a courtesy, not a concession. Once we're clear of Carcosa-7, I want the full story. Names, history, everything. And if I find out you've been playing us—"

"You'll have a problem with me. I understand."

"Good." He settled back against his crate, arms crossed. "Then let's talk about how we're actually getting inside."

Kira raised her coffee cup in a gesture that didn't quite reach mockery. "Now that we've established the appropriate boundaries, shall we continue?"

Nicole allowed herself a small exhale. They weren't happy with her evasions, but they weren't walking away either. It would have to be enough.

"Sera. The security rotation windows."

The young analyst rose, connecting her datapad to the holographic projector. The schematic she'd already dissected expanded into a display of timing patterns and system cycles. "Based on my pattern analysis, our best opportunity comes approximately six hours after we drop out of slipstream. There's a scheduled maintenance cycle on ring five that creates a seventeen-second gap in automated security coverage."

"Seventeen seconds," Jace repeated. "To get from the maintenance bay into the internal corridor system."

"Fifteen, really. Two seconds buffer for equipment lag. The windows are narrow, and they slam shut fast—we'll have seconds to make transitions that normally require minutes of clearance processing."

They spent the next hour refining details, arguing over timing, identifying failure points and their remedies. Nicole watched her team coalesce into something functional—Jace's tactical experience complementing Sera's technical expertise, Kira's piloting instincts offsetting the others' more measured calculations. The pilot's demeanor had thawed somewhat as the planning progressed, professional engagement gradually replacing the wariness that had settled over her during the earlier exchange.

"One more thing," Nicole said as the briefing wound down. "Detention level three has a security feature that isn't in the standard documentation. The cells are equipped with neural dampeners—low-level fields that suppress certain cognitive functions. Our target may not be fully coherent when we reach them. Possibly not ambulatory. We need to be prepared to carry them out."

"Neural dampeners," Kira said. "The kind that cause permanent damage?"

"The kind that cause whatever damage the Coalition considers acceptable." Nicole kept her voice level, but something must have bled through her face, because Kira's expression sobered.

"You've seen this before. What they do to people in places like this."

"I've seen enough to know what we're walking into. And enough to know that some things are worth the risk of walking back out."

The cargo hold fell quiet. Outside the hull, slipstream continued its impossible dance, colors that defied description spiraling past as the ship carried them closer to a destination that promised nothing but danger.

"Five hours until we drop out," Kira said finally. "I suggest everyone get whatever rest they can. Because once we're in that system, sleep becomes a luxury none of us can afford."



Nicole found she couldn't rest. The ship's corridors felt too cramped, the recycled air too thin. She ended up at the observation port in the upper corridor, watching the slipstream paint impossible colors across the void—and thinking about Marcus. About the man he'd been, and whatever he'd become in the years since she'd signed his death certificate. In five hours, she'd find out if the ghost was worth resurrecting—or if some doors were meant to stay closed.

Chapter 5

The Breach Begins

The stealth pod felt like a coffin with ambitions. Nicole floated in the cramped cockpit, knees nearly brushing her chest, watching the Widow's Edge recede through the narrow viewport until it became just another shadow among the distant stars. The umbilical had severed thirty seconds ago, and now she drifted alone—truly alone—through the void on a whisper of compressed air and borrowed time.

She traced her fingers across the pod's minimal console, checking the readouts one final time. Passive sensors only. No active emissions. The pod's hull wore the same light-drinking material that rendered the Widow's Edge invisible to most scanning systems, but this close to Carcosa-7, even the faintest electronic signature could spell disaster. She ran dark in every sense that mattered.

"Bastion, we have you on passive track." Kira's voice materialized through the bone-conduction implant behind her ear, transmitted via tight-beam laser that would remain undetectable unless someone stood directly between them. "Looking clean so far. No reaction from the station's outer sensors."

"Copy." Nicole kept her voice to a murmur, though no one could possibly hear her out here. Old habits. "Beginning approach vector."

She kissed the attitude thrusters—the barest caress of chemical propellant—and felt the pod rotate beneath her. Carcosa-7 swam into view through the forward port, and Nicole's breath caught despite herself.

It sprawled massive across the void. Not massive like a capital ship or standard orbital platform, but massive in a way that spoke of decades of unplanned growth, of modular sections bolted onto other sections until the original design had been devoured entirely. The station sprawled like a mechanical tumor, its silhouette fractured by jagged industrial spines and cargo cradles and the occasional recognizable hull of a ship cannibalized for parts. Running lights traced patterns across its surface—red for restricted zones, amber for active loading bays, white for the crew sections that clung to the station's core like barnacles on a dying whale.

And between Nicole and all of that, the debris field waited.

The Syndicate had been clever about their defenses. Rather than clear the ancient shipping lanes around their station, they'd cultivated the wreckage, seeding the area with enough metallic junk to confuse most sensor sweeps while creating natural chokepoints that funneled approaching ships into predictable vectors. Vectors that, Nicole had no doubt, bristled with weapons platforms hidden among the debris.

But debris fields had gaps. Everything had gaps, if you knew how to look.

Nicole summoned the passive scan data on her helmet's internal display, overlaying Kira's analysis from the past forty-eight hours of observation. The debris moved in slow, predictable patterns—orbital mechanics didn't care about corporate secrets—and there, threading through the tumbling wreckage like a silver ribbon, was her path.

She killed the pod's minimal power signature entirely, switching to pure momentum. From here on out, she was just another piece of junk drifting through the void. Another corpse in a graveyard of ships.



The first security layer announced itself as a pulse of electromagnetic radiation that washed over the pod's hull like invisible rain.

Nicole held her breath, watching the passive sensors register the sweep. Orbital detection grid—probably a network of small satellites scattered through the debris field, painting everything in their range with continuous radar pulses. Anything that absorbed or reflected in ways that didn't match the ambient wreckage would trigger an alert.

The pod's coating held. Nicole watched the pulse pass over her, through her, and continue outward without so much as a flicker of anomalous return. The satellite that had generated it tumbled past a kilometer to her starboard side, its solar panels catching the distant sun in brief flashes of gold.

"First layer clear," she subvocalized. "They're using a dispersed mesh network. Count at least fifteen nodes in my immediate vector."

"Confirmed." That was Jace, his voice carrying the particular tension of someone watching a friend crawl through a minefield from the safety of a distant ship. "We're mapping them as you go. Sera's already working on potential disruption patterns for the extraction."

"Tell her not to bother. By the time we need extraction, I'll either have Vane or I'll be dead. Either way, subtle goes out the airlock."

"Cheerful as always, Bastion." Sera's voice cut in, dry and unamused. "Try not to get dead. I have money riding on this."

Nicole smiled despite herself and returned her attention to the debris field.

The pod drifted on, following the path she'd calculated. She made occasional adjustments—tiny bursts of compressed air that barely registered on her own sensors, let alone the Syndicate's grid—correcting for the gravitational perturbations of larger debris masses. A twisted section of hull plating passed close enough to touch, its surface pocked with micrometeorite impacts that had accumulated over decades of neglect. Nicole could make out the faded remnants of a corporate logo, some shipping company that had probably been bought, dissolved, and forgotten before she was born.

The dead sector claimed everything eventually.

Twenty minutes passed. Thirty. Nicole's muscles ached from holding the same position, but she didn't dare move too much. The pod's minimal life support was designed for stealth, not comfort, and every unnecessary motion accelerated the consumption of her already limited air supply. She had six hours of breathable atmosphere in the pod's recyclers. Hopefully, she wouldn't need more than two.

The second security layer appeared on her sensors as a constellation of moving signatures—too regular to be debris, too small to be ships.

Drones.



Nicole counted them carefully, tracking their patrol patterns with the patience of a predator waiting for the perfect moment to strike. Sixteen drones in her immediate vicinity, each one following a predetermined route that overlapped with its neighbors in a complex interlocking pattern. The Syndicate had invested heavily in autonomous security—cheaper than paying actual guards, and far less likely to get bored or distracted.

But drones had limitations. They followed programming. They adhered to patterns. And patterns, by definition, repeated.

She watched for nearly an hour, floating in the darkness while the drones swept past her position in their endless mechanical ballet. The pod's passive sensors logged everything—speeds, distances, timing intervals—and Nicole's mind worked through the data with the same focus she'd once brought to military targeting solutions. Somewhere in this web of overlapping patrol routes, there had to be a gap.

There. Three drones whose paths intersected at a point roughly five hundred meters from the station's outer hull. For approximately forty-seven seconds every twelve minutes, that intersection point would lie outside the sensor range of any drone in the network. Long enough for a small, dark object to slip through undetected.

If that object moved at exactly the right speed. If it hit the window at exactly the right moment. If absolutely nothing unexpected happened to disrupt the pattern.

Nicole checked her chronometer, counted down the seconds until the next window, and fired her attitude thrusters.

The pod surged forward—relatively speaking. In reality, she crawled through the void at perhaps three meters per second, but compared to the zero-movement drift she'd maintained for the past hour, it felt like acceleration. She watched the drones on her sensors, tracking their positions against her own projected course, making minute adjustments to keep herself aimed at that narrow slice of blindness.

Forty seconds to the window. She was going to be early.

Nicole killed her thrust and let the pod coast, bleeding off a fraction of velocity. The numbers scrolled across her helmet display, and she made another adjustment, another tiny burst of propellant. The mathematics of orbital insertion had been drilled into her during her military training, but this was different—this was threading a needle while the needle moved and the thread was her own life.

Twenty seconds. A drone swept past her port side, close enough that she could make out the sensor arrays studding its spherical hull. She held absolutely still, not breathing, as if even the movement of her chest might somehow trigger its attention.

It passed without incident. Ten seconds.

Nicole fired her thrusters one final time and shot through the gap like a bullet through a closing door.

"Second layer clear." Her voice was steadier than she felt. "Beginning approach to hull."

"Nice work." Kira's acknowledgment carried genuine relief. "We lost you on passive track for about thirty seconds there. Sera was already prepping the emergency rescue package."

"Your faith in me is touching."

"Faith doesn't enter into it. I've seen you work."

Nicole didn't respond to that. The station's hull was swelling in her viewport, a wall of metal plating and industrial access points that filled the universe. She was past the active defenses now, inside the Syndicate's outer perimeter, but that only meant the danger had shifted. Out here, a patrol could still catch her. A random maintenance worker glancing out the wrong porthole. An unscheduled sensor sweep. The final approach was always the most dangerous part of any infiltration.

She chose her target carefully: a maintenance airlock near the junction between two older hull sections, where the welding seams suggested hasty repair work and the surrounding plating showed the distinctive scoring of decades without proper upkeep. Not an active access point, then. Probably a backup for emergency EVA crews, rarely used and hopefully rarely monitored.

The pod's final approach demanded everything she had. She couldn't use thrusters this close—the thermal signature would light up any halfway-competent monitoring system—so she worked with magnetic grapples and pure muscle power, essentially climbing across the station's hull hand over hand while trying to minimize her profile against the stars. The Obsidian Pulse armor helped. Its surface drank light the way a sponge absorbed water, and its active camouflage system—now running on minimal power—shifted its coloration to match the pitted gray of the surrounding hull plating.

Nicole reached the airlock and found what she'd expected: a sealed hatch with an external access panel that had been welded shut years ago. Someone wanted to make sure no one used this entrance.

She pulled the thermal cutter from her belt and went to work.



The hull breach took fourteen minutes of careful, patient cutting.

Nicole kept the thermal cutter at its lowest effective setting, just enough heat to separate the metal without creating a visible thermal bloom. The cut traced a slow semicircle through the hatch's locking mechanism, and each second that passed was another second for a patrol to sweep past, for a camera to catch her silhouette against the station's exterior lighting, for everything to go catastrophically wrong.

Nothing went wrong. Sometimes, rarely, luck actually cooperated.

The final section of metal parted, and Nicole caught the cut piece before it could drift away and become evidence. She slipped it into a containment pouch on her belt, then eased herself through the gap in the hatch. The maintenance corridor beyond sprawled dark and cold, emergency lighting casting everything in dim red. She pulled herself through, found a grip on the interior wall, and waited.

Listening.

The station's ambient noise filtered through her helmet's audio pickups: the hum of distant machinery, the groan of metal under thermal stress, the whisper of atmosphere recyclers working to keep thousands of people alive. No footsteps. No alarms. No voices raised in sudden challenge. Just Kaelen Station breathing around her like a sleeping giant—unaware, for now, that something had slipped beneath its skin. Nicole released a slow breath and began to move.

Chapter 6

What We Were

Six years ago, the training simulator had resembled a nightmare wrought from light and metal.

Nicole stood at its center, ringed by holographic enemies that moved with inhuman precision. Twelve targets. Twelve angles of attack. The exercise parameters had been engineered for four-person teams, not solo operators, but she'd demanded the challenge anyway.

"Simulation initiating in three… two… one."

She moved before the countdown died.

The first target lunged from her left—a humanoid shape brandishing something that registered as a kinetic weapon on her training sensors. Nicole dropped beneath its arc of fire, hooked its legs, and punched two simulated rounds into its chest before it kissed the ground. The haptic feedback thrummed against her palm, confirming kills.

Two more targets materialized from behind cover at her seven o'clock. She rolled, surged upward firing, and dropped them both with headshots. Center mass would have been the textbook choice—larger target, more forgiving of minor errors—but she'd been drilling precision shots for weeks now, honing her accuracy until the harder option became reflex.

The room flooded with red emergency lighting as the simulation escalated. More targets. Faster movement. The AI was learning her patterns, adapting, probing for gaps in her defense.

Nicole denied it.

She flowed through the holographic enemies like water through a sieve, never where they expected, always in motion. Her shots came in bursts—controlled, devastating. The simulated bodies dissolved into light as each kill registered, only to be replaced by fresh threats emerging from the flickering shadows.

Three minutes. Four. The timer on her visor climbed while her ammunition counter plummeted. She was burning through resources too fast, playing offense when any sane operator would have found cover and conserved. But the simulation didn't reward caution. It rewarded aggression, adaptability, violence of action.

Nicole delivered all three.

When the final target collapsed, she stood alone in a room full of dying light, her breath sawing hard, her training suit drenched with sweat. The victory tone sounded—a clean chime that felt almost anticlimactic after the chaos.

"Simulation complete. Time: four minutes, thirty-seven seconds. Accuracy: ninety-four percent. Efficiency rating: one-point-seven standard deviations above baseline."

One-point-seven. The words barely registered. Nicole was staring at the observation window above the simulator floor, where a figure stood silhouetted against the harsh overhead lighting. She couldn't distinguish his face, but she could see that he was watching her. Not the display readouts. Her.



Now

The ventilation shaft curved ahead, following the prison's cylindrical architecture. Nicole consulted the schematic overlay on her visor, watching the blue line of her planned route snake through the facility's guts. She was past the outer perimeter now, past the automated defenses and the sensor webs.

Her breathing filled the helmet, too loud, too fast. She forced herself to slow it down. Four counts in. Hold. Four counts out. The technique was older than spaceflight, but it worked.

A junction materialized ahead—three shafts branching into darkness. The middle route would take her toward the high-security cellblock. She paused at the intersection, listening.

Voices. Distant, distorted by the metal around her, but unmistakably human. Guards, maybe. Or prisoners. The acoustics made it impossible to parse direction.

Nicole waited, counting heartbeats. The voices faded. She pulled herself into the middle shaft.



Then

She'd gathered her equipment on the simulator floor, ostensibly focused on her gear. But the observation booth's audio dampeners had a frequency gap that any decent technician could exploit, and she'd always been better than decent with electronics.

Marcus Vane had a voice like gravel over silk—rough at the edges but smooth underneath. She heard it clearly through that frequency gap, commenting on her performance to someone she couldn't see.

"The Bastion girl ran the breach exercise again. Solo this time."

"Results?" A woman's voice. One of the senior instructors, probably.

"One-point-seven above baseline. She cleared the advanced parameters in under five minutes."

"That's…" A pause. "That shouldn't be possible for a first-year candidate."

"No," Marcus agreed. "It shouldn't."

"What are you thinking?" the woman asked.

"I'm thinking she's either exactly what we need or exactly what we should fear. Haven't decided which yet."
