"Those aren't mutually exclusive."

Marcus laughed—a short, surprised sound that suggested he didn't do it often. "No. I suppose they're not."

Nicole kept her head down, continued packing her gear, and pretended she hadn't heard anything at all. But she felt his eyes on her through the observation window. Watching. Evaluating. Deciding.



Now

The shaft terminated in a grate overlooking a maintenance corridor. Nicole pressed her face against the mesh, studying the space below. Ten meters of empty hallway. Service doors on either side. A junction at the far end where the corridor branched toward what her schematic identified as the facility's primary detention blocks.

No movement. No guards. No cameras tracking back and forth.

That last absence struck her like ice water. A maximum-security facility housing a high-value Coalition prisoner should have had overlapping coverage—human patrols supplementing automated systems, maintenance corridors treated as potential breach vectors rather than afterthoughts. Either the Coalition had grown complacent, or someone had cleared her path.

Neither option settled well.

Nicole pulled back from the grate, reassessing. The intercepted intelligence that had led her here—fragmented transmissions, partial coordinates, a prisoner manifest with Marcus's designation buried in the encryption—had felt almost too convenient at the time. She'd attributed that to luck, to the chaos of Coalition communication protocols, to her own skill at pattern recognition.

Now she wasn't sure.

She activated her suit's passive sensors, scanning for electromagnetic signatures beyond the visible spectrum. The results painted an ugly picture: camera positions that should have been active showed no power draw, patrol routes that should have overlapped left gaps large enough to fly a shuttle through, and the environmental controls in this section were running on backup systems that didn't feed data to the main security hub.

Someone had created a corridor. A path leading exactly where she wanted to go.

Abort, her training screamed. Retreat, reassess, find another approach.

But there was no other approach. The intelligence she'd intercepted had included transfer orders—Marcus was scheduled to be moved to a black site within forty-eight hours. A site that didn't appear on any map she could access, run by people who made prisoners disappear in ways that had nothing to do with release.

This was her only window. Trap or not, she had to move forward.

But she didn't have to move forward blind.

Nicole adjusted her route, abandoning the direct path through the maintenance corridor. Instead, she worked her way through a secondary shaft that would bring her out in a storage area adjacent to the detention block. Longer, tighter, more physically demanding—but it would let her approach from an angle that whoever had cleared her path might not have anticipated.

If they'd anticipated everything, she was dead anyway.



Then

Three weeks after the simulator, she found him alone in one of the research labs, long after curfew.

"You're here late."

Her voice echoed through the empty space, startling Marcus from his work. He turned from the holographic display he'd been studying—some complex simulation she couldn't immediately parse—and regarded her with an expression that wasn't quite surprise.

"As are you," he said. "The barracks curfew was two hours ago."

"I couldn't sleep."

"Insomnia or intention?"

She considered lying. The smart play was always to lie, to keep people at a distance, to never reveal more than necessary. But something about the lab's amber lighting and Marcus's steady gaze made deception feel pointless.

"Intention," she admitted. "I wanted to see what you work on when you think no one's watching."

His eyebrows rose slightly. "And what have you concluded?"

"That you're running scenarios for operations that don't officially exist. The parameters are too specific for theoretical exercises."

"You extracted all that from a thirty-second glimpse?"

"I've been watching from the doorway for ten minutes."

Marcus laughed again—that same surprised sound she'd heard through the simulator speakers. "Most people would lie about that."

"Most people are boring."

He studied her then, really looked at her in a way that felt like being analyzed by one of his simulations. Nicole held her ground, refusing to fidget under the scrutiny.

"You're talented," he said finally. "The breach simulation scores aren't a fluke—I've reviewed your other results. Combat metrics, technical aptitude, adaptive problem-solving. You're performing in the top fraction of a percent across nearly every category."

"I know."

"That wasn't a compliment. It was a warning."

Nicole frowned. "A warning about what?"

Marcus turned back to his display, gesturing at the complex web of data points and projection lines. "Do you know what this facility actually does, beyond the training exercises they show you?"

"I know it's more than just special operations development."

"That's diplomatic." He manipulated the display, summoning a new set of parameters. "There are people watching your progress. People who see talent like yours and think opportunity. The wrong kind of opportunity."

"The wrong kind for whom?"

"For you." He met her eyes again. "For anyone who ends up in their programs. I've seen what happens to exceptional recruits who catch the wrong attention. They disappear into projects that don't have names, doing work that doesn't get acknowledged. Most of them don't come back. The ones who do…" He shook his head. "They come back different."

Nicole felt a chill that had nothing to do with the lab's climate control. "Are you trying to scare me?"

"I'm trying to inform you. There's a difference." Marcus turned away, shutting down his display. "You're good enough to succeed in normal operations. Better than good. You don't need to chase the attention of people who see operatives as resources to be expended."

"You think I'm chasing attention?"

"I think you're asking questions after curfew in places you shouldn't be." He glanced back at her. "That's how it starts."

Nicole should have left then. The conversation had given her more than enough to think about, more warning than she'd expected from someone who barely knew her name. But she found herself rooted to the spot, unwilling to sever their exchange.

"You're one of them," she said. "One of the people who watches promising recruits. One of the programs that doesn't have a name."

"Yes."

"Then why warn me?"

Marcus was quiet for a long moment. When he spoke, his voice had lost some of its professional distance. "Because I remember what it was like to be talented and ambitious and completely certain that I could handle whatever came next. I was wrong. I'd rather not watch someone else make the same mistake."

"Even if it means losing a promising recruit?"

"Especially then." He moved toward the door, passing close enough that Nicole could smell the chemical tang of simulation gel on his clothes. "Go back to the barracks, recruit. Get some sleep. And stop wandering into places where people might notice you."

He left her alone in the lab, surrounded by the ghosts of his abandoned simulations. Nicole stood there for a long time afterward, trying to decide whether she'd received a kindness or a test.

She never did figure out the answer.

Now

Chapter 7

Deeper Down

Nicole crouched in the shadow of a maintenance alcove, studying the blast door thirty meters ahead. Three meters tall, two wide, its surface swallowed light rather than reflecting it—reactive armor plating engineered to redistribute kinetic energy from explosive breaches.

Move now, or lose the window.

She unclipped Sera's spoofing device, thumbing its smooth casing. The tech specialist had been precise about its limitations: the device could intercept biometric challenge signals and broadcast spoofed responses, but only for credentials captured from Carcosa-7's external network traffic. It wouldn't fool everything—couldn't fool everything—but Sera had identified a vulnerability in how the system validated sequential authentications.

"The DNA sampler and the retinal scanner share a verification handshake," Sera had explained, pulling up schematics on her cluttered workstation. "Crack one, and you've got a thirty-second window where the other assumes you're already cleared. Paranoid engineering, but paranoid in predictable ways."

Nicole slipped from cover, her suit's camouflage rippling to match the corridor's gray monotony. Twenty meters. Fifteen. Ten.

The thermal sensor above the door remained dormant—her suit bleeding body warmth through controlled vents that mimicked ambient air circulation. But electromagnetic sweeps prickled against her awareness as she closed to five meters, subsonic pulses probing for what eyes couldn't see.

She stopped just outside the biometric stations' range and placed the device on the floor, angling it toward the panels. The indicator light blinked amber.

Processing.

One heartbeat. Two. Three.

Green.

The palm reader's micro-needles retracted. The retinal scanner cycled through a verification sequence for someone who wasn't there. Above, servo motors whirred as the thermal array accepted false readings.

Heavy clunks echoed through the corridor—mechanical bolts withdrawing. The door slid upward into its recess.

Nicole snatched the device and moved.



The corridor beyond struck her like a physical weight—narrower, lower, conduits exposed along the ceiling in dense clusters that made the passage feel like a vein inside some massive organism. Amber lighting bathed everything in shades of warning.

Her suit registered the shift: lower temperature, higher humidity, a chemical tang the filters couldn't identify. She pressed forward, the blast door sealing behind her, and found herself in what facility maps had labeled simply "Isolation Wing A."

The maps hadn't mentioned the cameras.

Seven in the first twenty meters. Fixed and tracking, their overlapping fields weaving a surveillance web that seemed impossible to navigate. But Nicole had threaded worse. Every system had blind spots—moments when lenses pointed elsewhere, shadows where algorithms failed.

This is what you trained for. This is what you're good at.

She studied the patrol patterns, building the map in her mind. Three sweep cameras, staggered intervals. Two fixed on the midpoint. Two covering the far junction.

She didn't think. She moved.

Twelve seconds for the leftmost camera to pass. Four seconds of motion—ten meters covered—then down behind a junction box as the lens swept back. Her body knew this rhythm, had learned it through years of missions where hesitation meant death.

The fixed cameras left only a narrow shadow along the base of the left wall, where cable clusters blocked their view. Nicole pressed into that darkness, edging sideways, shoulders brushing conduits.

Her suit's camouflage strained under the amber lighting, power drain showing as a declining bar on her HUD. She was pushing the Obsidian Pulse beyond its design parameters, burning through reserves meant for a mission measured in hours, not—

Her boot snagged a loose cable.

Metal scraped against metal. One of the cameras twitched.

Nicole froze. Breathing stopped. Heartbeat stuttered once before discipline clamped down.

Don't move. Don't think. Just wait.

The camera resumed its fixed position.

She exhaled, long and controlled, and continued forward.



The first patrol found her pressed into an equipment alcove, camouflage stretched to its limits as two guards passed within arm's reach.

Not standard prison staff. Darker uniforms, heavier, bearing insignias she didn't recognize. They moved with the alertness of soldiers expecting contact, weapons ready rather than slung. One wore a helmet with an enhanced visor that swept the corridor with thermal imaging.

Nicole watched the scan pass over her position. Her suit bled her signature into the ambient temperature gradient of the surrounding equipment.

The guard paused. Adjusted something on his helmet.

He sees something. He knows something's wrong.

Then he moved on.

She waited until their footsteps faded before allowing herself to breathe.

The second patrol caught her in the open.

Boots on metal, low murmur of conversation, three seconds to find cover in a corridor that offered none. Bare walls. Ceiling too high. Floor holding nothing but—

Pressure sensors. Tiny plates at irregular intervals, nearly invisible under amber light. She'd been threading between them without knowing, her footwork finding safe paths by instinct. But now, guards approaching, she had nowhere to go that wouldn't trigger an alarm or expose her to visual contact.

She went up.

The cable clusters bore her weight as she distributed it across multiple conduit bundles, hauling herself into the overhead maze. The space was cramped, barely enough room, but the shadows up here were absolute.

The guards passed beneath. Through gaps in the cables, she caught fragments of their conversation.

"—transferring the new one tomorrow—"

"—Frost's orders. Something about the processing facility—"

"—doesn't matter what we think. Just follow protocol—"

Tomorrow.

Nicole's grip tightened on the conduit. Three years she'd been searching. Three years of dead ends and false leads and the slow erosion of hope that anyone else refused to acknowledge. And now tomorrow might mean she was already too late.

She lowered herself back to the floor, avoiding the sensors she could now clearly identify, and moved faster.



The cells began at the first junction.

Not holding areas—standard prison accommodations, bare but recognizable. These were something else. Sealed units, doors that were featureless metal slabs with small viewports at eye level. Dark viewports, but as Nicole moved past the first row, she glimpsed what lay beyond.

A figure huddled in the corner, unmoving. No bed, no facilities. Just bare walls and a floor drain.

A body strapped to a central table, connected to machines by tubes and wires. Blue glow from medical equipment. A machine beeped, injected something, and the body convulsed before going still.

Don't stop. Don't look.

But she couldn't help it.

A man hung suspended in viscous fluid, breathing through a mask, eyes open but unseeing. A woman lay on a cot, shaved head bristling with electrode contacts, mouth frozen in silent scream. Two figures sat facing each other, movements synchronized with mechanical precision, as if something had overwritten whatever made them individuals.

Nicole's hand found the wall. Her stomach churned.

These people need help. All of them.

The thought cut through her tactical focus, raw and unwelcome. This wasn't what she'd come for—she had a mission, a single objective—but the mission suddenly felt small against the weight of what she was witnessing.

You can't save them all.

The math was brutal. Even if she freed everyone, where would they go? One person, one extraction point that might not work for one person, let alone dozens.

Save one, or save none.

Marcus's face flickered through her mind—not the scientist, not the man she'd buried, but the version of him who'd sat with her after her first failed mission and said: You can't carry everyone, Nicole. You'll break under the weight. Choose who you can save, and save them well.

She'd hated him for that advice. Still did, some days.

But she kept moving.



The target cell waited in the deepest section, past three more checkpoints and two patrols she barely avoided. By the time she reached it, her suit's power reserves were critical, camouflage flickering at the edges, heat suppression struggling to maintain coherence.

Cell 47-Sigma.

The door matched every other—featureless metal, small viewport, biometric lock. Nicole approached and peered inside.

Dark, but not empty. A figure sat on the bare floor with their back against the far wall, knees drawn up, head bowed.

That posture. The same way Marcus had always sat when he was thinking, when he retreated into himself to process something the world had thrown at him.

She couldn't see his face. Wrong angle, lighting too dim.

What if it's not him?

The doubt surfaced, cold and familiar. Three years since she'd seen him. Intelligence filtered through sources she'd never met. The cell number could be wrong. The identification mistaken. She could find a stranger—or worse, something that used to be Marcus but wasn't anymore.

What if the man you're risking everything to save no longer exists?

Nicole pressed her palm against the viewport, feeling the cold metal through her glove. She thought of his smile, rare and genuine. The sound of his laugh. The warmth of his hand in hers during stolen moments when duty gave way to something human.

That man is worth saving. Whatever they've done to him, whatever's left.

She knelt beside the biometric lock and pulled out her toolkit. The figure in the cell didn't move, didn't look up, gave no sign of awareness.

That was fine. That was expected.

She'd come this far.

Behind her, forty meters down the corridor, a camera she'd threaded past began to turn. Its arc was wrong—too fast, too deliberate—tracking backward along a path she'd already cleared.

Nicole's fingers stilled on the lock mechanism. Her suit's sensors pulsed a warning.

Motion detected. Hostile sweep pattern.

The camera stopped, lens fixed directly on the stretch of corridor between her position and the last junction. Seconds passed. Then, barely audible, a distant alarm began to pulse—not the facility-wide klaxon she'd feared, but something localized. Targeted.

A door opened somewhere behind her.

Footsteps. Multiple sets. Moving fast.

Nicole looked at the cell door, still locked. At her toolkit, spread before her. At the power gauge on her HUD, blinking red.

She had maybe ninety seconds before they reached her position.

And she wasn't leaving without him.

Chapter 8

The Forbidden

Nicole's hand hovered over the cell door control, fingers trembling in the sterile light of the detention level. The tremor wasn't from the cold—the environmental systems maintained this section at a precise twenty degrees—but from something far more treacherous. Something that had coiled inside her chest for four years, patient and waiting.

The security panel glowed amber beneath her palm, demanding authorization she shouldn't possess. Her reflection stared back from the polished metal surface: hollow cheeks, dark crescents beneath her eyes, hair scraped back in the severe regulation style she'd maintained even after everything collapsed. She looked like a ghost haunting her own life.

Beyond this door, Marcus waited. Marcus, who had taught her everything. Marcus, who had destroyed everything. Marcus, who she had loved with a ferocity that still burned like radiation poisoning in her bones.

Her finger brushed the override sequence—the codes she'd copied during the security hub infiltration three days ago, the ones Jace had nearly died helping her extract. The ones that would brand her a traitor if anyone discovered what she was about to do.

Footsteps echoed in the corridor behind her—two sets, approaching from the east junction. She had maybe forty seconds before they rounded the corner and found her standing at the door of the facility's most dangerous prisoner.

No more time for hesitation. No more time for the memories clawing at the edges of her mind—that first conversation in the training facility, the night in the simulation chamber when everything shifted, the years of stolen moments that followed. All of it pressed against her skull, demanding attention she couldn't afford to give.

Her finger touched the override control. The panel shifted from amber to green.

The cell door slid open.

Marcus sat on the narrow cot, exactly where she'd known he would be. He looked up as light from the corridor spilled across his face—thinner than she remembered, bruises yellowing along his jaw, but those gray eyes still sharp as cutting lasers. The same eyes that had studied her across the tactical simulation chamber four years ago, cataloging her like a problem to be solved. The same eyes that had looked at her with something like wonder the first time he'd spoken her name.

"Nicole." Her name in his voice, after all this time. It struck her like a blow to the chest. "You shouldn't be here."

"I know." She stepped into the cell, letting the door slide shut behind her. The panel clicked red—locked now, sealing them in together. "Get up. We have about three minutes before the patrol cycle brings guards to this corridor."

"You'll destroy yourself." He didn't move. "Everything you've worked for. Everything Cross built in you. Frost will hunt you to the edges of known space."

"I said get up."

"Nicole—"

"You don't get to protect me anymore." She crossed the cell in two strides, seized his arm, hauled him to his feet. He was lighter than she remembered—they'd been starving him, or close to it. "You lost that right when you sold our operation codes to the Syndicate and got six of my people killed."

Marcus flinched. She'd wanted him to, and hated herself for the satisfaction.

The words tasted like ash. Six operatives. Six people who had trusted the intelligence Marcus provided, who had walked into an ambush because the man Nicole loved had traded their lives for something she still didn't understand. Reyes, who collected pre-collapse music files. Tanaka, who'd been three weeks from retirement. Morrison, who'd taught Nicole how to disable a security grid with nothing but a maintenance tool and spite.

She'd attended six memorial services. Stood at attention while Frost delivered six identical speeches about sacrifice and duty. Watched six families receive folded flags and classified condolences.

And through all of it, she'd kept her face blank. Professional. Unbroken.

"There's more happening here than you know," Marcus said, his voice carrying that same measured quality she remembered from a hundred mission briefings—low, steady, designed to make you believe him. She'd fallen for that voice once. Let it teach her to shoot, to fight, to see tactical patterns in chaos. Let it whisper her name in the dark. "The codes, the Syndicate, what Frost is really building in the Meridian sector—"

"Save it for the debrief." She pressed her palm to the hidden panel she'd identified three days ago, during the security hub infiltration. The panel slid open, revealing a maintenance shaft barely wide enough for one person. "If you've got information, Sera and Jace will extract it. My job is just getting you out alive."

"And you trust them? Trust anyone in this facility?"

The question cut closer than he probably intended. Sera, with her careful silences and her habit of knowing things she shouldn't. Jace, who'd nearly bled out in a ventilation shaft three days ago because Nicole had asked him to help with a mission she couldn't fully explain. Kira, waiting three levels below with a transport that would make all of them fugitives the moment they launched.

I'd be terrified, she'd told him once, a lifetime ago. But the spacing would still be correct. She'd been so certain then. So convinced that competence could carry her through anything.

Now she wasn't certain of anything except the clock counting down in her head.

"I trust them more than I trust you." She gestured toward the shaft. "Move. Now."

For a moment, she thought he might argue. Might try to explain, to justify, to transform what he'd done into something she could forgive.

"There was a girl," he said instead. "Years ago. Seventeen. Neural engineer. She swallowed a toxin capsule rather than let us extract her."

Nicole's throat constricted. She remembered that night—his hands pressed against his eyes, his voice cracking on words he'd never meant to say aloud. The first time she'd seen past the legend to the person underneath.

"I remember," she said, the words barely audible.

"I told myself then that I'd never be the monster she saw." His gray eyes held hers. "I need you to know—whatever you think happened, whatever Frost told you—"

"I don't have time for this."

"Nicole." The way he said her name. God, the way he still said her name. "You came here. You're throwing away everything to get me out. That means some part of you still—"

"That means I need answers." She cut him off before he could finish the sentence, before he could name the thing still burning in her chest. "That means six people are dead and nobody will tell me why. That means Frost is building something in the Meridian sector and you're the only one who knows what it is." She stepped closer, close enough to see the new lines carved around his eyes, the way captivity had sharpened his features. "Don't confuse this for forgiveness."

"I'm not." He held her gaze. "I'm just telling you it wasn't what you think."

"Then what was it?"

He was silent. The seconds stretched like wire between them.

"That's what I thought." Nicole gestured toward the shaft again. "Move."

This time, he climbed into the darkness without argument.

Nicole followed, dragging the panel shut behind her, sealing them both into the narrow space between the facility's walls. Above them, three levels of armed security. Below them, Kira waiting with the transport they'd spent two weeks positioning.

The shaft smelled like recycled air and old metal, the same scent that permeated every maintenance tunnel in the facility. In the darkness ahead, she could hear Marcus breathing—slower and more labored than it should have been. Whatever they'd done to him in interrogation had left marks she couldn't see.

You make me remember what it felt like to be human. He'd said that, once. In a simulation chamber at midnight, with a failed scenario frozen around them and the weight of a dead girl heavy on his shoulders. Before this job hollowed me out.

She'd believed him. She'd kissed him, that night—reckless and desperate and certain it was the beginning of something instead of the start of a very slow ending.

She'd been twenty-three and stupid. She'd thought love was something you could trust.

"Left at the junction," she said, her voice flat and professional. "And Marcus?"

"Yes?"

"If you try to run, if you've led me into a trap, if any of this is another layer of whatever game you're playing—" She let the silence speak for her.

"Understood." His voice drifted back through the darkness. "For what it's worth, Nicole, I never stopped—"

"Don't." The word came out sharper than she intended. "Just move."

They crawled forward into the dark, the past pressing close around them, the future utterly uncertain. Behind them, the empty cell waited for guards who would find nothing but shadows.

Nicole focused on the path ahead and tried not to think about what it meant that she'd just become everything Frost had accused her of being.

For him. Still for him. After everything.

Some lessons, it seemed, she'd never learn.

Chapter 9

Resurrection

The door slid open with a pneumatic hiss, and darkness exhaled.

Nicole stood frozen in the threshold, her tactical suit's sensors screaming data into her retinal display—temperature readings, atmospheric composition, the faint heat signature of a body inside. But her brain refused to process any of it. Twenty-five years of certainty collapsed in the space between one heartbeat and the next.

The cell was small. Gray walls, a cot, a shape sitting on its edge.

And a figure.

He sat with one hand raised to shield his eyes from the corridor's harsh illumination spilling past her shoulder. The light carved him in stark relief—the slope of his shoulders, the way his spine curved with fatigue rather than defeat, the shape of a face she had memorized in childhood and never quite forgotten.

Nicole couldn't breathe.

"Who—" His voice cracked from disuse, rough as gravel dragged across glass. He cleared his throat, tried again. The second attempt died somewhere in his chest, emerging as little more than a rasp. He swallowed hard, working moisture into a throat that had forgotten how to speak to anyone but ghosts.

She should move. Should step forward, identify herself, begin the extraction protocol that Sera's voice was already murmuring in her earpiece. But her legs had turned to lead, her muscles locked in place by something stronger than fear.

He was older. Of course he was older—twenty-five years would age anyone—but knowing that intellectually hadn't prepared her for witnessing it. His hair had gone gray, steel-colored in the artificial light, cropped short in a way that suggested practical maintenance rather than choice. Deep lines bracketed his mouth and fanned from the corners of his eyes. His cheeks were hollow, the bones of his face more prominent than she remembered, as if time and confinement had slowly worn away everything soft.

But his eyes. When he lowered his hand and squinted toward her, she saw his eyes, and they were exactly the same. Dark brown, sharp with intelligence despite the circumstances, still holding that quality she'd never been able to name—the one that had made her feel, even as a small child, that he could see straight through to the truth of things.

"Not… standard." He gestured vaguely at the door, at her, struggling to assemble words that had lived only in his head for so long. "You're not… rotation."

He rose from the cot, slowly, carefully—and she watched how much effort even that simple movement cost him. One hand pressed against the wall for support. His legs trembled beneath him, thin and wasted under the shapeless gray prison uniform. He swayed, nearly fell, caught himself against the concrete with a grunt of frustration. But he stood. He faced her directly, chin lifting with something that defied the weakness in his body.

She recognized that defiance. She had inherited it.

His gaze swept over her tactical suit, and she watched his mind engage despite his body's rebellion—cataloging details with the methodical precision she remembered from childhood. Advanced tech, military-grade by the look of it. Not Consortium standard issue. His brow furrowed.

"That armor," he managed, his voice growing marginally stronger as he used it, each word coming easier than the last. "Who are you with?"

Nicole opened her mouth, but no sound emerged. Twenty-five years of imagining this moment, and she had no words. All the things she'd rehearsed in her mind—the accusations, the questions, the desperate pleas for explanation—had evaporated like water on hot metal.

Her hand rose to her helmet seal. The movement felt automatic, as if her body had decided to act while her brain remained paralyzed. She heard the click of the magnetic locks disengaging, felt the rush of recycled cell air against her face as the visor retracted.

He stared at her.

She watched him take in her features—the dark hair pulled back tight against her skull, the sharp angles of her face, the scar that curved along her left jawline where a piece of shrapnel had caught her three years ago. She was not the nine-year-old girl who had watched him board a transport ship and never return. She knew that. She had become someone else entirely in the intervening decades, someone harder, someone capable of killing without hesitation, someone who had buried every soft thing she'd ever felt beneath layers of mission parameters and tactical objectives.

But something in his expression shifted. His eyes narrowed, then widened. His hand fell away from the wall, and for a moment she thought he might collapse—but he caught himself, one step forward, then another, moving toward her with the painful deliberation of a man whose body had forgotten how to move quickly.

"Nicole?"

Her name in his voice.

The sound of it cracked something in her chest. Not broke—not yet. Cracked, the way ice fractures before it gives way entirely. She felt the fissure spread through her, threatening the architecture of control she'd spent two decades building.

"I'm getting you out," she said. The words came out flat, professional. The soldier in her speaking because the woman couldn't—because if she let the woman who'd loved him speak, the soldier would shatter completely.

He stopped two meters away, close enough that she could see the pulse jumping in his throat, the slight tremor in his hands. Close enough that she could smell the staleness of long confinement, overlaid with something sharper—fear, maybe, or hope, or some toxic mixture of both.

"You can't be here." His voice had changed, urgent now, a protective edge that twenty-five years hadn't erased. "How did you—they'll know. The security protocols, the monitoring systems—"

"Already handled." Sera's code had bought them thirty minutes. They'd used twelve. "We have time."

"Nicole—" He stopped, struggling with words again, but this time she understood it wasn't disuse. It was emotion, overwhelming the vocabulary he was trying to reconstruct. He pressed a hand to his forehead, collecting himself. "You don't understand. They have… contingencies. Layered ones. Security you can see, security you can't. Whatever breach got you in—they'll have logged it, analyzed it. Might even have allowed it."

"Allowed it?" The professional detachment in her voice wavered. "Why would they—"

"Because they've been waiting." His words came faster now, his mind outpacing his atrophied voice. "Twenty-five years, and no one's tried to extract me. They knew eventually someone would. They've had decades to prepare countermeasures for exactly this scenario."

The implication settled into her tactical awareness like a weight. Not that the security had failed—but that the failure itself might be intentional. A mousetrap with the cheese already taken.

"Then we move faster," she said. "We deal with whatever's coming."

"I know what they've done." The words came out harder than she intended, redirecting from the tactical problem to the personal one. She couldn't process the idea that this might be a trap—not now, not when he was standing in front of her. "I've seen the data. The experiments. What they've turned you into."

He flinched as if she'd struck him.

"Nicole." He said her name differently now, softer, weighted with something she couldn't interpret. "Look at me."

She was looking at him. She hadn't stopped looking at him since the door opened. Cataloging every change, every sign of damage, every proof that he had been alive all these years while she had believed him dead.

And standing there, watching him watch her, she felt the careful compartmentalization begin to fail. The soldier, the lover, the weapon, the woman she'd been before he died—all of them demanding attention at once, pulling her in directions she couldn't reconcile. Her hands trembled slightly, and she clenched them into fists to hide it.

The anger she'd been suppressing since the transport ship surged upward, mixing with relief and grief and a childish sense of abandonment that should have died decades ago. She let it come. Anger was easier than the alternative. Anger, she knew how to use.

"Why didn't you contact me?" The question escaped before she could stop it. "All these years. You were alive. You were here. Why didn't you—"

"They would have killed you." Simple. Matter of fact. As if explaining basic physics to a student. "Anyone connected to me, anyone who might know what I knew—they were all eliminated. I watched it happen. Colleagues, friends. Their families." He paused, and his jaw tightened. "I let them think I had no family. No children. I erased every record I could access, buried every connection. I thought… I thought if I was dead to you, you might survive."

The answer was logical. Strategic, even. Exactly what she might have done in his position.

She hated how much sense it made.

"I spent four years believing you were a monster." Her voice cracked on the last word. "The data they released. The testimonies. You killed thirty-seven people in that lab—"

"I know what the official record says."

"Is it true?"

He met her gaze without flinching. "Some of it."

The admission hit her like a physical blow. She had prepared herself for denial, for explanations, for elaborate justifications. Not for this quiet acknowledgment that some part of the nightmare had been real.

She let the silence stretch for three heartbeats. Four. Processing, recalibrating. The soldier reasserting control because she needed answers more than comfort.

"Which parts?" Her voice had steadied. Interrogator mode—not because she wanted distance, but because distance was the only way she could keep standing.

"Nicole—"

"Which parts?"

He exhaled slowly. Up close, she could see how much weight he'd lost—the way his collarbone jutted beneath the thin fabric of his uniform, the prominence of his wrists where his sleeves had ridden up. They had kept him alive, but just barely. Just enough to be useful.

"The project was called Threshold," he said. "Consciousness transfer technology. Theoretical basis for neural mapping that could survive physical death. The military applications were obvious—imagine soldiers who could survive any injury, whose minds could be downloaded and reuploaded into new bodies. Immortal armies." He laughed, a sound without humor. "But that wasn't what interested me. I was thinking about preservation. About the people we lose and can't get back. About your mother."

Nicole's throat tightened. Her mother had died when she was six—a neural degenerative condition that ate away at her mind over the course of three years, leaving nothing but a shell by the end. She remembered fragments. Warm hands. A laugh that seemed to fill every room. And then the slow, terrible forgetting, the way her mother's eyes had stopped recognizing her in those final months.

"I thought if I could map consciousness, truly preserve it, we'd never have to lose anyone again." His voice had gone distant, lost in memory. "I was arrogant. Brilliant and arrogant—the worst combination. I pushed too fast, ignored the ethical reviews, cut corners I shouldn't have cut." He paused. "There were test subjects. Volunteers from the terminal ward. People with nothing to lose. They signed the consent forms. They understood the risks—or they thought they did."

"Thirty-seven people," Nicole said.

"Thirty-seven subjects in the final trial. The one that went wrong." He closed his eyes. "The transfer process was unstable. Instead of copying their consciousness, it… fragmented it. Distributed pieces across the neural network we'd built. They died, technically. Their bodies failed. But their minds—portions of their minds—remained. Screaming. Trapped in the system. Unable to die completely, unable to live."

Nicole felt sick. The horror of it clawed at her, mixing with the relief she still felt at seeing him alive, at touching the fabric of his sleeve where her hand had somehow come to rest.

"I tried to shut it down," he continued. "To let them go. But by then, the Consortium had taken an interest. They saw weapons potential where I'd seen salvation. And when I refused to cooperate, they found ways to make refusal impossible." His eyes met hers, hollow with a guilt that had been festering for years. "That's what I am now, Nicole. Not a scientist. A leash they haven't finished pulling.

Chapter 10

Running Hot

The cell door's locking mechanism surrendered to the override code Sera had excavated from Carcosa-7's archived maintenance logs—a thirty-year-old backdoor some long-dead engineer had installed out of laziness, and which everyone since had simply forgotten. Nicole heard the bolts disengage and felt something unclench beneath her ribs. Forty-seven minutes of crawling through service ducts, bypassing three security checkpoints, holding her breath each time a guard patrol drifted within meters of where she pressed herself against the wall. Forty-seven minutes of Sera's voice threading through her ear, guiding her through the station's labyrinthine guts while Jace orchestrated diversions on the opposite side of the facility.

And now she stood here.

She eased the door open, weapon raised, her heart hammering against her ribs in a rhythm that had nothing to do with exertion.

The cell was small—three meters by three, bare metal walls, a cot bolted to the floor, a sanitation unit crouched in the corner. Standard isolation housing. Maximum security. The kind of place where the Federation buried people it wanted forgotten.

Marcus Vane sat on the edge of the cot, hands resting on his knees, watching the door as though he'd been expecting her.

He looked old.

That was Nicole's first thought, cutting through all the years she'd spent imagining this moment. The man she remembered had been tall and vital, dark-haired and sharp-eyed, the kind of presence that swallowed a room just by entering it. The man before her was gaunt, his hair more gray than black, his cheeks hollowed by years of inadequate nutrition. Prison pallor had bleached his skin the color of old paper.

But his eyes were the same. Dark and calculating, already processing tactical data even as he studied her face with an intensity that made her skin prickle.

"Nicole." His voice scraped out rough from disuse, but he spoke her name with absolute certainty. Not a question. A recognition.

"We need to move." She kept her voice professional, controlled. There would be time for everything else later—the questions, the accusations, the twenty-five years of silence that sat between them like a physical mass. "Can you walk?"

"I can do what's necessary." He rose from the cot, and she saw what it cost him—the way his hand pressed briefly against the wall for balance, the slight tremor rippling through his legs. But he straightened, met her eyes, and nodded. "Lead the way."

Nicole turned toward the door, scanning the corridor through her helmet's enhanced display. Clear, for now. Sera's diversions were holding, but they wouldn't hold forever. She had maybe six minutes before the next patrol cycle swept guards back through this section.

"Stay close," she said. "Stay quiet. Do exactly what I tell you."

"I remember how this works."

She glanced back at him, caught the ghost of something almost like a smile on his cracked lips, and felt a response flicker in her chest before she crushed it.

"That was twenty-five years ago. Things have changed."

"Some things," he agreed. "Not everything."



They made it through two sections before the alarms erupted.

Later, Nicole would review the mission logs and identify the exact moment security realized something was wrong—a camera that should have been looped catching three frames of movement, an automated system flagging an anomaly in atmospheric processing. But in the moment, all she knew was that the emergency lighting suddenly blazed red and the corridor filled with the shriek of klaxons.

"Contact," Sera's voice crackled through her comms, fighting through sudden jamming interference. "Multiple units converging on your position. They've made you, Nicole."

"I noticed." Nicole seized Marcus's arm, feeling the tremor in muscles that hadn't been asked to run in years. "Service corridor thirty meters up. Guard rotation?"

"Ninety seconds to cycle through," Marcus said, his voice still sharp even as his body struggled to keep pace. "You know this place better than I expected."

"I've had twenty-five years to learn it." She hauled him around a corner just as plasma fire scorched the wall where they'd been standing, heat washing over her back like a furnace blast. "Come on, Marcus, move."

His name came out like a command, like a plea—the way she'd said it a lifetime ago, in darkened corridors and stolen moments and one terrible night when she'd begged him not to walk into that lab. He'd looked at her with those dark eyes and said he had to. Said it would be fine. Said he'd come back to her.

He hadn't. Not for twenty-five years.

She shoved the memory down, buried it beneath the operational focus she'd spent a decade cultivating. Later. Deal with it later.

The service corridor was exactly where Marcus said it would be—a narrow maintenance shaft running parallel to the main passage, its entrance hidden behind a panel that resembled solid wall unless you knew exactly where to push. Nicole counted the seconds as she worked, her fingers finding the hidden release while Marcus braced himself against the opposite wall, his breathing labored but controlled.

"Nicole." Sera's voice crackled through her comms, distorted by whatever jamming Carcosa-7 was running. "I'm reading massive security response converging on your position. They've locked down sections seven through twelve."

"Copy. Working on it." The panel slid aside, revealing darkness and the smell of recycled air. Nicole grabbed Marcus's arm again, pulling him into the shaft. "Route to extraction point?"

"Calculating. They're sealing bulkheads throughout the station. Give me a minute."

"Don't have a minute." Nicole sealed the panel behind them, plunging them into darkness. She tapped her wrist display, activating the low-light enhancement built into her tactical lenses. The shaft resolved into shades of green and gray—barely wide enough for two people standing side by side, the ceiling low enough that Marcus had to duck his head. "Jace, what's your status?"

Static. Then: "Little busy here, Bastion."

The sound of plasma fire punctuated his words, followed by what might have been an explosion or might have been her comms cutting out. Nicole swore under her breath and started moving, one hand on the wall for guidance, the other maintaining contact with Marcus.

"How far?" she asked.

"Three hundred meters. Industrial processing sector." His breathing was already ragged, but he kept pace. "Security protocols changed six months ago when they moved me from general population. Sera will need to adapt."

"She will." Nicole matched her stride to his, fighting the urge to sprint. "Keep moving."



The shaft ended in a vertical drop—a maintenance ladder descending into what her display identified as Processing Sector 4. Nicole switched her tactical lenses to full enhancement mode, the darkness resolving into grainy but navigable detail. The ladder glistened with condensation, the air growing warmer as they descended, heavy with the chemical tang of industrial solvents.

"I'm going first," she told Marcus. "Wait for my signal before you follow. And take this." She pressed a small adhesive disc into his palm. "Backup light source, if you need it. Squeeze to activate."

He nodded, his face a pale blur in her enhanced vision.

Nicole descended carefully, testing each rung, her weapon holstered to keep both hands free. The ladder groaned under her weight but held. Above her, she heard Marcus begin his own descent, his grip on the rungs uncertain. Once his foot slipped—the scrape of prison-issue shoes against metal making her heart seize. But he didn't fall.

At the bottom, she paused, scanning the space. Processing Sector 4 opened before her in shades of industrial green—massive equipment hulking in the darkness, the distant hum of dormant machinery, and at the far end of a corridor, a splash of warmer light.

Two guards at a security station, their backs to her, their attention fixed on a bank of monitors.

Nicole crept closer, using the equipment for cover, until she could see the screens they were watching. Surveillance feeds cycling through different sections of the station—corridors, cell blocks, maintenance areas. She spotted the shaft entrance she'd just emerged from, the image showing empty darkness.

The processing sector's surveillance ran on a dedicated local system, Sera had told her during mission prep. Forty years old, perpetually lagging behind the rest of the station's network. But that meant the guards were watching footage already thirty seconds out of date. They hadn't seen her emerge because the system hadn't caught up yet. And right now, both of them were focused on a different screen entirely—one showing Jace's firefight in section nine, their heads tilted together as they watched the action.

A small window. But real.

Nicole closed the distance in silence.

The first guard went down before he could turn, Nicole's knife finding the gap between his helmet and collar with surgical precision. The second got his weapon up, got his mouth open to shout, but Nicole was already moving—a palm strike to his throat that turned his warning into a strangled gurgle, followed by a knee to the midsection that dropped him. She caught his head before it could strike the deck and alert anyone else, lowering him to the floor with the same care she'd once used arranging flowers in her mother's garden.

Funny, the things you thought about when you were killing people.

"Clear," she said softly, and heard Marcus drop the last few feet from the ladder, landing with a grunt that spoke of protesting joints and weakened muscles.

She activated the light strips along the corridor's baseboards—emergency illumination that wouldn't show on external sensors but would give Marcus something to navigate by. He blinked at the sudden brightness, then his gaze found the bodies. The blood on her hands.

For a moment, he just looked at her.

"You're good," he said quietly. "Better than I was."

"I learned from the best." The words came out sharper than she intended. Nicole wiped her knife on the dead guard's uniform and sheathed it, then bent to retrieve his plasma rifle. Standard Carcosa issue, magnetic acceleration, thirty shots before the cell needed cycling. She checked the charge, found it full.

She looked at Marcus—at his wasted muscles, his trembling hands. Then she crossed to him, pressing the rifle into his grip and holding it there until she felt his fingers close around the stock.

"Can you manage the weight?"

His hands found the grip and balance point without conscious thought—muscle memory older than his imprisonment. For just a second, she saw the man he'd been. Then his arms started shaking with the effort of holding the weapon level.

"I can still shoot," he said, reading her expression.

"I know. But tell me if you can't carry it."

"Nicole—"

"We can talk later." She moved past him, checking her display for the route Sera was still calculating. "If there is a later. Sera, I need that path."

"Working on it. Jace is pinned down in section nine—they've got him boxed in. I'm trying to find him an alternate route, but they're sealing everything."

"Where's Kira?"

"Inbound. ETA twelve minutes to docking bay seven."

Twelve minutes. Nicole looked at Marcus, at the sweat already beading on his forehead, at the way he was favoring his left leg. Twelve minutes might as well be twelve hours.

"Then we need to reach docking bay seven," she said. "What's between us and there?"

The answer came in the form of boots on metal—dozens of them, echoing from both ends of the corridor they'd just cleared. Security had found them.



Nicole shoved Marcus into an alcove and opened fire.

The plasma rifle kicked against her shoulder, sending bolts of superheated energy screaming down the corridor. The first wave of guards dropped—three, four, she lost count—their bodies creating obstacles for those behind them. But more kept coming—too many, flooding the corridor like a tide that refused to break. "Move!" she screamed at Marcus, laying down suppressing fire as they ran. The docking bay was six sections away, and every meter between here and there had just become a war zone.

Chapter 11

After the Fire

The medical bay aboard Widow's Edge was smaller than anything Nicole had worked with during her Authority days—a converted cargo hold with sterile panels bolted over industrial plating, equipment salvaged from a dozen different sources humming in frequencies that refused to harmonize. She lingered in the doorway, shoulder wedged against the frame, watching.

Daven Cross moved around the examination table with the unhurried precision of someone who had spent decades reading bodies the way others read star charts. His hands bore the weathering of age, spotted and creased, but they remained steady as he calibrated the diagnostic scanner. Gray hair pulled back in a practical tail. Deep lines carved around eyes that had witnessed too many patients and too few miracles.

"Pulse is weak but steady," Daven said, his voice carrying that particular roughness of someone who'd breathed recycled air for too many years. "Blood pressure's lower than I'd like. When's the last time you had a proper meal, Marcus?"

On the table, Marcus looked like something dredged from deep water. The harsh medical lighting stripped away any illusion of health, exposing the full extent of what Carcosa-7 had done to him. His cheekbones jutted too sharply. The tendons in his neck stood visible, the skin stretched thin over them like paper over wire. His eyes, though—his eyes remained unchanged. Still carried that particular quality of seeing through surfaces to the structures beneath.

"Define proper," Marcus said. His voice emerged as a rasp, damaged by years of minimal use and whatever else they'd done to him. "The nutrient paste they provided was technically sufficient for biological function."

"Technically sufficient." Daven made a sound that might have been a laugh in someone with more humor left in them. "I'm going to run a full panel. You're malnourished—severely. Muscle atrophy in all the major groups. Your body's been eating itself to stay alive." He pressed the scanner along Marcus's arm, watching data populate on the screen beside the table. "How long were you down there?"

"Four years. Approximately."

"Four years in a cell with minimal movement, minimal stimulation, minimal nutrition." Daven shook his head slowly. "It's remarkable you're still breathing, let alone walking."

"They needed me alive. Needed my mind intact enough to work." Marcus's gaze drifted past Daven, found Nicole in the doorway. Something crossed his face—recognition, pain, something harder to name. "The body was secondary to their requirements."

Nicole didn't move. Didn't speak. She kept her shoulder wedged to the doorframe, kept her arms folded across her chest, kept the distance between them like a physical barrier she could control.

Daven followed Marcus's gaze, offered Nicole a brief nod before returning to his examination. "I'm going to need more comprehensive scans. Check for long-term organ damage, bone density loss. This level of atrophy doesn't reverse overnight."

"I'm aware of my condition," Marcus said. "I've been tracking my own deterioration for years. The cognitive effects were my primary concern—they seemed to accelerate when the nutrition dropped below certain thresholds."

"We can address the cognitive concerns later. Right now, I want to stabilize your physical—"

"No sedation."

Daven paused, scanner still in hand. "I wasn't going to suggest—"

"You were considering it. I can see it in how you're positioning yourself relative to the medication cabinet." Marcus pushed himself up slightly on the table, and Nicole watched his arms tremble with the effort. "I spent four years in a controlled chemical haze. I need to stay awake. I need to stay present."

"Marcus—"

"I won't go under again. Not willingly." His voice cracked on the last word, and for a moment, Nicole glimpsed something breaking through the surface—fear, naked and raw, the kind that lived in bones. "Please."

The word hung in the recycled air between them. Daven set down the scanner, crossed to the small cabinet, and returned with a packet of concentrated nutrient gel and a hydration tab.

"No sedation," he agreed. "But you need to eat. And you need to rest—actual rest, not the kind they forced on you. Your body can't rebuild what it's lost if you're running on empty."

Marcus accepted the packet with hands that shook slightly. He tore it open with his teeth when his fingers couldn't manage the seal, and Nicole remembered him doing the same thing in their old kitchen, impatient with packaging, always three steps ahead in his mind. The memory struck her like a blade between the ribs.

She remained in the doorway.



Daven worked in silence for the next hour, running scans, administering fluids through an IV that Marcus tolerated with visible reluctance. Nicole observed it all from her position at the threshold—close enough to see, far enough to maintain the fiction that she wasn't fully present.

The ship hummed around them, slipstream transit smooth now that they'd cleared Carcosa-7's interference zone. Somewhere else on the Widow's Edge, Jace was running post-escape diagnostics. Sera was probably already dismantling the ship in her head, cataloging everything she wanted to modify. Kira was wherever Kira went when she needed to process violence—probably the cargo bay, working through forms, burning off adrenaline through movement.

Normal operations. As if they hadn't just extracted a dead man from a secret prison. As if Nicole's entire understanding of the past decade hadn't been gutted and rearranged in the span of a few hours.

"His vitals are stabilizing," Daven said finally, stripping off his examination gloves. "The IV will help with the immediate deficiencies. I'll put together a nutrition schedule—small portions, high density. His digestive system needs time to readjust to actual food." He looked at Nicole properly for the first time since she'd taken up her position. "He needs rest. Real rest. And probably conversation that isn't medical in nature."

"I'm capable of participating in my own care discussions," Marcus said from the table. "I haven't lost that much cognitive function."

"You haven't lost any cognitive function that I can measure," Daven replied. "Which is its own kind of miracle, given what your scans show. Whatever they wanted from your mind, they were invested in keeping it intact." He gathered his equipment, stored it with the efficiency of long practice. "I'll be back in four hours to check your levels. Try not to remove the IV before then."

Marcus didn't answer. His attention had shifted back to Nicole, and Daven tracked it, understanding passing across his weathered features.

"Right," the medic said. He moved toward the door, and Nicole stepped aside to let him pass. As he did, he paused—close enough to speak quietly, pitched for her ears alone. "He's alive, Nicole. Whatever else happened, whatever else is coming, he's alive. That has to count for something."

She didn't respond. Daven didn't seem to expect her to. He passed through the doorway and his footsteps faded down the corridor, leaving her alone with the ghost she'd spent years learning to live without.



Silence.

The medical equipment kept up its soft symphony—the beep of the heart monitor, the hum of the IV pump, the background static of the diagnostic screens. Marcus lay on the table, propped up slightly now, nutrient gel half-consumed in his lap. Nicole stood just inside the doorway, unwilling to leave, unwilling to approach.

The silence stretched.

She counted her breaths. A technique she'd learned somewhere between Marcus's death and her first command —a way to maintain control when everything else was chaos. In through the nose, four counts. Hold for seven. Out through the mouth, eight counts. Simple arithmetic to keep the heart steady and the mind clear.

Marcus watched her do it. Of course he did. He'd always been too observant, too capable of reading the patterns in people's behavior.

"You became something different," he said finally. His voice was still rough, still damaged, but there was something underneath it now—curiosity, maybe. Assessment. "The woman I knew was impulsive. Emotional. She would have been at this bedside the moment the fighting stopped."

Nicole felt her spine straighten, felt the walls rise behind her eyes. "People change."

"They do. But not like this." He shifted on the table, a small movement that still made him wince. "The way you moved back there—I saw combat training, but more than that. I saw strategy. Calculation. You assessed the situation and identified the optimal path through the engagement before the first shot was fired."

"We have bigger problems than my career trajectory."

"I'm not talking about your career." Marcus set down the nutrient packet, gave her his full attention. "I'm talking about what they made you into. The Authority. Frost."

Hearing that name in Marcus's voice felt wrong. It belonged to a different part of her life—the part that came after, the part he wasn't supposed to know about. But of course he knew. He'd had four years in a cell to gather information, to piece together what had happened to the world he'd left behind.

"What I became isn't relevant right now," Nicole said. Her voice came out flat, professional—the voice she used in briefings, in debriefs, in all the situations where emotion was a liability. "What's relevant is understanding what happened at Carcosa-7 and what the Authority is actually planning."

"Nicole—"

"We pulled extensive data from the facility before we extracted you. There are files I haven't even begun to process. Research logs, communication records, project documentation. Whatever they were doing to you, whatever they wanted from you, it's in there somewhere."

Marcus was quiet for a moment. Then: "You still do that."

"Do what?"

"Redirect. Deflect. Find a problem you can analyze instead of dealing with the one you can't." A ghost of something crossed his face—not quite a smile, too sad for that. "You learned it from me. I used to do the same thing to your mother when she wanted to talk about anything that mattered."

Nicole's hands curled into fists at her sides. "Don't."

"Don't what?"

"Don't talk about her. Don't talk about us. Don't—" She stopped, caught herself, forced her breathing back under control. "We don't have the luxury of personal conversations right now. Every hour we spend processing the past is an hour the Authority has to track us, to regroup, to erase whatever evidence they need to erase."

"I spent four years in a cell thinking I would never see you again." Marcus's voice cracked, and this time he didn't try to hide it. "Four years watching everything I worked for get twisted into something monstrous. Four years wondering what happened to you after they told the world I was dead.”

Nicole's jaw tightened. She could feel the pressure building behind her eyes, the tears that wanted to come, the grief that demanded release. She shoved it down. Boxed it. Filed it away in the same place she'd learned to store everything that threatened to compromise her effectiveness.

"I survived," she said. "Obviously."

"That's not an answer."

"It's the only one I have right now."

Marcus held her gaze for a long moment. She watched him read her face, watched him catalog the changes—the harder lines around her mouth, the way she held herself like she was always expecting an attack, the distance she maintained like armor.

"Alright," he said finally. Quietly. "The mission, then. What do you need to know?"



Nicole moved further into the medical bay—not to the bedside, but to the diagnostic station along the far wall. She pulled up the screens, let the data wash over her like cold water. This she could handle. This was manageable.

"Project Prometheus," she said. "That's what the files reference. You were the lead researcher."

"I was." Marcus shifted on the table, trying to find a position that didn't strain his atrophied muscles. "Before I understood what they actually intended to do with it." He stared at the ceiling, his voice flattening into something that sounded rehearsed, like a confession he'd given a thousand times in his own head. "They weren't trying to save lives, Nicole. They were trying to weaponize death itself. And I handed them the blueprint.

Chapter 12

The Shapes of Silence

The ship was moving.

Marcus Vane felt it in his bones before his conscious mind could name it—the subtle thrum of engines, the faint directional pull of artificial gravity that wasn't quite calibrated to standard. For twenty-five years, he had known only the unchanging stasis of his cell, the perfect stillness of walls that never shifted. Now everything moved, and his body didn't know what to do with the information.

He sat on the edge of a narrow bunk in what appeared to be crew quarters, his hands gripping the mattress beneath him like a man afraid of floating away. The room was small—perhaps three meters by four—but it felt impossibly vast after his cell. No restraints. No force field humming at the threshold. The door was closed, but when he'd tested it earlier, it had opened at his touch. Unlocked. Unmonitored, as far as he could tell.

Free.

The word sat strangely in his mind, a foreign concept that his thoughts kept circling without quite grasping. Nicole had come for him—the woman he'd loved, whom he'd last seen twenty-five years ago in the hours before he faked his death—and she had extracted him from a Blacksite facility that shouldn't have been penetrable.

And she had done it without meeting his eyes once during the extraction.

When he felt stable enough to stand, Marcus rose slowly and crossed to the door. His legs felt uncertain beneath him, unaccustomed to walking distances longer than the six steps his cell had permitted. But he needed to move. He needed to see where he was, what manner of vessel had carried him away from hell.

And he needed to understand who Nicole had become.



The corridor beyond his quarters was narrow but functional, its walls a dull gray metal marked by conduit runs and access panels. Marcus walked slowly, trailing one hand along the wall for balance. The ship had a lived-in quality that Authority vessels lacked—tool marks visible in the metalwork, personal touches scattered here and there. A string of small lights someone had strung near a junction point. A crudely painted symbol on a bulkhead door that he didn't recognize.

He passed what appeared to be a common area and paused at the threshold, peering inside. Empty now, but the signs of recent habitation cluttered every surface: cups abandoned on tables, a jacket thrown over a chair, data pads scattered across a counter near what looked like a food preparation station. The detritus of people living in close quarters.

Nicole's people. Her crew.

The thought troubled him for reasons he couldn't quite articulate. She had come for him with trained operatives, had infiltrated an Authority Blacksite with military precision. These weren't the actions of a woman chasing a ghost from her past. This was an operation—planned, resourced, executed with the kind of discipline that spoke to experience.

What had Nicole been doing for the past twenty-five years?

Marcus continued down the corridor, his curiosity overriding his uncertainty. The ship's layout suggested a modified transport vessel—bulk hauler, perhaps, retrofitted for purposes its original designers hadn't intended. He noted reinforced bulkhead doors, blast scoring that had been repaired rather than replaced, structural modifications that spoke to combat capability.



He turned a corner and nearly collided with a man emerging from a side passage.

The stranger reacted instantly, one hand dropping to the weapon holstered at his hip. Marcus froze, his own reflexes sluggish after years of disuse, and for a moment they simply stared at each other.

The man was younger than Marcus—mid-thirties, perhaps, with dark skin and close-cropped hair. His face bore the kind of controlled alertness that Marcus recognized from Authority military personnel, but something about his posture suggested a different kind of training. More flexible. Less institutional.

"You're the package," the man said. Not a question.

"I prefer Marcus."

The stranger's eyes narrowed slightly, assessing. "Jace. Ship security." He didn't offer his hand, didn't relax his stance. "Shouldn't be wandering."

"The door was unlocked."

"Yeah, your door. Not an invitation to take a tour."

Marcus studied him, reading what he could from body language and positioning. Jace stood like a man accustomed to violence—weight balanced, limbs loose, gaze tracking Marcus's hands even while his eyes maintained contact. But there was something else there, too. A guardedness that went beyond simple wariness of a stranger.

"You don't trust me," Marcus observed.

"Don't trust anyone I haven't vetted myself." Jace's jaw tightened. "Haven't vetted you."

"Then vet me. Ask your questions."

For a moment, something flickered across Jace's face—surprise, perhaps, at the directness. Then his expression smoothed into careful neutrality. "Not my call. Captain decides how we handle assets."

Assets. The word landed with the weight of implication. Not guests. Not rescued prisoners. Assets.

"The Captain," Marcus repeated. "You mean Nicole."

Jace's only response was a fractional lift of his chin—confirmation without admission.

"She leads this crew?"

"She leads this ship."

"And what kind of ship requires the kind of operation I witnessed back at the Blacksite?"

Jace's stance shifted almost imperceptibly, his weight settling more firmly. "Kind of ship that does what needs doing. Kind of crew that doesn't ask questions they don't need answered." He paused, and when he spoke again, his voice carried an edge of warning. "Might want to remember that."

Marcus nodded slowly, taking the measure of this man who stood between him and the rest of the vessel. Jace was dangerous—that much was clear. But the danger wasn't random or malicious. This was controlled capability, held in check by discipline and loyalty.

Loyalty to Nicole.

"I'll stay out of the way," Marcus said carefully. "But I've been in a cell for twenty-five years. I need to move. I need to see something other than walls."

Jace considered this for a long moment. Then he stepped aside, gesturing down the corridor. "Galley's that way. Observation deck at the end. Everything else is off-limits until the Captain says otherwise." His hand remained near his weapon. "And Vane? Don't make me regret not putting you back in that room."

Marcus moved past him, feeling those watchful eyes tracking his every step.



He found the observation deck as Jace had directed—a small compartment at the ship's bow with a wide viewport that looked out into the star-scattered darkness. Marcus stood before it, watching unfamiliar constellations drift past, and tried to remember what it felt like to exist in a universe larger than six steps by four.

The door whispered open behind him.

He turned to find Nicole standing in the entrance, her expression carved from stone. She'd changed out of her tactical gear into simpler clothing, her dark hair pulled back from a face that held no warmth for him. But Marcus barely registered these details. He was too busy seeing the ghost of the young woman she'd been—twenty-three and fierce and certain—in the hard lines of the soldier she'd become.

"Nicole—"

"Jace told me you were wandering." She stepped inside, the door closing behind her. "Said you handled it well. Didn't push back, didn't make trouble."

"Should I have?"

"No." She moved to stand beside him, though she kept her gaze fixed on the viewport rather than meeting his eyes. "I need to explain some things. About what you're going to see on this ship. About what I've become."

Marcus waited, his heart hammering against his ribs.

"During the extraction," Nicole said slowly, "you saw what I could do. The speed. The precision." She paused. "The armor."

"I saw."

"Did you recognize it?"

The question landed like a blow. Marcus closed his eyes, and behind his lids he saw the classified schematics, the integration matrices, the endless nights spent perfecting a design he'd told himself was defensive technology.

"The Obsidian Pulse," he said quietly. "I designed the original framework. Thirty years ago, for Oversight."

"You designed a concept." Nicole's voice remained flat, professional. "Other people turned it into reality. And then they needed operators who could survive the integration process." Finally, she turned to face him. "They trained me for two years before they let me near the integration chair. Psychological conditioning, combat programming, sensory augmentation. They rebuilt me at the cellular level, Marcus. They unmade the woman you remember and constructed something else in her place."

His name in her mouth, spoken like that—cold, clinical—cut deeper than any accusation could have.

"Nicole, I didn't know what they would do with my work. I didn't—"

"Didn't what? Didn't consider that Oversight might use your weapons for violence?" Her laugh held no humor. "You designed armor that could turn a single operative into a walking extinction event. What did you think they were going to do with it?"

Marcus had no answer. Every justification he'd offered himself during his years of imprisonment—I didn't know, I couldn't have known, I was misled—withered in the face of Nicole's cold fury.

"I didn't extract you because I wanted a reunion," she continued, her voice clinical now, distant. "I didn't do it for sentiment or family feeling or whatever other motivation you're imagining. I did it because Oversight is planning something, and stopping them requires assets I don't currently have."

Assets. The same word Jace had used. Marcus heard it differently now—not as a categorization, but as a protection. If he was an asset, he was useful. If he was useful, she didn't have to think about what else he might be.

"What do you need from me?"

"Information. Technical specifications. Everything you know about Oversight's classified projects." Nicole's expression remained closed, impenetrable. "You worked for them for fifteen years before your imprisonment. You know things—facility locations, operational protocols, scientific programs they don't want discovered."

"And after you have what you need?"

She didn't answer. She didn't need to.

Marcus looked at Nicole—at the weapon she had become—and he understood, finally, the shape of the silence between them. She had rescued him, yes. But she didn't love him anymore. She might not even be capable of it anymore, not after what Oversight had done to her.

What he had helped make possible.

"I'll tell you everything I know," he said quietly. "Whatever I can remember, whatever might be useful. You don't have to ask twice."

Nicole studied him for a long moment. Then she nodded, a single sharp motion. "Tomorrow. Get some rest tonight—you look like you're about to collapse."

She turned toward the door, then paused.

"The crew doesn't know who you are. What you built." Her voice carried a warning. "I'd keep it that way."

She left without waiting for a response.

Marcus stood alone before the viewport, watching the stars blur past, and remembered—with the clarity that only comes from profound regret—the first time he'd sketched those preliminary designs. He had been excited that day. Proud of the elegance of his concepts. Certain that he was building something remarkable.

He had been right, in a way. He had built something remarkable.

He had built the foundation for her transformation into a living weapon.

And now, twenty-five years later, he would have to find a way to live with that knowledge—in the belly of a ship crewed by dangerous people who answered to a woman who no longer knew how to love him.

Marcus pressed his palm against the cold viewport glass and let the tears come silently, his reflection a ghost superimposed over the indifferent stars.

Chapter 13

First Blood

The proximity alarm shattered the mess hall's quiet—three sharp pulses followed by a sustained tone Nicole recognized as threat classification immediate.

She was on her feet before conscious thought engaged, the datapad clattering against the table where she'd been reviewing their course through the Dead Sectors. Across the room, Marcus Vane moved with her, his position near the doorway—where he'd spent the last hour observing both exits while pretending to relax—granting him a clear path to the corridor.

"Bridge! Now!"

They ran. Nicole heard Jace's boots hammering from engineering, heard Sera's curse from somewhere aft. The Widow's Edge wasn't large, but those seconds stretched into something eternal.

Kira had the displays up when Nicole slid into the tactical station, holographic threat indicators already painting the air in shades of red.

"Multiple contacts, bearing one-seven-mark-three." Kira's hands never stopped moving. "Five ships. Military configuration. They're running minimal signatures, but the drive patterns—these are pursuit craft."

Military configuration. Not corporate security, not local enforcement. Nicole's stomach tightened.

"How long to weapons range?"

"Eight minutes. Maybe less if they push it."

Jace reached his station, Sera a step behind him. Nicole watched the five blips converge on their position with relentless precision, her mind cycling through possibilities even as she brought targeting systems online. A tracking beacon they'd missed—but Jace had swept the ship twice. A compromised comm channel—but they'd established new protocols at every jump. A random sighting at the last refueling stop—possible, but the timing was too precise.

Predictive modeling. An embedded asset in their equipment. Or capabilities she'd never been cleared to know about.

Or Marcus himself. Twenty-five years in Directorate custody. Implanted tracking technology would be simple, invisible, impossible to detect without specialized equipment they didn't have.

She filed it away. All of it. Survive first. Investigate later.

Marcus moved toward the communications console, his hand hovering over the controls. He glanced back at her—a silent request.

"Do it," she said. "You know these tactics better than I do."

He nodded once and began adjusting frequencies. The tremor she'd noticed in his hands over the past days had steadied, his movements becoming fluid as instinct seized control.

"Their formation," Marcus said. "Containment pattern. Two wings, one center. The lead ship pushes, the others close off escape vectors."

"You've seen this before?"

"I've used this before. It's effective against runners. Forces them into predictable patterns until there's nowhere left to go."

"So how do we break it?"

His pale eyes met hers. "You don't. Not by running." Something shifted in his expression—a shadow of the commander he had been, rising through layers of doubt and time. "You make them think they have you contained. Then you punch through where they're weakest."

"Which is?"

"The lead ship. They expect you to flee. If you turn and fight—"

The communications console crackled to life.

"Unregistered vessel Widow's Edge, this is Coalition Naval Intelligence pursuit element. You are ordered to power down engines and prepare for boarding. Any resistance will be met with lethal force. You have thirty seconds to comply."

The words struck Nicole like a physical blow. CNI. Not prison response, not standard military. Coalition Naval Intelligence ran hunter-killer operations—sanctioned eliminations, black operations, missions that never appeared in official records.

"They're not here to arrest us," she said. "They're here to kill us."

"Yes," Marcus said simply. "They are."

Five people. One aging freighter. A CNI pursuit element.

"Kira." Nicole's voice had hardened into something she barely recognized. "On my mark, full burn toward the lead ship."

"Toward them?"

"Marcus, you said the lead ship is the weak point. Is that still true if they know we're coming?"
