"They won't believe it. Not at first. Runners run—it's what they expect, what their formation is designed to exploit. By the time they realize we're serious, we'll be inside their engagement envelope."

"And then?"

"Then we kill them before their wings can respond."

Nicole looked at Kira. "Do it."

"Nicole—"

"Now."

Kira's hesitation lasted less than a second. Then her hands moved, and the Widow's Edge surged forward.

The acceleration pressed Nicole back against her console, the compensators whining in protest. On the display, their blip closed the distance toward the enemy formation—five against one, suicide odds by any reasonable calculation.

"They're adjusting," Marcus reported. "Lead ship accelerating to meet us. The wings are moving to flank."

"How long until the wings reach firing position?"

"Ninety seconds. Maybe less."

"Jace, divert all available power to forward shields and weapons."

"Life support?"

"Minimum levels. We won't need air if we're dead."

The ship's systems shifted as power rerouted. On the display, the lead CNI ship swelled larger—a Viper-class pursuit craft, fast and heavily armed, crewed by officers who had probably trained their entire careers for exactly this moment.

"Forty seconds to weapons range," Kira announced.

"Hold course."

"Their weapons are charging."

"Hold."

The lead ship fired first. Twin streams of coherent energy lanced across the void, and Kira threw the Widow's Edge into a spiraling roll that sent Nicole's stomach lurching. The shots passed close enough to scorch their portside hull plating.

"Return fire!"

The particle cannon spoke. A bar of white light caught the lead Viper on its starboard wing. The enemy ship's shields flared, absorbing most of the impact, but Nicole saw the telltale flicker of stress.

"Again!"

Another shot, another hit. The Viper's shields buckled, a visible crack appearing in the energy envelope.

"Wings are closing!" Marcus called. "Thirty seconds!"

"Kira, roll us ninety degrees. Give me their engine section."

The ship twisted, artificial gravity straining. Nicole's hands went white-knuckled on the console as she fought to maintain her targeting lock. The Viper loomed in her display, its damaged shields struggling to regenerate.

She fired the missiles.

Both weapons streaked away, drives burning hot and fast. The Viper's point defenses tried to intercept—one missile died in countermeasure fire—but the other punched through, slamming into the pursuit craft's exposed engine section.

The explosion was silent in the vacuum. A ship died, and with it the officers aboard. CNI personnel. Soldiers following orders.

She didn't have time for guilt.

"Lead ship destroyed," Jace reported, his voice tight with disbelief. "The wings are—they're breaking off."

It was true. The four remaining Vipers had abandoned their containment pattern, scattering into defensive formations.

"They didn't expect us to fight," Marcus said quietly. "When we killed their lead ship, they reassessed."

"Slipstream calculations complete!" Kira announced. "I have a viable exit vector, but we need to go now."

"Do it."

The Widow's Edge shuddered as Kira punched them into slipstream, the stars stretching into infinite lines before dissolving into the gray-white nothing of faster-than-light travel. Nicole watched the tactical display until the last enemy blip faded, then released a breath she hadn't realized she was holding.



The bridge fell quiet. Jace slumped in his chair, pale with aftermath. Sera had retreated to the back of the compartment, her expression unreadable. Kira kept her hands on the controls, though slipstream navigation was automated.

Nicole stood at the center of it all.

"Coalition Naval Intelligence," she said finally. "Hunter-killers."

"Yes," Marcus confirmed.

"Those aren't standard pursuit assets. CNI doesn't deploy for prison breaks."

"No. They don't."

Nicole turned to face him. Something had changed in Marcus Vane—he stood straighter, movements more certain, as though the fight had reminded him of who he had been.

"You knew what they were the moment you saw their formation."

"I suspected. CNI has been running operations like this for decades. Sanctioned eliminations. The kind of work that never makes it into official records."

"And now they're hunting us."

"They're hunting you, specifically. Whoever ordered this wants to ensure you never have a chance to speak about what you know."

Nicole thought about the facility. The prisoners who shouldn't have existed. The experiments she'd barely glimpsed.

"The Directorate," she whispered.

"Yes. Whatever you stumbled into—whoever those prisoners were—the people responsible have decided silence is worth killing for."

Nicole's hands curled into fists. She had spent her career believing in the system. Now she was running for her life while sanctioned assassins pursued her across the Dead Sectors.

"Then we need to know what those secrets are. We need to understand why they're so afraid."

"And how do you propose to do that?"

"We find someone who knows more than we do."

Jace looked up. "That's a short list. Everyone on it either works for them or is dead."

"Not everyone," Sera said. She'd moved closer, arms crossed. "There are people in the black markets who trade in classified data. Brokers. Fixers."

"You have contacts?"

"A name. Someone who used to move information for the resistance cells during the Fringe Wars. Went independent after the armistice. If anyone knows what the Directorate is hiding, it's him."

"Where?"

"Thessaly Station. Three jumps through the Narrows."

Nicole looked at her crew—the people who had followed her into the unknown and fought beside her.

"The Narrows are contested space. Corporate militias, independent warlords, Directorate patrols. No guarantee this contact helps us."

"There's no guarantee of anything," Sera replied. "But right now, we're flying blind. We don't know who's hunting us, we don't know why, and we don't know how they found us."

"That last one worries me most," Nicole said. She pulled up the tactical logs. "We were running dark. Transponder spoofed, comms encrypted, random course corrections."

"Either they got incredibly lucky," Marcus said, "or they have something we haven't considered."

Nicole met his gaze. The obvious possibility hung between them—the one she'd filed away during the combat, the one she couldn't ignore now.

"You were in Directorate custody for twenty-five years," she said carefully. "Long enough for them to implant any kind of tracking technology they wanted."

Marcus went still. The bridge seemed to hold its breath.

"Yes," he said finally. "I've considered that."

"And?"

"I don't know. I have no memory of any procedure, but that means nothing—they could have done it while I was sedated, during any of a hundred medical examinations. I have no way to verify whether I'm carrying something."

"Then we need to find out."

"Agreed." His voice was steady, but Nicole saw the shadow in his eyes—the weight of possibly being the weapon turned against people who had saved him. "When we reach Thessaly, we find someone with scanning equipment capable of detecting embedded technology. Until then…"

"Until then, we operate on the assumption that we might be compromised." Nicole looked at the others. "Full sweep of the ship anyway. Every system, every component. If there's something else—a beacon in our equipment, anything—I want it found."

"And if we don't find anything?" Kira asked quietly.

"Then we know where to look next."

Marcus nodded slowly. The acknowledgment of his potential role as unwitting tracker hung in the air, but he didn't flinch from it. Nicole filed that away too—the measure of a man willing to face the possibility that his freedom had come with invisible chains.

"Thessaly Station," she said. "Three jumps through the Narrows. We find Sera's contact, we get answers, and we figure out our next move."

Chapter 14

What You Carry

The bunk was precisely two meters long, one meter wide, and seventy centimeters from the deck—dimensions Marcus had measured during his first hour aboard the Widow's Edge, the same way he'd measured every cell he'd occupied for the past five years. The habit persisted even now, weeks after escaping the black site, his mind still cataloguing the geometry of confinement.

He lay on his back, hands folded across his chest, staring at the pipes and conduits that webbed across the ceiling. The position was wrong for sleep—he knew that. In Sentinel custody, guards had made rounds every forty-five minutes, their boots echoing down metal corridors with metronomic precision. He'd learned to sleep in twenty-minute intervals, jerking awake at the slightest sound, ready to sit up and demonstrate compliance before the viewport in his cell door flickered to life.

The Widow's Edge was quiet at night. Too quiet. His body couldn't process the silence, kept interpreting it as the held breath before violence.

Twenty-three minutes since he'd last checked the time. He didn't look again. Knowing wouldn't help.

The ship hummed around him—life support cycling through its processes, the distant throb of engines maintaining their drift course through the sector. Normal sounds. Safe sounds. His pulse didn't care. It hammered against his ribs like something trying to escape, responding to threats that existed only in the muscle memory of trauma.

Marcus closed his eyes. Counted breaths. Made it to twelve before the darkness behind his lids became the darkness of the sensory deprivation chamber they'd used during his third year, when Director Frost had decided conventional interrogation wasn't producing results fast enough.

He opened his eyes. The ceiling pipes hadn't moved. The bunk was still two meters long, one meter wide, seventy centimeters from the deck. He was aboard a ship with people who had chosen to fight alongside him, not guards calculating his compliance metrics.

Sleep wasn't coming. He'd known that before he'd even tried.

Marcus sat up, swinging his legs over the edge of the bunk. The deck was cold through his socks—he'd stopped sleeping in boots only recently, another conditioning response that was slow to fade. In custody, keeping your boots on meant you were ready. Ready for transfer, ready for interrogation, ready for the violence that could erupt at any moment when you were housed among desperate people.

He stood, dragging a hand through hair that still felt too short. The black site barbers had kept it cropped close, eliminating any possibility of prisoners hiding contraband or using their own bodies as weapons. Sentinel efficiency extended to the smallest details.

The corridor outside his quarters stretched in both directions, dim emergency lighting casting everything in shades of amber and shadow. Ship's night, the Widow's Edge running on a twenty-four-hour cycle that bore no relation to any planet's rotation. The others would be asleep—Jace in the cockpit where he preferred to stay close to his systems, Kira in the quarters she'd claimed near the medical bay, Eve powered down or whatever the combat drone did when she wasn't needed.

And Nicole. Somewhere aboard this ship, probably sleeping soundly like someone who hadn't spent five years having her circadian rhythms systematically destroyed.

Marcus started walking. Not toward anywhere in particular—just moving, because motion was the only thing that quieted his racing thoughts. He'd walked thousands of kilometers in his cell, four steps in one direction, four steps back, wearing a path in the deck plating that the guards had eventually noticed and punished. Walking without walls felt different. Better. He could go as far as the ship allowed, turn when he chose, stop when he wanted.

Small freedoms. The kind he'd forgotten existed.

The Widow's Edge wasn't large, but it had the compressed complexity of vessels designed for long missions with minimal crew. Corridors branched and reconnected, creating routes that could be varied enough to satisfy his restless legs. He passed the galley, dark and empty, smelling faintly of the meal Kira had synthesized for dinner. Past the common area where Jace's equipment cases remained stacked against one wall, blinking status lights the only illumination. Past the sealed door to the cargo hold—

Not sealed. Cracked open, light bleeding through the gap.

Marcus stopped. Someone else was awake. The odds said Jace, who kept erratic hours and often worked through ship's night on projects he didn't explain. But something about the quality of the light, the absolute silence from within, made his pulse quicken in a way that had nothing to do with prison conditioning.

He knew who was in there before he pushed the door open. Knew it the way he'd always known when she was near, some frequency of awareness that five years in a concrete box hadn't managed to extinguish.

Nicole sat cross-legged on a cargo crate, her disassembled rifle spread across a cloth in front of her. The work lights above cast harsh shadows across her face as she ran a brush through the weapon's barrel, her movements precise and meditative. She didn't look up when the door opened.

"Can't sleep either," she said. Not a question.

Marcus stepped inside, letting the door close behind him. The cargo hold was cold, temperature regulation apparently lower priority in spaces not regularly occupied. Nicole wore only a tank top and fatigue pants, seemingly unbothered by the chill.

"No." He stayed near the door, uncertain of his welcome. "Sorry. Didn't mean to intrude."

"You're not intruding." She still didn't look up, but something in her voice shifted—less flat, more complicated. "This is a common space."

"You seem like you wanted to be alone."

"I seem like I'm cleaning my weapons at oh-three-hundred because my brain won't stop running scenarios about everything that could go wrong tomorrow." Nicole finally raised her eyes, and Marcus felt the impact of her gaze like a physical blow. "Ritual. It helps."

He understood that. Had his own rituals, though none of them involved anything as productive as weapons maintenance. Mostly he just walked and counted and tried not to remember.

"I could leave," he offered.

"I know you could." She held his gaze for another moment, then returned to her rifle. "There's a crate that's not covered in gun oil. If you want to sit."

It wasn't quite an invitation. It wasn't quite a dismissal. Marcus found the crate she'd indicated, positioned about two meters from where she worked, and lowered himself onto it carefully. The metal was cold through his pants.

Silence stretched between them, filled by the soft sounds of her cleaning—the rasp of the brush, the click of components being checked and set aside. Marcus watched her hands move, remembering a time when those hands had touched him without the weight of everything that came after.

"The bunk is wrong," he said finally.

Nicole's hands paused. "What?"

"Too soft. Too quiet. I keep waking up expecting guards." He wasn't sure why he was telling her this. Maybe because she was here, awake in the small hours just like him. Maybe because he'd spent five years with no one to tell anything. "In custody, they condition you. Punishment for sleeping too deeply, rewards for staying alert. After enough time, your body forgets how to rest."

"Sounds like hell."

"It was efficient." The word came out bitter. "Frost was very proud of his rehabilitation protocols. Said he could break anyone's resistance given enough time."

Nicole set down her brush, giving him her full attention for the first time since he'd entered. "Did he? Break you?"

Marcus turned the question over seriously. He'd asked himself the same thing, in the dark hours when his thoughts turned cruel. Had they broken him? He'd kept their secrets for five years. Never told them about the Bastion files, never revealed what he knew about the Achilles Protocol, never betrayed the network of contacts he'd cultivated during his years as an operative. But he'd also never escaped. Never even tried, until Nicole's raid handed him an opportunity he hadn't earned.

"I don't know," he admitted. "Some days I think yes. Other days I'm not sure there was anything left to break."

Nicole was quiet for a long moment. When she spoke, her voice was softer than he'd heard it since his rescue. "I told myself you were dead. For years. It was easier than thinking about what they might be doing to you."

"I thought about you every day." The words came out before Marcus could stop them, five years of accumulated silence suddenly demanding release. "Every single day, Nicole. You were the only thing that kept me—" He stopped, aware that he was saying too much, pushing too hard against walls she'd made clear she wasn't ready to lower.

"Don't." Her voice went tight.

"I'm not trying to—"

"I know what you're trying to do." She picked up the brush again, but didn't resume cleaning. Just held it, knuckles white around the handle. "You're trying to explain. Make me understand why you disappeared for five years without a word, why I had to hear from a Sentinel prosecutor that the man I—that you had been captured during a solo operation I didn't even know about."

"That's not—"

"You left me, Marcus." Now she did look at him, and there was old pain in her eyes, the kind that had had years to calcify into something harder than anger. "Whatever your reasons, whatever noble justification you've been constructing in that cell, the fact remains. You left. You didn't tell me about the mission. You didn't give me a chance to have your back. And then you were just gone, and I spent months—" Her voice cracked. She looked away.

Marcus didn't move. Didn't try to close the distance between them, though every instinct screamed at him to cross those two meters and take her in his arms the way he would have done once.

"I tried to forget you," Nicole continued, quieter now. "Threw myself into the rebellion. Told myself it didn't matter what happened to one captured operative when billions of people were suffering under Sentinel control. Tried to make you small in my memory. Insignificant." She laughed, a harsh sound without humor. "I failed. Obviously. Or I wouldn't have spent three years planning a raid on a black site that wasn't even strategically important."

"Nicole—"

"I found you by accident. You know that, right?" She met his eyes again. "We were after intelligence on the Achilles Protocol. You weren't even supposed to be there. And then we breached the detention level and there you were, in that cell, looking like a ghost, and I—"

She stopped. Breathed. Marcus watched her collect herself, watched the soldier reassert control over the woman who'd briefly surfaced.

"I thought I was over you," she said. "I was wrong."

Marcus felt something loosen in his chest—some knot of tension he'd been carrying since the moment he'd seen her face through the cell door, illuminated by muzzle flash and emergency lights. She hadn't moved on. Hadn't forgotten. The knowledge was painful and precious in equal measure.

"I need to explain," he said carefully. "About the mission. About why I didn't tell you."

"No."

"Nicole, please—"

"I said no." She held up a hand, and there was something in her expression he couldn't quite read. Not anger. Not rejection. Something more complicated. "I'm not ready to hear it. Whatever you're going to say, whatever justification you've built—I can't process it right now. Too much is happening. The mission tomorrow, the crew depending on us, the constant certainty that Frost is out there hunting us." She shook her head. "If I let myself feel all of this right now, I'll break. And I can't break. Not yet."

He wanted to argue. Five years of silence, five years of holding onto the truth of what he'd done and why, and she was asking him to keep waiting. But he remembered what she'd said earlier—about rituals, about keeping herself functional when everything threatened to fall apart. So he swallowed the words he'd rehearsed in a hundred prison cells and simply nodded. "When you're ready," he said. "I'll be here." She didn't answer—but she didn't leave either, and for now, that was enough.

Chapter 15

The Handler's Game

The secure channel's authentication stages flickered across Sera's console—handshake protocols nesting inside paranoid encryption that Nicole neither fully appreciated nor possessed patience for. She sat in the Widow's Edge's cramped communications alcove, watching each verification crawl past, her jaw clenched tight enough to ache.

"Stage eleven," Sera murmured, her fingers dancing across haptic interfaces that bloomed and collapsed like luminescent flowers. "He's routed through… let me see… eight different relay stations. Two of them are supposedly decommissioned military hardware."

"Careful bastard."

"Smart bastard. There's a difference." Sera glanced up, green-streaked hair falling across her eyes. "You sure about this? Once we open the channel, he'll have confirmation we're still breathing. That information has value."

"Everything has value to him." Nicole's knuckles cracked—an old tension habit. "That's why I need answers. Someone pointed CNI at us, and if it wasn't the Handler—"

The console chimed. A soft pulse of amber light.

"Connection established," Sera said. "Audio only, as requested. He's being careful."

Nicole leaned toward the pickup. "You're breathing, which means you're listening. Start talking."

A pause stretched through the static—the particular kind of silence that belonged to someone choosing their words with surgical precision. When the Handler's voice emerged, it carried the same cultured neutrality she remembered from their first meeting, like a sommelier describing a particularly complex vintage of poison.

"Bastion. I must say, your continued existence is a pleasant surprise. The odds were… unfavorable."

"CNI almost had us. Twice." Nicole let the words hang, heavy with implication. "They knew where we were, Handler. They knew our heading, our timeline. They were waiting."

"An unfortunate development."

"That's not an answer."

Another pause. She could picture him—wherever he was, whatever identity he currently wore—calculating the angles, weighing what each word might cost him. The Handler dealt in information the way others dealt in currency or violence. Every exchange was a transaction, and he never surrendered anything for free.

"I assure you, Ms. Bastion, I have no knowledge of CNI's pursuit. My involvement in your current situation ended the moment payment cleared and coordinates were transmitted." His voice remained smooth, unruffled. "If I had wanted you dead, I wouldn't have dispatched the Directorate's security apparatus. Far too messy, too many variables. I prefer cleaner solutions."

"That's supposed to reassure me?"

"It's supposed to remind you that I'm a pragmatist. Your demise offers me nothing. Your continued operation, on the other hand, represents future revenue potential."

Nicole's hands curled into fists beneath the console where the pickup couldn't register the tension. "Then explain how they found us."

"I cannot explain what I do not know." A note of something that might have been frustration colored his words—or perhaps it was merely an artful imitation of frustration, designed to sell his innocence. "I'm a broker, Ms. Bastion. Not a detective. I connect buyers with sellers, facilitate extractions, arrange the impossible for those willing to pay my fees. I do not investigate the internal mechanics of my clients' motivations."

"Speaking of clients," Nicole said. "I want to know who hired you for the Vane extraction."

The silence that followed lasted exactly three seconds—long enough to communicate consideration, short enough to avoid seeming evasive. "That information falls outside the parameters of our current arrangement."

"Then let's renegotiate the parameters."

"With what leverage? You're running dark in hostile space with a fugitive the Directorate wants badly enough to mobilize significant resources. Your bargaining position is, shall we say, compromised."

Nicole smiled grimly. "I've got Vane. That means I've got whatever secrets made him valuable enough for someone to spend what I'm guessing was a considerable sum extracting him from a black site. Secrets have a way of becoming leverage, Handler. You taught me that."

More silence. Then, slowly: "You've grown more interesting since our last transaction."

"I've grown more desperate. It sharpens the mind."

Sera caught her eye, held up a hand, fingers spread. Five minutes of connection time remaining before the encryption protocols demanded a refresh. Nicole nodded, kept her focus on the audio pickup.

"The client," the Handler said finally, "used military-grade encryption. Not commercial, not criminal—military. The specifications suggested someone with access to classified communication infrastructure. Possibly active, possibly retired, definitely connected."

"Which military?"

"Directorate. The authentication codes were old—discontinued protocols from a program that officially ceased to exist nearly a decade ago." His voice took on a contemplative quality. "I found it curious enough to pursue verification, but the payment trail led nowhere. Deliberate obfuscation. Whoever hired me knew exactly how to cover their tracks."

Nicole exchanged a look with Sera, who was already pulling up additional search parameters on her secondary display. "You're telling me someone inside the Directorate wanted Vane extracted from their own facility?"

"That would be the logical conclusion, yes."

"Why?"

"I don't know." For once, the Handler's tone carried something that sounded almost like genuine admission. "And more to the point, I don't want to know. There are depths to which even I decline to dive, Ms. Bastion. Whatever game is being played here, it operates at a level I have no desire to engage with."

The words sent a chill down Nicole's spine. The Handler was many things—mercenary, amoral, calculating—but he was never afraid. The fact that he was actively avoiding information suggested the kind of danger that made even predators cautious.

"You're scared," she said.

"I'm prudent. The distinction keeps me alive."

The console flickered. Sera held up three fingers.

"One more question," Nicole pressed. "The encryption. The old military codes. Can you give me the protocol designators?"

A long pause. "You're asking me to provide data that could potentially identify the origin point of my client's communication infrastructure."

"I'm asking you to give me something I can use to protect myself. And you've already admitted you don't know who's behind this. What difference does it make?"

"The difference between information I stumbled across during standard transaction security—information I would be expected to possess—and information I actively provided to a third party." His voice remained measured, but she caught an edge beneath the polish. "Plausible deniability, Ms. Baston. When things go wrong—and at this level, things always go wrong—survival depends on what you can credibly claim not to have done."

"Then don't give it to me. Sell it." Nicole leaned closer to the pickup. "Future revenue potential, remember? Consider this an investment in continued business relations."

The silence stretched long enough that she thought he'd disconnected. Then, quietly: "The protocol signature matches a subset of Directorate military encryption. I've already said too much identifying the source as discontinued black-budget infrastructure. That's as far as I go."

"That's not enough."

"It's what you're getting. The encryption architecture is distinctive—if your technical specialist is competent, she can trace its lineage through the archived databases. But the specific program designation?" A pause. "That, you'll have to find yourself. I won't be the one who pointed you there."

The channel terminated with a soft click. Sera's console went dark, then cycled back to standby mode.

"He gave us something," Sera said, fingers already flying across her interfaces. "The encryption signature—if I can match it against the Directorate archives I pulled from the Obsidian Terminal, we might be able to identify the program."

"Do it." Nicole stood, her legs stiff from sitting in the cramped alcove. "Whatever he's scared of, I want to know—"

She stopped.

Marcus Vane stood in the doorway, his face the color of old bone, his eyes fixed on Sera's console with an expression that had nothing to do with the Handler's words—and everything to do with whatever had drawn him here in the first place.



"How much did you hear?" Nicole watched him carefully, reading the tension in his shoulders, the way his hands had gone still at his sides.

"Enough." His voice came out rough, scraped hollow. "Discontinued black-budget program. Directorate military encryption."

Sera's fingers paused over her console. "Mean something to you?"

Marcus didn't answer immediately. He stepped into the communications alcove, moving like a man wading through deep water, and lowered himself onto the edge of a storage crate. His gaze remained distant, focused on something neither of them could see.

"There was a program," he said finally. "Before. Before everything." He dragged both hands down his face, the gesture of someone trying to surface from a dream. "It was… compartmentalized. Black budget, no official acknowledgment. We were supposed to be developing new approaches to contested-space operations. Counter-insurgency, asymmetric warfare, that kind of thing." He laughed bitterly. "That's what they told us, anyway."

"And what were you actually doing?"

"I'm not sure anymore. That's the truth." He looked up at her, and she saw something raw and wounded behind his eyes. "Five years in that place, they took things from me. Memories. Context. I remember fragments—operations I can't place, faces with no names, code phrases that feel important but lead nowhere when I try to follow them."

Sera pulled up a secondary display, cascading data flowing across its surface. "Running the encryption signature against archived Directorate databases now. If there's a program match…"

"They didn't just dissolve it," Marcus said quietly. "Whatever it was. They buried it. There's a difference."

Nicole crossed her arms, studying him. "So someone from your old unit—from this buried black project—spent significant resources extracting you from a Directorate black site. And you have no idea who or why?"

"No." The word carried the weight of genuine anguish. "That's what they took from me, Nicole. The connections. The understanding of why I mattered enough to lock away, why anyone would want me out." He met her gaze. "I know I did something. I know it was important. But I can't remember what, and the not-knowing is—"

He stopped, jaw clenching.

Sera's console chimed softly. "Got something. The encryption signature—I found a match in the archived databases." She highlighted a section of her display. "Prometheus Division. Dissolved six years ago, officially due to budget reallocation. But there are gaps in the records. Big gaps."

Marcus went rigid. The name hit him like a physical blow—Nicole could see it in the way his breath caught, the sudden tension that locked his spine straight.

"Prometheus Division," he repeated, barely above a whisper. "That's… that's it. That's the name."

"You remember it?"

"No. Yes." He pressed his palms against his temples. "It's like—the shape of it fits. The space where the memories should be. But the actual memories…" He shook his head in frustration. "Gone. Just the outline of what was taken."

Sera was already pulling up additional data streams, her face bathed in the shifting glow of her displays. "The payment protocols the Handler mentioned—I'm running a comparative analysis against the encryption signature. The mathematical structure isn't just Prometheus Division standard. It's a specific subset." She highlighted another section. "This signature was only used by command-level personnel. Someone with authority over the entire operation."

"The Prometheus Division commander," Nicole said slowly.

"Or someone who inherited their access codes." Sera's fingers froze over her console. "Either way, we're not dealing with a rogue operative or a concerned colleague. Whoever funded this extraction had the keys to the entire program." She looked at Marcus. "Someone wanted you out badly enough to crack open a ghost.”

Chapter 16

Bodies Remember

The datapad's screen had gone dark three times already. Each time, Nicole coaxed it back to life, stared at the mission coordinates without comprehending them, and let her mind drift until the display surrendered again. The fourth time it happened, she hurled the pad onto her bunk and ground the heels of her hands against her eyes until she saw sparks.

The Widow's Edge hummed around her—environmental systems, the distant thrum of engines, the bone-deep vibration of a ship in transit. Familiar sounds. Comforting, even, after years of learning to sleep through them. But tonight her quarters felt too small, the air recyclers laboring overtime to scrub the staleness from a space meant for brief rest between shifts, not extended habitation.

She dropped her hands and studied the ceiling, watching the faint play of indicator lights against the metal. Thirty-seven hours to the Dead Sectors safe harbor. Thirty-seven hours of sitting with her own thoughts while Marcus Vane existed somewhere else on this ship, breathing the same recycled air, walking the same narrow corridors.

The knock at her door had come four hours ago. She'd watched the indicator panel, knowing exactly who stood on the other side by the deliberate, patient silence that followed. Three minutes he'd waited before his footsteps retreated down the corridor. Three minutes she'd spent with her hand hovering over the release panel, her heart hammering against her ribs like she was nineteen again.

Nicole swung her legs off the bunk and sat up, pressing her palms flat against her thighs. Her body felt wrong—too aware of itself, too keyed up for simple exhaustion. She knew this feeling. Hated it. Had spent two years convincing herself she'd outgrown it.

Bodies remember. The Fleet counselor had told her that once, after a mission went sideways and left her jumping at shadows for weeks. Trauma lives in the muscle, in the nerve, in the bone. Your mind can move on while your body stays frozen in the moment.

But what lived in her body now wasn't trauma. Or maybe it was—just not the kind the counselor meant.

She pushed herself off the bunk before the memories could swallow her. The corridor outside her quarters stretched empty in both directions, emergency lighting casting everything in dim amber. Ship's night cycle. Most of the Widow's Edge skeleton crew would be in their bunks or manning essential stations.

Nicole walked.

Not toward anywhere specific. Just—away. From her quarters, from the datapad, from the silence that left too much room for thinking. Her boots rang against the deck plating, each footfall a small rebellion against the stillness.

The first time had been a mistake.

That's what she'd told herself then, in the aftermath—

No. She stopped walking, pressed her palm flat against the corridor wall. The metal cooled her fingers, real, present. She wasn't doing this. Wasn't going to stand here dissecting memories while thirty-seven hours ticked down and the Dead Sectors waited.

The mission briefing materials sat untouched on her datapad. Safe harbor protocols. Intel about Coalition patrol patterns and the gaps in their coverage. All of it vital, all of it demanding her attention instead of—

She started walking again, faster now. The Widow's Edge was small enough that the corridors looped back on themselves, a single main artery with branching capillaries leading to quarters, cargo holds, the cramped engine room. Cross ran a tight ship with a minimal crew; as a guest, Nicole had limited access, limited options. The observation deck, such as it was. The small common area. The training bay.

The training bay.

She heard it before she reached the doorway—the rhythm of bodies in motion, the sharp exhales of controlled exertion. Sound carried strangely on ships this size, bouncing through ventilation shafts and maintenance corridors. Nicole's feet had brought her here without conscious decision, drawn by something her mind was still pretending not to recognize.

Marcus and Jace circled each other on the practice mat.

They'd stripped down to basic workout gear, their movements fluid despite the cramped space. Jace was faster—younger, less burdened by old injuries—but Marcus had reach and experience, his body still carrying the coiled efficiency of Fleet combat training.

Nicole watched from the doorway as Marcus deflected a strike, redirected Jace's momentum, and used the pilot's own aggression to throw him off-balance. The move was precise, economical—and then Marcus followed it with something that wasn't. An elbow strike that came a half-second too fast, carrying an edge of real aggression before he pulled it back.

Jace rolled with it, came up grinning. "Getting slow, old man. Sera hits harder than that."

"Sera fights dirty." Marcus reset his stance, but Nicole caught the way his shoulders stayed tight. The coiled energy that hadn't dissipated with the sparring. "You want to learn how to win, or you want to learn how to survive?"

"There's a difference?"

"The difference is what you're willing to do when winning stops being an option."

The words landed with weight that had nothing to do with training. Nicole saw Jace's grin falter, saw him recalibrate. He was young, but not stupid—he'd caught the undercurrent.

This is the Marcus she'd known. Not the stoic protector of her memories, but the man with edges sharp enough to cut. The one who'd pushed back when she took stupid risks, who'd told her exactly what he thought even when it meant fighting. The one whose patience was a discipline, not a temperament—something he chose because the alternative was something darker.

She remembered now: the argument three days before everything ended. The way his voice had gone quiet and cold when she'd accused him of holding back in tactical briefings, of undermining her authority by going around her to their commanding officer. He'd let her finish her tirade, let her throw every accusation she'd been nursing for weeks, and then he'd said, "You're right. I don't trust your judgment anymore. And if you can't figure out why, we have bigger problems than my chain of command."

They'd barely spoken for those three days. And then the investigation started, and speaking became impossible.

Marcus stepped back from Jace, signaling a pause, and reached for a water container. His eyes found hers.

The training bay went silent. Jace, following Marcus's gaze, turned to look at her with an expression that shifted rapidly from confusion to realization to a diplomatic decision to be elsewhere.

"Captain." Jace grabbed his jacket from the bench against the wall. "I was just heading out. Need to check on some calibrations with Sera anyway."

"Jace—" Nicole started, but he was already sliding past her with a nod, and then the door hissed shut behind him and she was alone with Marcus.

Neither of them spoke.

The training bay's ventilation hummed. Somewhere deeper in the ship, she could hear the engines working. Marcus stood on the practice mat with water dripping down his chin, his chest rising and falling from exertion, his eyes locked on hers with an intensity that made her want to bolt.

"I wasn't looking for you," she said.

"I know." He set down the water container. Didn't move toward her. "Ship's small. Bound to happen eventually."

"We should probably talk about that."

"Probably."

Neither of them moved.

Nicole felt the silence stretching, filling with everything they weren't saying. Her body remembered his hands, his mouth, the sound of his voice saying her name in the dark. Remembered the way it felt to be known by someone who had seen her at her worst and stayed anyway.

But it also remembered the end. The investigation that had torn through their unit like shrapnel. The way he'd shut her out, refused to let her help, refused to let her share the fallout. The morning she'd woken up to find his quarters empty, his resignation filed, his name already being scrubbed from the duty roster.

No goodbye. No explanation. Just silence, and then two years of it.

"You left." The words came out harder than she intended. "When everything fell apart, you just—left. You didn't let me help. You didn't even let me know what was happening until it was already over."

Marcus's jaw tightened. "I was protecting you."

"I didn't need your protection. I needed you to trust me."

"Nicole—" He stopped, exhaled. The fight drained from his shoulders, replaced by something that looked like exhaustion. "There was no version of that situation where you came out clean if you were connected to me. Frost was building a case. He was looking for any leverage he could find, and you—you were the biggest leverage I had."

"So you cut me out."

"I cut you loose. There's a difference."

"Is there?" She stepped into the training bay, let the door slide shut behind her. The space felt smaller now, the air thicker. "Because from where I stood, it looked exactly the same. You decided what was best for me without asking what I wanted. You made the choice and then you disappeared before I could fight you on it."

"Would you have let me go if I'd asked?"

The question hung between them.

Nicole opened her mouth to say yes—of course, she wasn't someone who clung, wasn't someone who couldn't survive alone—but the lie wouldn't come. She'd have fought. She'd have dug in. She'd have put herself directly in Frost's crosshairs trying to protect Marcus from the consequences of whatever had happened, and they both knew it.

"That's what I thought." His voice was quieter now. "I did what I had to do. I'm not going to apologize for it."

"I'm not asking you to apologize." She closed the distance between them, stopped when she was close enough to touch him but didn't. "I'm asking you to stop deciding what I can handle. I'm asking you to actually talk to me instead of making calls behind my back and expecting me to live with the fallout."

Marcus met her eyes. Up close, she could see the lines that hadn't been there two years ago, the weight of exile written into his features. But underneath it—underneath all of it—he was still the man she remembered. The one whose patience was a discipline, whose protectiveness was a flaw, whose stubbornness matched her own.

"You're right," he said. "About all of it. I shut you out. I made the decision for both of us and didn't give you a say." He paused. "And I've spent two years telling myself it was the right call while knowing exactly how much it cost."

"What do you want now?"

The question surprised her as much as it surprised him. She hadn't meant to ask it—hadn't meant to open that door when they were thirty-seven hours from a mission that might kill them both.

But she'd asked. And now they were both standing in the silence it left behind.

Marcus reached out—slowly, giving her time to pull back—and brushed his fingers against her jaw. The touch was light, questioning. Nothing like the desperate collision of bodies she'd been bracing for.

"I want to stop making decisions alone," he said. "Whatever this is, whatever we're doing—I want you to have a say in it."

Nicole felt something shift in her chest. Not resolution, exactly. Not forgiveness. But a door opening, just a crack, onto something that wasn't just memory and wasn't just pain.

"The mission comes first," she said. "Whatever we figure out, it waits until we're through the Dead Sectors. Until we know we're going to survive long enough for it to matter."

"Agreed."

"And we talk. Really talk. About what happened, about what went wrong, about—" She stopped, steadied herself. "About whether there's anything left worth rebuilding."

Marcus nodded. His hand dropped from her face, but he didn't step back. "Thirty-seven hours."

"Thirty-seven hours."

The training bay hummed around them. Somewhere in the ship, the engine pitch shifted—Sera adjusting their approach vector, maybe, or Cross doing whatever Cross did during the night cycle.

Nicole stepped back first.

Chapter 17

Safe Harbor

"You're still leading with a gun, Bastion." The voice drifted across the darkened transit hub, warm with amusement, its particular cadence shaped by years spent mastering languages across a dozen systems. "Some things never change."

Nicole held her weapon steady, the portable lamp on the floor throwing long shadows across the figure who stood with hands raised and empty. Behind her, Jace covered their rear while Kira monitored a handheld scanner whose readings stuttered and flickered with interference. Marcus drifted between them, his attention fragmented between the physical world and whatever digital landscape he perceived through the station's dormant systems.

"Identification, Torres."

"Really? After seven years, you can't recognize me by my charm and devastating good looks?"

"Identification."

The figure sighed theatrically and reached slowly into a jacket pocket, producing a small device that projected a holographic verification code—a sequence of symbols and numbers that matched a pattern Nicole had memorized years ago, seared into her memory alongside extraction routes and dead-drop protocols.

"Satisfied?"

Nicole lowered her weapon, gesturing for the others to do the same. "You've aged."

"And you've grown paranoid." Torres stepped into better light, revealing a compact figure in practical clothing—neither young nor old, with features that suggested origins somewhere in the Consortium's outer territories. Dark hair threaded with premature gray, eyes that moved constantly, cataloging every detail of the group. An information broker's eyes, trained to extract value from nothing. "Though given your current situation, I suppose paranoia qualifies as good sense."

"You know about our situation?"

Torres's smile sharpened. "I know about everyone's situation. It's what I do." The broker's gaze slid to Marcus, lingering there with obvious interest. "Though I'll admit, some situations prove more interesting than others. Marcus Vane. I heard you were dead."

"Rumors," Marcus said quietly.

"Clearly." Torres circled them slowly, the way a collector might examine new acquisitions. "I also heard about Carcosa-7. Someone struck a Directorate black site in Consortium space, obliterated a research facility, and walked away clean. Or mostly clean." The broker paused, professional admiration threading through the words. "My contacts in the sector started flagging unusual traffic patterns within hours—emergency frequencies, encrypted Directorate channels suddenly blazing hot, CNI assets repositioning. When you know what to look for, the silence around a classified incident screams louder than any report."

Torres's eyes returned to Nicole. "The descriptions vary, but your name surfaces repeatedly, Bastion. Along with some very impressive speculation about what you extracted from that facility."

"We didn't take anything."

"Didn't you?" Torres's gaze slid back to Marcus. "Voss was running something called Project Prometheus at Carcosa-7. Neural integration, cognitive enhancement, human-machine synthesis at levels the Consortium officially considers impossible. And now here you are, traveling with a dead man who very much appears to be breathing. Coincidence, I'm sure."

"You're well informed," Nicole said carefully.

"Information is currency, and I prefer being wealthy." Torres's expression shifted, amusement dissolving into something harder. "Which is why I agreed to meet you here, despite the considerable risks. What you've done, what you've witnessed, what you've taken—it has value. But it also carries consequences. Shall we discuss them somewhere more comfortable? I've prepared refreshments. Real coffee, if you can believe it."

Nicole weighed options. Torres had always been reliable—not trustworthy, exactly, but reliably self-interested in predictable ways. "Lead the way."



Torres's quarters occupied a converted supervisor's office transformed into something between a safehouse and an operations center. Displays lined the walls, most currently dark but clearly capable of accessing data networks that shouldn't exist outside major intelligence agencies. The promised coffee was already brewing, filling the space with an aroma that seemed impossibly luxurious after weeks of synthesized ship rations.

"Make yourselves comfortable," Torres said, gesturing toward mismatched chairs salvaged from various parts of the station. "We have considerable ground to cover."

Nicole remained standing near the door. Old habits. "You said our situation carries consequences. I'm assuming you don't mean the obvious ones."

"You've been assuming you're running from two groups." Torres poured coffee into ceramic cups—actual ceramic, another extravagance—and distributed them. Kira accepted hers gratefully. Jace sniffed his suspiciously before sipping. "CNI wants you for treason and unauthorized disclosure. The Directorate wants you for attacking their facility and stealing their property." A glance at Marcus, who stood near a dark display terminal. "Or perhaps recovering their property, depending on how one categorizes Mr. Vane."

"Two groups is bleak enough," Nicole said.

"Then you'll love hearing about the third." Torres settled into a chair with obvious pleasure. "Do you know what Obsidian Solutions is?"

Nicole frowned. The name was vaguely familiar—she'd encountered it in reports years ago, during her intelligence days. "Private military contractor. Consortium-adjacent, operates in grey areas."

"That's the public profile. The reality proves considerably darker." Torres leaned forward with unfamiliar intensity. "Obsidian Solutions is what happens when corporate interests decide that national intelligence agencies are too shackled by rules, oversight, and the occasional inconvenience of morality. They're freelance operators—highly trained, lavishly funded, and utterly unconstrained by legal niceties like extradition treaties or rules of engagement."

"Corporate killers," Jace said flatly.

"The term has been used. Though 'problem solvers' is how they describe themselves." Torres's smile held no warmth. "They solve problems by making the problems disappear. Sometimes that means acquiring assets. Sometimes it means eliminating threats. Most often, it means both simultaneously."

"And they're involved in this how?"

"Someone—I haven't been able to determine who exactly, though the smart money says it's a Consortium corporate interest with ties to the Directorate's research programs—has contracted Obsidian Solutions to recover the Carcosa-7 project assets. All of them. Including any personnel with knowledge of what transpired at that facility."

Nicole felt the coffee's warmth in her stomach turn cold. "They're hunting us."

"They've been hunting you since approximately six hours after the Carcosa-7 incident." Torres leaned back. "They're not as swift as CNI or as well-connected as the Directorate, but they're considerably more flexible. Where the formal agencies must worry about diplomatic consequences and jurisdictional issues, Obsidian Solutions just… operates. No oversight, no accountability, no limits on methodology."

"Wonderful." Kira's voice was brittle. "So now we've got CNI trying to arrest us, Directorate spooks trying to silence us, and corporate mercenaries trying to—what, exactly?"

"That remains unclear. Their contract could specify acquisition or elimination—possibly both, with different target profiles for different individuals." Torres's gaze returned to Marcus. "Mr. Vane, I suspect, they want very much alive. The technology embedded in his skull is worth more than most planetary economies. The rest of you are probably secondary objectives—useful for interrogation, but ultimately disposable."

The room fell silent. Nicole processed the information, running tactical calculations that produced increasingly bleak results. Three factions hunting them. Multiple jurisdictions. Different capabilities, different motivations, but all converging toward the same outcome.

"You mentioned value," she said finally. "The information we possess. You wouldn't have called this meeting just to deliver bad news."

"Astute as always." Torres smiled thinly. "I've told you what's hunting you because that information has reciprocal value—now you know I have access to intelligence you desperately need. What I want in return is simpler: I want to know what Voss was really doing at Carcosa-7. I want to know what Marcus Vane has become. I want information that I can verify, trade, and profit from."

"Some of that information could get people killed."

"Everything valuable can get people killed. That's what makes it valuable."

Nicole looked at Marcus, who had remained silent throughout the exchange.

"What do you think?"

"I think Torres is right about Obsidian Solutions," Marcus said quietly. "I can sense them in the network traffic—not directly, but the patterns are there. Queries, searches, algorithmic hunting protocols searching for our signatures. Professional work. Expensive work." His gaze focused on Nicole. "We can't outrun all three of them. Not forever. Eventually we need allies, and allies require trust, and trust requires information."

"So we share."

"Carefully. Selectively." His attention shifted to Torres. "I'll tell you what I can verify about Project Prometheus. What I am now, what they did to forge me this way. But nothing about vulnerabilities, nothing about how to duplicate the process, and nothing about the data I carry that hasn't already been released through other channels."

Torres considered this, then nodded. "Acceptable. For now."



The station hummed around them as Torres began pulling files onto the main display—movement patterns, communication intercepts, the fragmentary intelligence picture of forces arrayed against them. Marcus answered questions with clinical precision, describing capabilities without revealing weaknesses, explaining what had been done to him without detailing how it might be undone.

Nicole watched the exchange, cataloging information while part of her mind wandered to darker places. Three years she'd spent believing Marcus was dead. Three years of grief compressed into a wound that had never fully healed. And now here he was—changed, fractured in ways she was still learning to understand, but alive. The universe had returned him to her, and promptly surrounded them both with enemies.

"The neural integration allows parallel processing at speeds that shouldn't be possible," Marcus was saying, his voice flat and clinical. "I can interface with systems, perceive patterns in information flows that human cognition isn't designed to recognize."

"Is that all?" Torres asked.

"No." Marcus looked at the broker, and Nicole saw something vast moving behind his eyes—intelligence that had been expanded, pushed beyond human limitation. "There's more. Things I haven't fully tested. Voss's people were working at the edge of what's technically possible, and I'm the result."

"The successful result?"

"The surviving result. There's a difference." His jaw tightened. "I was acquired like equipment, modified like software, tested like a prototype. Whatever I am now, I didn't choose it."

Nicole reached out, touching his arm. The gesture felt inadequate—a human response to inhuman circumstances. "You're still Marcus. Whatever they did, you're still the person who chose to help me on Carcosa-7. That's not programming. That's character."

"Is it?" His hand covered hers. "Or is it sophisticated simulation? The neural modification could have included behavioral parameters, personality constraints designed to make me seem normal while serving their purposes."

"Then trust the people who know you. Trust me."

Torres watched this exchange with obvious interest, filing it away as another piece of tradeable information. But the broker said nothing, allowing the moment to pass.

"You can establish a base of operations here," Torres said finally. "The station's facilities are primitive but functional, and the nebula provides natural interference that masks most sensor signatures. You'll be ghosts within ghosts." She smiled, teeth sharp against the dim lighting. "Of course, sanctuary has a price. And given what's hunting you, I expect that price will rise considerably before this is over."

Chapter 18

Enemy of My Enemy

The station's encrypted heart resisted him for the first forty minutes.

Marcus worked in silence, letting the neural link at the base of his skull establish its cold connection to systems that hadn't spoken to anyone in three decades. The interface was archaic—Compact military standard from the border war era—but functional. Emergency power cells had kept the core memory banks viable, a small miracle of engineering that meant whatever secrets this station held might still be readable.

If he could breach the authentication layers.

Torres had positioned herself at the junction where their entry corridor met the station's main ring, a tactical choice that made sense given their limited intel. The nebula's radiation masked them from long-range sensors, but that same interference degraded their own scanning capability. If another ship had docked on the far side of the station, they might not know until someone walked through a door. So Torres watched, and Marcus worked, and the station creaked around them like something alive settling into restless sleep.

The first encryption layer collapsed. Then the second.

The third stopped him cold.

Marcus leaned back, studying the architecture of the thing. Military-grade algorithms, yes, but layered over something stranger—a recursive pattern that seemed almost organic in its complexity. He'd encountered similar work before. Couldn't remember where.

His fingers moved to a secondary approach, testing boundaries. The system pushed back, adapted, sealed the gap he'd been probing. Fast. Too fast for static encryption.

"Adaptive security protocols," he murmured, more to himself than the room. "Someone really didn't want this found."

He tried a different angle. Failed. Tried again.

On the fourth attempt, something shifted in his hindbrain—not quite memory, more like the shadow of one, suggesting a rhythm his conscious mind couldn't name. His hands followed the suggestion before he could question it.

The encryption hesitated. Recognized something. Began to yield.



"The fragmentation's worse than I expected."

Nicole's voice came from behind him, closer than he'd anticipated. He hadn't heard her approach—too deep in the data streams cascading through his neural link, the station's damaged directories opening like reluctant flowers.

"I'm mapping the intact sections," he said without turning. "There's pattern to what survived. Someone prioritized certain archives for long-term preservation."

"Someone?" She moved into his peripheral vision, dragged over a storage crate, sat. Her eyes tracked across his displays with sharp, rapid movements—processing, categorizing. The Bastion analytical engine at work, though she'd hate him for putting it that way. "The station AI, or a person?"

"Person. The preservation choices are idiosyncratic. Emotional, even." He gestured toward a cluster of file headers. "See this? Supply manifests and shift rotations got highest-tier protection. Strategic intelligence assessments got standard. Someone cared more about remembering who worked here than what they accomplished."

Nicole went quiet for a moment. "That's not how you'd design an archive."

"No. I'd prioritize operational data. Mission records. Anything with tactical relevance." He paused, frowning at a corrupted directory that refused to resolve. "This was designed by someone who thought they might come back. Someone who wanted to remember faces."

"And didn't."

She watched him work for another few minutes. He could feel the weight of her attention, the questions she was holding back. Nicole didn't interrupt when she thought someone was onto something—a courtesy he'd learned to appreciate, and one she extended to few people.

The corrupted directory finally surrendered its ghost. Personnel records. Hundreds of them.

"There," he said. "Medical staff, research division, support crew. This station wasn't just a listening post—it was running some kind of long-term project." He scrolled through names, faces, service histories. Most meant nothing to him. One made him pause.

"What?" Nicole leaned forward.

"This researcher. Dr. Yuki Tanaka." He pulled up the file, expanded it. "Her work was in neural interface technology. Consciousness mapping. The kind of research that got very classified very fast after the war ended."

"Related to what the Directorate is doing now?"

"Maybe. The timestamps are right. If this project was running parallel to—" He stopped. Something in the file structure had caught his attention. A subdirectory, hidden beneath layers of administrative data, with encryption that felt different from everything else he'd encountered.

Not just different. Familiar.



The subdirectory fought him.

Every approach he tried, it anticipated. Every bypass he attempted, it had already blocked. The encryption wasn't just adaptive—it was predictive, as if designed by someone who knew exactly how he thought, what patterns he'd fall into, which shortcuts he'd instinctively reach for.

Marcus sat back, rubbed his eyes. His head had started to throb, the neural interface siphoning more energy than his body wanted to give after hours of sustained connection.

"Problem?" Torres's voice crackled over the comm. She'd been checking in every twenty minutes, each report identical: no movement, no contacts, no change.

"Encryption I can't crack."

"I thought that was your thing."

"It was. Is." He stared at the blinking cursor, at the wall of security that refused to yield. "This one's different."

Nicole had left an hour ago to review the personnel files he'd extracted, but now she reappeared in the doorway, datapad in hand. "I've been cross-referencing the researchers against Directorate records. Three of them show up in Frost's classified budget allocations from six years ago. Consultants."

"Anyone still alive?"

"Unknown. The records are incomplete." She moved closer, studied his display. "Still stuck on that subdirectory?"

"It's like it knows me." The words escaped before he could consider them. "Every time I find a weakness, it's already patched. Every trick I try, it's ready for."

Nicole's expression sharpened. "Knows you how?"

"I don't—" He stopped. Looked at the encryption architecture again, really looked at it, letting the patterns sink into some deeper level of processing. The recursive structures. The predictive algorithms. The particular rhythm of the security responses.

He knew that rhythm.

His hands moved to the console, typed a query he hadn't consciously decided to make. The system responded with an author tag, buried deep in the encryption's foundational code:

M. VANE - CLEARANCE LEVEL OMEGA

The throb in his head became a spike. Marcus gripped the console edge, breathing through the sudden vertigo.

"What is it?" Nicole was at his side now, hand on his shoulder.

"I built this." His voice came out strange, distant. "This encryption. I designed it myself."

"Before the memory blocks?"

"Must have been." He laughed, the sound bitter and hollow. "I built walls specifically to keep myself out. And I was good at it—better than I remembered being at anything."

"Can you still break through?"

Marcus stared at the screen. His own work, created by a version of himself he couldn't access, protecting secrets he couldn't remember having. The irony was exquisite and terrible.

"Maybe," he said slowly. "If I assume I was the enemy when I designed it. If I think about what I would have anticipated from someone trying to break in with exactly my skillset."

"You designed it to stop yourself."

"Which means I also knew what would eventually get through." His fingers hovered over the console. "I just have to think like someone who was planning for this moment. Someone who wanted to hide something completely—but maybe, someday, wanted to find it again."

Nicole's hand was still on his shoulder. Grounding. Real. "What if there's a reason you buried it this deep?"

"There probably is. A good one." He met her eyes. "I'm going to find it anyway."

She held his gaze for a long moment. Whatever she saw there made her nod once and step back.

Marcus turned to the console and began the slow work of outthinking his own ghost.

The encryption yielded in layers, each one surrendering secrets the last had protected. Research logs first—technical data on neural mapping, consciousness transfer protocols, experimental results that turned his stomach even without full context. Then personnel files, deeper records than the ones he'd found before, documenting a team of researchers who'd worked on something called—

ARCHON.

The word hit him like a physical blow.

His vision fractured. Laboratory white. Isolation suits. Screens displaying neural architecture in colors that shouldn't exist. His own voice, younger, certain, saying something about threshold states and consciousness persistence—

The pain came then, hot and sharp, lancing through his skull from the neural interface into whatever broken places his memory blocks had left behind. Marcus gasped, tried to disconnect, but the data was still flooding in and his brain couldn't tell the difference between past and present anymore.

Bodies in stasis tubes. Eyes open and moving. Fire consuming a building. Someone screaming—him? someone else?—and then—

The floor tilted. No. He was falling.

Hands caught him before he hit the deck. Nicole, moving faster than he would have expected, her arms around his shoulders as his legs gave out. Her face swam into focus, close to his, sharp with fear and something harder underneath.

"Marcus. Focus on me." Not a request. An order. "You're on the station. Present tense. What's your full name?"

"Marcus—" The pain was receding now, leaving exhaustion in its wake. "Marcus Vane."

"What ship did we take to get here?"

"Widow's Edge." He could think again. Barely. "I'm fine. I'm—"

"You're not fine. You just collapsed." But she was already helping him sit up, positioning him against the console with efficient movements. "Torres, I need you in the control room."

The comm crackled. "On my way."

Marcus pressed his hands against his eyes, trying to push back the lingering fragments of memory that swam at the edges of his consciousness. ARCHON. The word carried weight he couldn't quite lift, implications he couldn't quite grasp.

"What did you find?" Nicole asked. Her voice had steadied, shifted into the analytical mode that meant she was processing a problem. Good. He needed her thinking clearly, because his own thoughts felt like shattered glass.

"A project. ARCHON." He swallowed hard. "Neural interface technology. Consciousness transfer. And I was—" Another flash of memory, briefer this time: himself at a podium, presenting to uniformed officials, charts behind him displaying success rates that should have been impossible. "I was involved. Deeply. Before."

"Before the memory blocks."

"They weren't just blocks." The realization crystallized slowly, painfully. "They were protection. I didn't just forget this project—I specifically, surgically removed my own access to it. With help. Because whatever ARCHON was, knowing about it was dangerous."

Torres appeared in the doorway, assessed the situation in one sharp glance. "He going to live?"

"Probably," Nicole said. "The neural interface triggered some kind of memory cascade. Buried trauma."

"Great." Torres moved into the room, positioned herself where she could watch both Marcus and the corridor behind her. "So what's worth getting your brain scrambled over?"

Marcus looked at the console, at the files that had nearly broken him. The encryption was gone now—his collapse had somehow completed the breach, as if his subconscious had finished the work his conscious mind couldn't handle.

"Immortal soldiers," he said. The words sounded insane even to him. "ARCHON was trying to create consciousness transfer technology for military applications. A way to—" He stopped, sorted through the fragments, tried to find language that would make the impossible sound real. "A way to move a soldier's consciousness into a new body if the original died. Perfect continuity. No training loss. No experience loss. Just…transfer and redeploy."

Chapter 19

Convergence

The proximity alarm screamed through the station's corridors like a wounded animal.

Nicole's hand found her sidearm before her brain caught up to the sound. Already moving—muscle memory seizing control—her boots hammering the grated floor while red emergency lighting strobed across the walls. The Obsidian Pulse armor plates responded instantly, unfurling across her body like a second skin. Nanoscale plates interlocked. Integrated weapon systems hummed online. The HUD overlay painted tactical data across her field of vision: heart rate, ammunition count, shield capacity—all rendered in clean blue text at the edges of her sight.

"Contact!" Kira's voice crackled over comms, pitched high with urgency. "Multiple signatures decloaking at bearing zero-seven-two. They came out of nowhere—stealth approach from the debris field."

Nicole rounded the corner into the station's central hub. Marcus still sat connected to the command console, eyes unfocused with that distant look that meant he was swimming through data streams. Sera hovered beside him, fingers flying across a secondary terminal.

"How many?" Nicole demanded.

"Three ships." Sera's face had gone pale beneath the emergency lights. "Corvette-class. Obsidian Solutions markings confirmed."

"They found us." Not a question. Nicole felt the cold certainty settle into her chest like a blade of ice. "Marcus, disconnect. Now."

Marcus's eyes snapped back to focus, and she saw the fear there—real, human fear that cut through whatever fugue state the neural link had induced. "The download—it's not complete. Another three minutes—"

"We don't have three minutes. Grab what you've got."

The station shuddered, a deep groan of metal that Nicole felt in her teeth. Somewhere in the distance, the distinctive crack of a docking clamp being overridden. They were already boarding.

"Everyone to the ship," Nicole ordered, activating her comms. "Jace, what's your position?"

"Cargo bay, securing the last of the equipment." His voice was steady, but she heard the tension beneath it. "I heard the alert. We've got company."

"Obsidian Solutions. They're breaching now." Nicole was already moving toward the corridor that led to the docking bay where Widow's Edge waited. "Get to secondary position alpha. Hold the junction between cargo and engineering—that's our fallback route."

"Copy that."

"Everyone else, converge on the main docking corridor. We punch through to the ship and we don't stop."

She was ready.



The first mercenary came around the corner fast and low, carbine already tracking. Nicole's armor reacted before she consciously registered the target—her arm snapped up, the integrated pulse projector cycling to full power, and she fired.

The shot caught him center mass, punching through his tactical vest like paper. He went down without a sound, dead before he hit the deck.

Two more behind him. Nicole was moving, the Pulse armor amplifying her speed, her reflexes, transforming her into something more than human. She felt the rounds ping against her chest plate—kinetic dampeners absorbing the impacts, redistributing the force—and she closed the distance in three heartbeats.

The first one tried to bring his weapon up. She caught the barrel with her left hand, twisted, and drove her right fist into his throat. The armor-enhanced strike crushed his larynx. As he crumpled, she ripped the carbine from his grip and swung it like a club against the third, feeling bone shatter beneath the impact.

"Contact down," she reported, barely winded. "Three neutralized. Corridor seven-alpha clear."

"More coming." Kira's voice was strained over the comms. "I'm reading heat signatures converging on your position—at least a dozen."

Nicole dropped the carbine and checked her pulse projector's charge. Seventy-three percent. Enough.

"Everyone sound off."

"Marcus here. Sera and I are at junction twelve." A pause. "Nicole, whatever I pulled from that console—you need to see it. It changes everything."

"Later. Get to the ship."

"Cross here." Daven's voice was rough, the older man's breathing labored. "I'm pinned down near the medical bay. They've got the corridor blocked."

Damn. Nicole pulled up her mental map of the station's layout. The medical bay sat on the opposite side of the main junction—getting there would mean cutting through the heart of the Obsidian assault.

"Hold tight. I'm coming to you."

"Negative." Cross coughed, and Nicole heard weapons fire in the background. "Don't double back. Get the others to the ship. I'll find another route."

"Cross—"

"That's not a request, Bastion. You know I'm right. Move."

She did know. And she hated that she did.

"Copy. Stay alive."



The corridor erupted into chaos as Nicole rounded the next junction. Three Obsidian operatives were advancing on Marcus and Sera's position, suppressive fire pinning them behind a collapsed bulkhead. Nicole's armor assessed the threat pattern in microseconds—two riflemen providing cover, one trying to flank through a maintenance access.

She dealt with the flanker first. A single pulse shot through the access hatch, and he tumbled out in a spray of blood. The other two reacted fast—professionals, then—but not fast enough. Nicole was already among them, a whirlwind of armored violence.

The Obsidian Pulse sang against her skin, feeding her data, enhancing her movements. She felt the rifle butt coming before she saw it, ducked under the swing, and drove her elbow into the operative's visor. Plasteel cracked. He screamed.

The second one managed to get his sidearm up. Nicole caught his wrist, twisted until she heard the snap, and hurled him into the wall hard enough to dent the metal. He slid down and didn't move.

"Clear," she called out. "Move!"

Marcus and Sera emerged from cover, both pale, both clutching weapons Nicole suspected neither really knew how to use. It didn't matter. They just needed to reach the ship.

"Jace—status?"

"Holding position." Jace's voice was tight with controlled aggression. "They keep probing, but we're keeping them back. How far out are you?"

"Two minutes. Keep that corridor open."

They ran. Nicole took point, the Pulse armor's sensors painting the path ahead in shades of threat assessment. Green corridors, yellow junctions, red—

Red.

She threw herself to the side just as the heavy pulse cannon opened up, dragging Marcus down with her. The shot passed close enough for her armor to register the thermal bloom, close enough to sear the air in her lungs.

"Emplacement!" she shouted. "Heavy weapons!"

The Obsidian team had set up a defensive position at the main junction—four operators behind portable barricades, one manning a crew-served weapon that was chewing through the corridor's infrastructure. Sparks flew. Metal screamed.

Nicole's mind raced through tactical options. A frontal assault was suicide, even with the armor. The heavy weapon would punch through her shields before she closed half the distance.

"Kira, I need options."

"Working on it." Keys clattered. "There's a maintenance shaft thirty meters back—it comes out behind their position, but it's tight. You'd have to ditch the armor's outer plates."

Not happening. The Pulse armor was what kept her alive. But—

"Can Marcus fit through?"

A pause. "Yes. Barely."

Nicole grabbed Marcus by the collar, pulling him close so he could hear her over the weapons fire. "Maintenance shaft, thirty meters back. Get behind them. Create a distraction. Anything—throw something, make noise. I just need three seconds."

Marcus's eyes widened. "Nicole, I can't—"

"You can." She held his gaze, willing him to believe it. "You got us the data. Now get us out of here. Three seconds, Marcus. That's all I need."

He swallowed hard. Then he nodded.



The seconds stretched into an eternity as Nicole waited, pressed against the corridor wall, listening to the heavy weapon's rhythmic bark. Sera crouched beside her, clutching her sidearm with white-knuckled hands.

"Is he going to be okay?" Sera whispered.

Nicole didn't answer. She didn't know.

Then—a crash. A shout. The heavy weapon's fire stuttered, swung toward a new target.

Nicole moved.

The Pulse armor unleashed everything it had. The integrated projector fired in rapid succession as she charged, dropping two operatives before they could react. The gunner tried to swing the heavy weapon back toward her, but he was too slow, far too slow. She vaulted the barricade and was on him, her armored fist caving in the side of his skull.

The fourth operative got a shot off—she felt the impact against her side, felt the armor strain to absorb it—but then she had him by the throat, lifting him off the ground.

"How did you find us?"

He gurgled something incomprehensible. She squeezed tighter.

"How?"

"T-tracker," he managed. "On the ship. Always… always knew…"

She threw him into the wall and didn't watch him fall.

"Marcus! Move!"

He emerged from the maintenance access, covered in dust and grime, eyes wild. But alive. Alive and moving.

They ran.



The junction ahead flickered with weapons fire—Jace's position. Nicole could see him now, holding the corridor with grim determination, a dead Obsidian operative slumped against the barricade beside him.

"Coming through!" Nicole shouted.

"Copy! We'll cover your exit!" Jace's voice was hoarse. "More hostiles inbound from starboard—they're trying to flank!"

Nicole skidded to a halt beside him, assessing the tactical situation. Widow's Edge was fifty meters ahead, her docking port still intact. But between here and there, at least eight more Obsidian operatives were advancing from multiple angles.

"Sera, Marcus—go."

The explosion came from nowhere. One moment the corridor was intact, the next it was a hellscape of twisted metal and screaming alarms. Nicole felt herself thrown backward, the armor absorbing most of the blast but still leaving her ears ringing.

Breaching charges. They'd blown through the hull itself.

She scrambled to her feet, searching through the smoke and debris. "Report! Everyone report!"

"I'm here." Jace, coughing. "Sera's with me."

"Marcus?"

"Alive. I think."

Fresh weapons fire erupted from the far end of the corridor. More Obsidian operatives, flooding in through the breach. Nicole made her choice—the only choice she could make—and ran.



The sprint to Widow's Edge was a blur of chaos and adrenaline. Jace was ahead of her, dragging Sera bodily toward the docking port. Marcus stumbled along behind, somehow keeping pace despite the terror written across his face. Nicole brought up the rear, the Pulse armor's projector spitting death at anyone who tried to follow.

"Cross, what's your status?" Static. She tried again. "Cross, respond."
