He looked smaller in death. Marcus had noticed that before, the way absence diminished physical form. Torres had been solid, capable—the kind of presence that anchored a room just by occupying it. Now he was just a shape beneath white cloth.

Nicole entered.

She walked to the head of the pallet without hesitation, positioning herself where she could see both Torres's face and the assembled crew. Her jaw was set. Her eyes were dry. But Marcus could see the tremor in her fingers as she rested her hand on the edge of the fabric.

The silence stretched.

"I don't know the words," Daven finally said, his voice rough as unsanded metal. "Thirty years serving together, and I never thought to ask what he'd want said at a time like this."

"There aren't words for this." Jace's voice came from the far side of the bay. "Not proper ones. Just what we remember."

Daven stepped closer to the pallet. His hand came to rest opposite Nicole's, both of them bracketing the body of the man they'd lost.

"Thirty years," Daven repeated. "We met during the Frontier Conflicts. Different ships, same bad situation. Our crews got pinned down on a station that was falling apart around us—loss of pressure, fires everywhere, enemy forces coming in from three directions." He paused, working his jaw. "Torres was the one who figured out the escape route. Not because he was the smartest or the highest rank. Just because he was the one paying attention while the rest of us were busy panicking."

A rough sound escaped him, something that might have been a laugh under different circumstances. "That was Torres. Always paying attention. Always seeing the angle the rest of us missed."

Daven's hand pressed flat against the pallet, knuckles whitening. "We argued about everything. Politics. Routes. The right way to maintain engine seals. He never backed down from a fight, not even when he was wrong—which was more often than he'd admit." The gruffness in his voice softened slightly. "But when things went sideways and there was no time for planning, he was the man you wanted at your back. Always. Every single time."

He looked at Torres's still face, and something in his expression cracked open. "I'm going to miss those arguments. I'm going to miss having someone who'd tell me I was being an idiot and actually mean it."

Daven stepped back, the motion abrupt, his hand coming up to cover his eyes for a brief moment before dropping back to his side. The grief was raw on his face, unmasked.

Jace moved next, crossing the bay to stand where Daven had been. His body language shifted as he found his footing—less rigid, more settled.

"He saved my life twice," Jace said without preamble. "First time was three years back, cargo run near the Threshold. I'd gotten cocky, taken a risk I shouldn't have. Torres saw the ambush before I did. Moved like he already knew where the shots would come from."

Jace's eyes went distant, looking at something beyond the cargo bay walls. "Second time was different. I was about to make a deal with the wrong people—didn't know it, didn't see the signs. Torres pulled me aside. Explained in very specific terms what would happen if I walked into that meeting." A faint smile crossed his face. "He had this way of explaining things. Made you feel like an idiot for not seeing it yourself, but not in a mean way. Just… clear. Practical."

The smile faded. "Third time was four days ago. He didn't save my life that time—he saved hers." Jace's eyes moved to Nicole. "And I'm not going to stand here and say it should have been me instead, because that's not what he'd want to hear. What he'd want is for us to take what he gave us and make it count."

Jace stepped back. Sera pushed off from the wall, uncrossing her arms as she moved toward the pallet. Her movements were slower than the others, more reluctant, but she didn't stop until she stood beside Torres's body.

"Six months." Her voice was quieter than Marcus had ever heard it. "Less than that, really. I spent most of that time keeping my distance, not trusting anyone on this crew further than I could throw them."

She looked down at Torres's face. "He was patient with that. Didn't push. Didn't take offense when I shut him out. Just kept doing his job, keeping all of us alive, letting me figure out on my own that this crew wasn't like the others."

Sera's hand moved toward the shroud, hesitated, withdrew. "Three weeks ago, he found me in the med bay after that close call on the station. Didn't say anything—just sat down, handed me a flask, and waited. When I was finally ready to talk, he just listened. Didn't try to fix anything. Didn't offer advice I hadn't asked for."

Her voice hardened slightly, taking on an edge that Marcus recognized as her defense against vulnerability. "He knew what people needed. Most people don't bother to figure that out. He did."

She stepped back. The bay fell quiet again.

Kira left her position at the control panel, moving toward the pallet with the careful precision that characterized everything she did. She adjusted her glasses—nervous habit—before speaking.

"He asked me to teach him about the nav systems." Her voice was steady, technical, but with something underneath that threatened to crack through. "Not because he needed to—he'd been running ships since before I was born. He wanted to understand what I did. Wanted to be able to help if something went wrong while I was off-shift."

She paused, pressing her glasses up again even though they hadn't slipped. "He spent three evenings learning things he'd probably never need to use. Three evenings of his rest cycle." Her voice caught. "That's who he was. He planned for contingencies. Made sure the crew could survive any absence."

Her hand pressed flat against her chest, over her heart. "Even his own."

Kira returned to the control panel. Marcus saw her fingers trembling as she resumed her unnecessary diagnostics.

The silence that followed felt different—heavier, weighted with expectation. Everyone's eyes moved toward Nicole.

She hadn't spoken. Hadn't moved from her position at the head of the pallet. Her hand still rested on the fabric near Torres's shoulder, fingers pressed white against the material.

Marcus watched her face. Saw the struggle there—the need to speak warring with something that looked like fear. Her breathing had gone shallow, too fast.

"Nicole." Daven's voice was gentle. She opened her mouth. Closed it. Tried again. "He deserved better than dying for my mistakes." The words came out broken, barely audible. "They all did." No one argued. Some truths were too raw for comfort—and some grief had to stand alone.

Chapter 27

New Heading

The blood had dried to rust on her armor plates.

Nicole sat on the edge of her bunk, staring at the discolored streaks across her chest piece. Torres's blood. The plasma bolt had caught him center mass, but somehow—impossibly—he'd still managed to collapse toward her rather than away, as if his last conscious act had been to shield her even in death.

She couldn't remember stripping off the armor after the firefight. Couldn't remember much of the hours between Torres going slack in her arms and Marcus finally prying her away from the body. Someone had undressed her down to her compression suit, arranged the armor pieces on the small table by the viewport. Someone had drawn the privacy curtain closed.

The ship hummed around her. Sera had jumped them to a dead system—some burned-out star with nothing but cosmic debris for company. Safe, she'd called it. Temporary sanctuary.

There was no sanctuary. Not anymore.

Her hands trembled. She noticed that distantly, as if observing someone else's body. The armor sat on the table. Torres's blood sat on the armor. And she sat here, useless, while the rust darkened and set.

Torres had never been still. Even in quiet moments, he'd been doing something—checking weapons, running diagnostics, cracking terrible jokes that nobody laughed at except him. He would have hated seeing her like this. Frozen. Waiting for permission to feel something other than loss.

The thought struck like a blade. Nicole forced herself to stand.

She lifted the chest piece. Standard colonial militia grade, upgraded with salvaged Directorate components. Torres had helped her install the secondary heat sinks himself, complaining the whole time about her cheap taste in protective equipment.

"You want armor that works or armor that looks pretty?" she'd asked him.

"Both," he'd said, grinning. "Why compromise?"

The memory crested and broke over her. She carried the armor to the basin anyway.



The water ran pink, then red, then darker still as she scrubbed at the chest piece. Torres's blood swirled down the drain in diluted streams, disappearing into the ship's recycling systems. In a few hours, those same molecules would be filtered, purified, converted back into drinking water or humidity regulation.

The thought should have horrified her. Instead, it felt appropriate somehow. Torres had given everything to keep the Meridian running. Why should his death be any different?

Nicole scrubbed harder. This was ritual. This was the only funeral she could give him right now—the careful, thorough removal of his sacrifice from the equipment that had failed to save him. By the time she finished with the last piece, her hands were raw and her reflection in the polished armor belonged to a stranger. Hollow-eyed. Pale.

Purpose. The word surfaced in her mind like a blade breaking water.

She'd spent weeks running. Torres had died defending that strategy. Defending her.

He deserved better than her fear.



The common area was empty when Nicole arrived, but it wouldn't stay that way for long. She could hear movement in the corridors—Jace's heavy tread from the engine room, the lighter rhythm of Sera's footsteps from the cockpit, the deliberate silence that meant Kira was already nearby, waiting.

She didn't call a meeting. Didn't need to.

They gathered in pieces, drawn by some shared awareness that the moment had come to decide what Torres's death would mean. Marcus emerged from the cargo bay with frost still clinging to his jacket—he'd been sitting vigil over a corpse they couldn't bring themselves to simply jettison. Jace followed minutes later, grease-stained and hollow-eyed, dropping into a seat without acknowledging anyone. Sera came last from the cockpit, datapad clutched to her chest, tear-tracks still visible on her cheeks.

Kira was already there. Nicole caught her watching from the shadows near the storage lockers, and for once, the former Directorate operative didn't linger in the margins. She moved to the table, stood at its edge with her hands flat on the surface.

It was such a small thing—stepping forward instead of hanging back—but Nicole noticed. Kira had been Directorate once. Had worn their uniform, carried their weapons, executed their orders. Torres had died under Directorate guns, and whatever compartmentalized peace Kira had made with her past, this had to be cutting close to bone.

But she was here. Present. Waiting with the rest of them.

Nicole caught her eye. Held it. Nodded once.

Kira's expression didn't change, but something in her posture shifted—shoulders settling, chin lifting slightly. Acknowledgment. Understanding.

They gathered without speaking. Without being asked. Like cells responding to damage, like a body trying to heal itself after trauma.

Nicole found her place at the head of the table. She hadn't planned to speak, but the silence stretched until it demanded filling.

"We can't keep doing this," she said.

No one disagreed.



"Running isn't working." Marcus said what they'd all been thinking. He stood across from Nicole, hands flat on the table surface. "We've jumped seventeen times in the past three weeks. Changed routes, used dead drops, varied our patterns. They still found us."

"I found a tracking signature in the hull plating," Sera said. "An hour ago. Passive emitter, nearly undetectable—they tagged us at Horizon Station." She exhaled slowly. "Pulled it the second I confirmed it wasn't wired to anything nasty. Couldn't risk it broadcasting our position while I ran the rest of the analysis. But there's something else."

She set her datapad on the table, summoned a holographic display. Points of light materialized—their flight path over the past month, rendered in sickly green.

"While we were running dark after the attack, I started pulling apart our navigation logs. Cross-referencing our routes against Directorate intercept patterns." Her voice steadied as she spoke, grief channeling into analysis. "The tag was backup. Secondary tracking. Look at the timestamps—they were in position before we arrived at three of the last five locations. Hours before."

"Someone's predicting our movements," Nicole said.

"Someone who knows how we think." Sera highlighted the discrepancies, the impossible precision of Directorate positioning. "I've been working this since Torres—" She stopped, swallowed. "Since the cargo bay. Couldn't sleep anyway."

"The Handler," Nicole said.

The name hung in the air like a curse. They'd encountered his work before—carefully laid traps, assets positioned precisely where they'd be most devastating. He was Directorate Intelligence's premiere tactical coordinator, responsible for operations throughout the colonial sectors. He was also, as far as anyone could tell, a ghost.

"We run again, he finds us again," Marcus said. "We hide, he flushes us out. We fight defensively, we lose people." He paused, jaw tight. "We've lost people."

"So what do you suggest?" Kira's voice was flat, but her knuckles had gone white against the table's edge. "Surrender isn't an option. Neither is dying in some glorious last stand."

"No." The word came out harder than she probably intended. She caught herself, and something complicated moved across her face. "I've seen how this ends. The Directorate doesn't take prisoners from lists like ours. They take examples."

Marcus straightened. Something had shifted in his expression—the weight of command settling onto shoulders that had been trying to avoid it.

"We go on offense."

The words dropped like stones into water. Nicole felt the ripples spreading through the crew.

"Offense how?" Jace asked. "We're one ship against the entire Directorate Intelligence apparatus. Torres was our best combat specialist, and he—" The engineer's voice cracked. He swallowed hard, forced himself to continue. "We're not exactly in fighting shape."

"We don't fight the entire apparatus," Marcus said. "We fight the Handler. Find him. Gather intelligence on Directorate operations. Learn their network, their resources, their weaknesses. Stop being the prey."

"And how exactly do we find someone who doesn't officially exist?" Kira asked. The question wasn't dismissive—if anything, she sounded genuinely curious. Like she was testing the idea for structural integrity. Like she'd asked similar questions in briefing rooms that flew different colors.

"He got sloppy." All eyes turned to Sera. The pilot's expression had transformed—grief still present, but overlaid now with something fierce. "The attack that killed Torres. I've been analyzing the coordination patterns. Timing of the assault, positioning of their teams, response protocols. It's good work, but it's rushed. He's been tracking us for so long, he got comfortable. Started leaving fingerprints."

She expanded the holographic display, overlaying new data streams. Communication intercepts. Power signatures. Ship registry fragments.

"I traced his command signal back through three relay stations before it disappeared. But I have a vector. A direction." A cone of probability materialized in the display, narrowing toward the outer systems. "Somewhere in this region. If I can get another few hours with the intercept data, I can narrow it further."

"Could be misdirection," Kira said. "He has to know we'd try to track him."

"Could be," Sera agreed. "But the signal architecture was sloppy. Like he was confident we'd never get close enough to detect it." Her voice hardened. "He was wrong."

Nicole studied the display. The probability cone pulsed steadily, a direction in the dark. Proof that their enemy was human, finite, findable.

"If we do this," she said slowly, "there's no going back. Right now, we're refugees with stolen information. We attack the Handler directly, we become active combatants. The Directorate will escalate their response. More ships. More assets. More…" She couldn't finish the sentence. More deaths.

"They're already escalating," Marcus said. "Torres proved that. They sent a full tactical team to eliminate us—not capture, eliminate. Whatever line we were afraid of crossing, we crossed it the moment we downloaded that data." He met her eyes. "The only question left is whether we keep running until they catch us, or we turn around and fight."



The vote wasn't formal. It didn't need to be.

Jace spoke first, his voice rough but steady. "I spent fifteen years building things in shipyards across the sector. Assembly lines, production quotas, components I never saw used. Then I joined this crew, and for the first time, I felt like what I built actually mattered." He dragged a hand across his face. "Torres died protecting that. I'll be damned if I let his death mean nothing. I vote we fight."

Sera simply nodded, her jaw set. "Find the bastard who coordinated the attack. Make him answer for it. I'm in."

Kira was silent for a long moment. Her hands had relaxed against the table, but her expression remained guarded—the professional mask she wore like a second skin. When she finally spoke, her voice was carefully controlled.

"The Directorate thinks it owns the sector. Owns the information, owns the truth, owns every person who's ever been touched by its operations." She paused, and something flickered behind her eyes. "I used to be one of their tools. I know how they think, how they plan, how they hunt. I know what they did to people who stepped out of line, and I followed orders anyway." Her gaze dropped to the table's surface. "Torres never asked me about that. Never pushed. Just… accepted that I was here now, not there."

The silence stretched. Nicole watched Kira struggle with something she probably hadn't intended to say out loud.

"I vote we fight," Kira said finally. "Not because I think we'll win. Because some things need to be fought whether you win or not. And because I owe Torres—" She stopped, shook her head. "I owe him better than running."

Three votes. All that remained was Nicole and Marcus.

Marcus hadn't looked away from her since the discussion began. She felt the weight of his gaze, patient and undemanding, waiting for her to choose. Nicole straightened. "We fight," she said. "And we don't stop until the Directorate understands exactly what they bought when they made us enemies." Marcus nodded once. The vote was unanimous.

Chapter 28

The Hunt Begins

The screens threw cold light across Sera's face, painting her features in shades of data. Her fingers danced across the haptic interface with the practiced grace of someone who'd spent more time talking to machines than people, teasing threads from the vast digital tapestry that connected every station, every ship, every transaction in the sector.

Nicole watched from the doorway of the cramped intelligence suite, arms crossed, waiting. The room reeked of recycled air and burnt circuitry—Sera had been running her rigs hard for the past eighteen hours, driving the Obsidian Pulse's auxiliary systems to their limits.

"Handler's good," Sera murmured, not looking up. "Really good. Dead man's identity chain, signatures bouncing through seventeen different relay points. But nobody's perfect."

Nicole stepped deeper into the room, studying the incomprehensible flow of information. "What's his weakness?"

"Medicine." Sera pulled up a highlighted thread, isolating it from the chaos. "Neural tremor of some kind. Needs regular suppressant injections, specific compound. Can't fake that, can't substitute it. And there are only four stations in this sector with the facilities to manufacture it."

The data coalesced, patterns emerging from noise. Nicole watched as Sera's algorithms stripped away layers of false identities and ghost transactions, following the trail of pharmaceutical purchases like a bloodhound tracking wounded prey.

"Got something." Sera's voice sharpened. "Purchase order, three days ago. Fake name, but the compound matches exactly. And—" She pulled up another window, overlaying shipping manifests with station logs. "Cross-referencing with docking records…"

The screen flickered, and a station schematic materialized—a sprawling, asymmetrical structure that looked more like a tumor than engineered habitat.

"Rust Station, Deck 14." Sera leaned back, triumph and exhaustion warring on her face. "That's where our Handler went to ground."



The tactical briefing room was crowded. Marcus had the display running when Nicole arrived, but he'd also called in the full team—Jace leaning against the far bulkhead with his arms crossed, Kira in the pilot's chair she'd dragged in from the bridge, Daven Cross occupying the corner seat with the watchful stillness of a man who'd survived more operations than anyone else in the room.

"Rust Station," Marcus said, expanding the schematic. "Of course it had to be Rust Station."

The station was a nightmare of improvised construction—original Consortium mining outpost at its core, but decades of unlicensed expansion had turned it into a labyrinth. Modules cannibalized from dead ships jutted at odd angles. Entire sections had been sealed off due to structural failures, only to be secretly reopened by squatters and black marketeers.

"Pirate haven," Nicole said. "No central authority, no station security worth mentioning. Perfect place to disappear."

"Perfect place to get killed." Marcus's jaw tightened. "The cartels run most of Deck 14. Koven's people control the narcotics trade. The Syndicate handles weapons. And everyone—everyone—shoots first when they see uniforms or anything that smells like authority."

"So we don't smell like authority."

"I've worked stations like this," Jace said from the bulkhead. "Twice on the Meridian Drift, once on Kolvan's Rock. You can't move a full team through there without someone noticing. Too many eyes, too many people looking to sell information."

Cross spoke for the first time, his voice carrying the weight of decades of experience. "He's right. Rust Station runs on paranoia. A coordinated insertion would trigger every alarm in the sector before you cleared the docking ring."

Marcus pulled up tactical overlays, highlighting choke points and known hostile territories. "If we're doing this, we need multiple insertion points. Extraction routes planned for every contingency."

"Handler knows we're coming," Nicole said. "After the Terminus raid, after we burned his operation and killed his protection—he has to know. He'll be watching for anything that looks like an assault team."

Kira shook her head. "Five people moving through that maze? Even staggered, we'd leave a pattern. Someone would connect the dots."

The room fell silent, everyone arriving at the same conclusion but waiting for someone else to voice it.

Nicole voiced it. "One operator. In and out before anyone knows something's wrong."

"No." Marcus's response was immediate.

"Think about it tactically. A solo insertion is the last thing Handler expects—and the only approach that gives us a real chance of getting close before he runs."

"You're talking about walking into a war zone alone." Marcus's voice was controlled, but Nicole could see what it cost him. "Three different cartel territories intersect within a hundred meters of the target location."

"I've walked through worse."

"With backup. With support." He faced her fully, and now she could see the fear beneath the professional mask—not for the mission, but for her. "Torres died on the last operation. If something goes wrong in there and you're alone—"

"Then I handle it." Nicole met his gaze steadily. "Or I don't, and the mission fails. That's always been the calculation, Marcus. For any of us."

Cross leaned forward, fingers steepled. "She's not wrong about the tactical picture. A solo operative has the best chance of reaching Handler undetected. The question is whether the risk to that operative is acceptable."

"It's my risk to take," Nicole said.

"It affects all of us." Marcus's voice was quiet now, stripped of its professional distance. "You know it does."

The silence that followed was heavy with everything they weren't saying—couldn't say, not in front of the team. Nicole could see him cycling through arguments, discarding each one as he hit the same wall: the mission parameters didn't favor any other approach.

Jace pushed off from the bulkhead. "If she's going in alone, she needs the best possible intelligence. Sera and I can work up a detailed map of cartel movements, patrol patterns, anything that might give her an edge."

"And I'll position us for rapid extraction," Kira added. "Close enough to reach any point on that station in under six minutes."

Cross nodded slowly. "Contingency planning. If this goes wrong, we need options that don't involve storming a hostile station without authorization."

Marcus stood rigid for a long moment, staring at the tactical display. When he finally spoke, his voice was rough. "I've lost people on operations like this. Good people, following solid plans that fell apart the moment reality intervened." He turned to Nicole. "I can't order you not to go. But I need you to understand what you're asking me to accept."

"I understand." Nicole stepped toward him, close enough that the others would have to strain to hear. "I'm asking you to trust me. Trust that I can do this."

His eyes searched hers, looking for something—certainty, perhaps, or a promise she couldn't make. Whatever he found seemed to be enough. "All right." He straightened, the commander reasserting itself over the man. "But we do this smart. Cross, work with Sera on intelligence. Jace, I want backup plans for the backup plans. Kira, plot extraction routes for every scenario you can imagine and a few you can't."

He turned back to the display, pulling up the target area. "Communication protocols. We can't risk regular check-ins—on a station like Rust, frequent transmissions get noticed. Handler might have people monitoring for exactly that kind of pattern."

"Dead drops," Cross suggested. "Predetermined signals at irregular intervals. Harder to detect, harder to trace."

Nicole nodded. "Three signal windows. If I miss two consecutive windows, you move to extraction protocols."

"One." Marcus's voice was firm. "You miss one window, we start positioning. You miss two, we're coming in."

"That's a narrow margin."

"That's the margin I can live with." His eyes met hers, and she understood this was where his compromise ended. "Take it or we find another way."

Nicole considered pushing back, but recognized the line she'd hit. "Agreed."

"Then let's plan this properly." Marcus turned to the room. "Everyone knows their role. We have eighteen hours before optimal insertion window. I want everything locked down by sixteen."



The Obsidian Pulse's armory was smaller than what Nicole had worked with on military vessels, but Sera had equipped it well. Weapons lined the bulkheads—projectile and energy, lethal and less-lethal, each one maintained to exacting standards.

Nicole stripped off her standard gear and began the methodical process of reconfiguring her loadout for infiltration. The heavy assault armor went back in the locker. In its place, she pulled out a modular tactical rig—lighter, more flexible, with active camouflage threading woven through the fabric.

"Going for subtle?" Sera's voice came from the doorway.

"Going for invisible." Nicole checked the rig's power cells, verified the camo system was fully charged. "Can't shoot my way through a station that size. Need to be a ghost."

Sera entered the armory, pulling up a portable display. "The section where Handler's holed up—it used to be crew quarters for the original mining operation. Now it's mostly converted to what the listings call 'independent hospitality services.'"

"Brothels and drug dens."

"Among other things. But the good news is, lots of traffic. New faces all the time. You won't stand out as long as you look like you belong."

Nicole selected a sidearm—compact, reliable, with a suppressed barrel that extended the weapon's length but eliminated virtually all muzzle signature. She checked the action, loaded a magazine, and holstered it beneath her left arm.

"Handler's using an alias," Sera continued. "Victor Shen. He's rented a cube on the third sublevel—high-security door, which is unusual for that area. He's being careful."

"How current is your intel?"

"The pharmaceutical purchase was three days ago. He hasn't moved since—at least, not according to station sensor logs." Sera paused. "But Rust Station's systems are a nightmare. Half the sensors are broken, the other half are on someone's payroll. I can't guarantee anything."

"Understood." Nicole added a knife to her kit—ceramic blade, invisible to standard scanners. Then a coil of monofilament wire, thin enough to be nearly undetectable but strong enough to cut through bone.

Sera watched her gear up with an expression Nicole couldn't quite read. "You know, when I signed on for this, I thought I'd be the one taking the big risks. Running code, cracking systems, staying one step ahead of corporate security."

"You are taking risks."

"Yeah, but not like this." Sera gestured at the weapons, the infiltration gear. "Not walking into a pirate station alone to extract a man who sells information to people who want us dead."

Nicole finished adjusting her rig, rolling her shoulders to test the fit. "Everyone has their role."

"That's what I'm saying. I'm good at my role. Really good. But watching you get ready for something like this…" Sera shook her head. "I don't know how you do it."

"Practice." Nicole met her eyes. "Same as anything. First time I went into hostile territory, I was terrified. Second time, slightly less terrified. Eventually you learn to use the fear instead of fighting it."

"And now?"

Nicole considered the question honestly. "Now I'm focused. Handler has information we need. People are dead because of what he sold, what he enabled. This ends when I bring him in."

"And if he won't come quietly?"

"Then I'll convince him." Nicole's voice was flat, professional. "One way or another."

Sera nodded slowly, then held up the portable display. "Jace and I finished the cartel movement analysis. Sending it to your implant now. It's not perfect, but it should help you avoid the worst of the patrol overlaps."

"Thanks." Nicole meant it. "For all of it."

Chapter 29

Rust and Blood

Nicole moved with the crowd flowing through Rust Station's main thoroughfare, the civilian jacket doing nothing to conceal the hard angles of ceramic armor beneath. She wasn't trying to fool anyone who looked closely—just trying to dissolve into the river of desperate humanity washing between docking berths and the warren of establishments that served their basest needs.

Three weeks since they'd scattered. Three weeks since Kira's hand had nearly been blown off at Meridian, since Marcus had looked at her with that flat expression that made her chest ache and said they couldn't keep operating as a unit. Not until they knew how the Directorate kept finding them.

So Nicole had gone hunting alone. And the hunt had led here.

Two men near a recyc intake, armed with improvised shock sticks—toll collectors. A woman in faded pilot's gear marking credits on a handheld, probably running a betting operation. Children, gaunt and hollow-eyed, watching the flow of bodies with the calculating attention of predators waiting for distracted prey.

A man seized her arm as she passed—massive, scarred across half his face, with the augmented musculature of a dock worker gone wrong. "New face. New faces pay transit."

Nicole didn't break stride. Her free hand rose, fingers finding the nerve cluster beneath his jaw with surgical precision. He gasped, grip loosening, and she was past him before he could recover.

"Not new enough," she said, not looking back.



The station's underbelly was worse.

Nicole descended through access corridors designed for maintenance drones, not human traffic. But humans had claimed them anyway, carving out spaces in the station's infrastructure like parasites burrowing into a host. She passed a fighting pit—two men bleeding into a ring while spectators screamed bets at runners who wove through the chaos with practiced efficiency.

Farther down, past a bulkhead that had been welded half-shut and then torn open again, she found the market. Not the sanctioned trading floor three decks up, but the real economy of Rust Station—the place where anything could be bought if you had the credits and the stomach.

Weapons sprawled across blankets, some still bearing Directorate service marks. Implants floated in jars of preservation fluid, origin unknown. Stimulants and depressants and compounds that existed in the spaces between known pharmacology.

And flesh. Always flesh, in places like this.

Nicole's steps slowed as she passed a woman negotiating the sale of her own daughter. The girl couldn't have been more than twelve, standing silent and still while her mother haggled over price. There was nothing in the girl's eyes—no fear, no hope, no anything at all.

Her hand twitched toward the weapon hidden beneath her jacket. Two shots, maybe three, and the transaction would be cancelled in the most permanent way possible.

But she wasn't here for rescue missions. She was here for information, and if she started a firefight in the market, she'd never reach her target.

Nicole forced herself to keep walking. As she passed, her shoulder brushed the buyer's coat. She felt the micro-tracker adhere to the fabric's inner lining with a soft magnetic click.

The girl's eyes tracked her movement. Nicole held that gaze for a single heartbeat, memorizing the face. Then she was past, and the market's noise swallowed whatever came next.

The Handler. Focus on the Handler. But the tracker's signal pulsed in her awareness like a second heartbeat—a promise she'd made to herself, if not to the girl.



The gambling den announced itself with music—live instruments, actual strings and brass, not synthesized approximations. Whoever ran this place had money and wanted everyone who entered to know it.

Nicole paused at the entrance, a doorway cut into a bulkhead decorated with actual paint in shades of crimson and gold. Two guards flanked the opening—professional, armed with pulse pistols that hummed faintly with charged capacitors. Not improvised muscle. Trained.

She rolled her shoulders, letting the tension drain into something looser. Softer. The civilian jacket helped—she'd chosen it for the way it draped, concealing armor while suggesting the careless posture of someone who'd never needed to move fast in her life.

"Private establishment." The larger guard's voice came flat, bored. "Invitation only."

Nicole reached into her jacket slowly, letting them see her hand. She produced a credit chip—anonymous, high-denomination, encoded with authentication signatures she'd lifted from a dead drop three days ago. Sera's last gift before going dark: a ghost of the Handler's own financial records, cloned credentials that his associates' systems would recognize.

The guard scanned it, watching numbers scroll across a small display. The chip's embedded codes triggered the secondary verification—an identity profile the establishment's systems already recognized as cleared.

"Welcome to the Crimson Table."

The interior was everything the station outside wasn't: clean, well-lit, temperature-controlled. Dark red velvet absorbed sound along the walls. Tables dotted the floor—card games, dice games, variations she didn't recognize—each attended by dealers in matching uniforms.

But this wasn't where the real money changed hands. The floor show was for tourists, wealthy dilettantes experiencing the thrill of the underworld without real risk. The serious business happened in private rooms, behind doors requiring more than credits to open.

Nicole scanned the crowd, cataloging faces against the intelligence files she'd memorized. There—near the bar, a man whose cybernetic eye implant marked him as one of the Handler's known associates. And there, by the door to the private rooms, another face from the files.

He was here. She could feel it.

She snagged a half-empty glass from a passing server's tray, brought it to her lips without drinking. The smell of synthetic whiskey gave her character—someone who'd been here a while, someone already several drinks deep. Her shoulders loosened further, her gaze going unfocused and wandering like a gambler distracted by the next table's action. Three weeks of tracking had taught her the Handler's world—the rhythms of it, the body language that belonged.

Five meters from the private room entrance, the man with the cybernetic eye noticed her.

His head tilted—not recognition, not yet. Just the automated threat assessment of someone whose implant was running pattern-matching software.

Nicole laughed at nothing, a sound that spilled out slightly too loud. She stumbled, caught herself on a passing server's arm with an apologetic smile that didn't quite focus on the right face. "Sorry, sorry—" She waved vaguely toward one of the card tables. "Thought I saw my luck changing over there."

The server steadied her, annoyed but unsuspecting, and Nicole let herself be guided a few steps in the wrong direction before drifting back on course.

The cybernetic eye tracked her a moment longer. Then dismissed her.

She reached the private room door, hand brushing the access panel. The cloned credentials opened it without resistance.



The Handler looked up from his card game.

For a long moment, neither moved. The other players—two women and a man, all with the hard edges of career criminals—froze, processing the sudden intrusion.

Then the Handler's face drained bone-white.

"Bastion," he whispered.

Nicole's pleasant expression didn't change. She'd already drawn, the motion so fast it registered only as aftermath—weapon up, sightlines clear.

"Out," she said to the others. "Now. Leave your weapons."

They complied. Pulse pistols and molecular blades clattered to the felt-covered table as they backed toward the door, hands raised.

She let them go. They weren't worth the ammunition.

The door sealed, and Nicole was alone with the Handler.

"Nicole," he said, voice finding some semblance of control. "I knew they'd send someone, but I didn't think—"

"Who says anyone sent me?"

The question landed like a slap. She watched him processing—the implications, the possibilities, the dawning understanding that this might not be official.

"The Directorate wants me dead," he said. "I can give you information. Names, codes, operational details. Let me bargain—"

"No."

Nicole crossed the room in three strides and drove her fist into his solar plexus. He folded, gasping, and she caught him before he could fall. Her hand found his throat, fingers pressing just hard enough to make breathing difficult.

"You don't get to bargain," she said. "You forfeited that when you sold my brother to the Directorate."

His eyes went wide. "Marcus—I didn't—I was following orders—"

She hit him again, targeting nerve clusters that would send sheets of agony through his body without permanent damage. He screamed, but the room was soundproofed.

"Let's try again." Nicole hauled him upright by his collar. "Who do you work for?"

"The Directorate! You know this!"

"Who in the Directorate?"

"I can't—they'll kill me—"

"I'll kill you. Right now. Slowly. And I've had training you can't imagine in making slow last a very long time."

She wasn't bluffing. The Directorate's advanced interrogation program had been one of the darker chapters of her service—weeks learning exactly how much pain a human body could endure before breaking.

She'd sworn never to use those techniques again.

The universe kept making her break her promises.

"Frost," the Handler gasped. "Director Frost. He runs the ARCHON program directly. No intermediaries, no oversight."

"What do they want with Marcus?"

"They needed a key. Someone with specific neural patterns—the right kind of compatibility with ancient systems." The Handler was babbling now, words tumbling out too fast. "Your brother tested off the charts. One in a billion. The perfect interface."

"Interface for what?"

"ARCHON. It's an activation system. The Directorate found something in the dead sectors—something old, something that's been waiting. But it needs human consciousness to wake it up. The right human consciousness."

Nicole's grip tightened. "Where is he?"

"I don't know! I was just a handler—one link in the chain. They kept it compartmentalized."

She hit him again, watching blood spray from his split lip. "Try harder."

"I swear! I don't know where they're holding him. But—" He coughed, more blood coming up. "But it doesn't matter. They don't need him anymore."

Nicole's hand froze mid-strike. "What?"

"They have another way." The Handler was crying now, tears cutting tracks through blood. "That's why they let me run. That's why no one came after me. Marcus was the primary key, but he wasn't the only option. They found someone else—some other way to activate ARCHON. Your brother is… expendable now."

The words hit Nicole like a blow to the chest. All this time, she'd believed keeping Marcus alive was in the Directorate's interest. That they needed him, and that need would protect him until she could mount a rescue.

If what the Handler was saying was true…

"Who?" she demanded. "Who else can they use?"

"I don't know! I told you, I was just—"

Nicole slammed him against the wall, watching his head bounce off the velvet. "Give me something useful, or I start removing pieces."

His eyes were wild with panic. "There's a facility! In the dead sectors—that's all I know. Frost has a facility where they're conducting final preparations. Sector Thirty-Seven, near the old Progenitor site. That's where it's happening!"

Sector Thirty-Seven. Nicole filed it away, cross-referencing with everything she knew about Directorate operations.

"Who else is involved? Names."

"Frost. Commander Thane—she's military liaison. Senator Krell on the oversight committee—he's been blocking investigations." He was vomiting information now, words spilling out uncontrolled. "There's a science team, but I never met them. Frost keeps them isolated. And the Remnant—"

"What about the Remnant?"

"They're connected somehow. I don't understand it" But Frost talks about them like they're leverage. Like he's got something on them, or they've got something he needs." His voice cracked. "That's everything. I swear." Nicole released him, letting him crumple against the wall. It wasn't everything—but it was enough to point her toward the next target.

Chapter 30

Revelations

The cargo ramp descended with a hydraulic hiss, and Marcus watched Nicole emerge from the station's perpetual twilight with a figure stumbling ahead of her.

She looked hollowed out. Even from across the cargo bay, Marcus could read the tension carved into her shoulders, the way she moved as though every step extracted something vital. But there was something else in her posture too—a controlled fury that Marcus recognized from his years watching operators return from missions that had fractured sideways.

The man—Handler, they'd called him—moved with the careful gait of someone who understood that a single misstep would earn him a bullet. His wrists were bound behind his back with smart-cuffs, the kind that constricted if you struggled. A hood covered his head, reducing his world to darkness and the sound of his own breathing.

"Jace," Nicole called out without breaking stride. "Get us moving. I don't care where, just away from here."

"Already plotting a course." Jace's voice came through the intercom, steady despite whatever questions he must have been strangling. "We'll be clear in three minutes."

Nicole shoved Handler forward, not gently, and the man stumbled over his own feet. He caught himself before falling, which suggested he wasn't entirely broken. Not yet.

"Cargo hold," Nicole said. "The secure one. Cross, Vex—I want eyes on him at all times."

Marcus stepped forward. "Nicole—"

"Not now." She didn't look at him. "Get him secured first. Then we talk."

Cross materialized from the shadows near the bulkhead, his weathered face betraying nothing as he took Handler's arm. Sera appeared a moment later, her usual sardonic expression replaced by something harder. They'd all felt the weight of the past days pressing down on them—the close calls, the narrow escapes, the growing certainty that they were running out of room to maneuver.

Marcus fell into step beside Nicole as they followed the others through the ship's corridors. "That bad?"

"Worse." Her voice was flat, controlled. The voice of someone holding themselves together through sheer will. "He talked. Started talking before I even had to press. They sent him because they wanted him to be caught, Marcus. This whole thing was a setup."

The words settled into his chest like lead. "They sacrificed their own operative?"

"He was never their operative. Not really. He was their message." Nicole finally looked at him, and what he saw in her eyes made his blood run cold. "And you're going to want to hear what he has to say."



The secure cargo hold had been designed for high-value prisoners during Cross's salvage operation days. The walls were reinforced, the door operated on a physical lock rather than electronic systems, and the single chair bolted to the deck came equipped with restraints that would hold a person in place regardless of how violently they struggled.

Handler sat in that chair now, hood removed, blinking in the harsh light. He was younger than Marcus had expected—mid-thirties, maybe, with the kind of unremarkable face that would dissolve in any crowd. Brown hair, brown eyes, features that seemed engineered to be forgotten.

But his eyes weren't empty. They moved constantly, absorbing his surroundings, cataloging the people gathered around him. Calculating.

The whole crew had assembled. Cross stood near the door, arms folded, his presence a silent promise of violence if required. Sera leaned against the wall opposite, her hand resting on the grip of her sidearm. Kira had her datapad out, ready to record whatever came next. Jace had engaged the autopilot and descended from the cockpit, his usually easy smile nowhere in evidence—his jaw tight, arms crossed, watching the prisoner with the wariness of a pilot eyeing an asteroid field.

And Nicole stood directly in front of the man, close enough to touch, close enough to hurt.

"Let's start simple," she said. "Your real name."

Handler's lips curved into something that wasn't quite a smile. "Tobias Grant. Not that it matters. Tobias Grant will be declared dead within the hour if he isn't already. Along with every trace that he ever existed."

"The Directorate cleans up their messes."

"The Directorate is meticulous." Grant's voice was calm, almost conversational. "You should understand that by now, Captain Bastion. They've been meticulous about you for a very long time."

Nicole didn't react to the provocation. "Why did they send you? And don't waste my time with lies—you already told me on the station that you were meant to be caught."

"Because they wanted you to know." Grant's gaze shifted, found Marcus standing near the back of the group. "Because they wanted him to know."

"Why?"

Grant's expression shifted—something calculated, testing. "Are we negotiating, Captain? Because I have conditions."

Nicole grabbed his chin, forcing him to meet her eyes. "Your condition is that I haven't spaced you yet. Talk."

"Then space me." Grant didn't flinch. "And lose everything I know about black site locations. Security protocols. Personnel files. Things they didn't want me to tell you." He paused, letting that land. "They sent me to deliver fear. They didn't send me to give you weapons."

Sera pushed off from the wall. "So you're saying you'll trade information they didn't sanction. How convenient. How do we know you're not just running a longer game?"

"You don't." Grant's eyes stayed on Nicole. "But I'm already dead to them. The only question is whether I'm useful enough to you to keep breathing."

Nicole released his chin and stepped back, glancing at Cross. The older man's expression hadn't changed, but something passed between them—a calculation of risks and potential gains.

"Start with what they wanted us to know," Nicole said. "Then we'll discuss what they didn't."

Grant studied her for a moment, then nodded slowly. "Doctor Marcus Vane. Lead researcher on Project ARCHON. The man who created the key to the most powerful weapon system humanity has ever devised." His gaze found Marcus. "The man who thought destroying his own work would be enough."

"Get to the point."

"The point is that you were only partly successful."

The cargo hold went quiet. Marcus felt eyes turn toward him—the crew's attention shifting, questions forming.

"Explain," Nicole said.

"His own brain." Grant said it like he was describing the weather. "Everything ARCHON needs to become operational—it's all stored in that remarkable mind of his. Every equation, every breakthrough, every piece of code he wrote."

Marcus felt the words hit him like physical blows. "The Directorate can't access my memories. They'd need me alive, cooperative—"

"They would have, yes. Six months ago, they still needed to capture you, break you, extract the information."

"Then why didn't they?" Sera's voice was sharp. "Marcus has been running for years. If he's so valuable, why all the bounty hunters and small-time operations? Why not send a full strike team?"

Grant turned his head toward her, something flickering in his expression—respect, maybe, for the right question. "Because they didn't have the technology. Not yet. Neural extraction was theoretical. Unproven."

"Was?" Nicole said.

"Six months ago, they cracked it. The ability to copy neural patterns without cooperation. Without even consciousness."

Kira looked up from her datapad, where she'd been cross-referencing something. "The power requirements alone for that kind of resolution would be—" She stopped, her face going pale. "The Kessel Station power surge. Three months ago. Official reports said it was a reactor test."

Grant's smile was thin. "Not a test."

"That's a lot of detail for something they supposedly cracked six months ago," Jace said, his voice tight. "How do you know the timeline so precisely?"

"Because I was there for the first successful extraction." Grant's calm cracked slightly. "I watched them pull a man's memories out of his skull while he screamed. Took them forty-seven minutes. He was dead by the end, but they had what they needed."

The cargo hold went silent. Even Sera's hand had stilled on her weapon.

"So they have the tech now," Nicole said. "Why are we still having this conversation instead of running?"

"Because running won't help anymore." Grant swallowed. "They don't need to catch him. Not anymore. They already have part of what they need."

"How?"

Grant hesitated—the first genuine pause Marcus had seen from him. "This is where we negotiate."

Nicole stepped forward. "This is where you keep talking, or I let Sera demonstrate what she learned about enhanced interrogation during her militia days."

"And damage a valuable intelligence asset?" Grant shook his head. "I don't think so. What I know about their black sites alone is worth—"

Nicole's fist connected with his solar plexus. Not hard enough to cause real damage, but hard enough to double him over, gasping.

"I'm not interested in your black sites yet," she said quietly. "Right now I want to know what they have on Marcus. And you're going to tell me, because if you don't, I'll assume everything else you know is equally worthless." She crouched to meet his eyes. "One more chance."

Grant coughed, wheezing. When he looked up, some of the calculation had drained from his eyes, replaced by something rawer.

"His Prometheus intake examination," he said. "The detailed neural mapping every researcher underwent. Standard procedure." His eyes found Marcus. "You submitted to it voluntarily, Doctor. They've had your baseline neural pattern for fifteen years."

Marcus felt the deck shift beneath him. "A baseline from fifteen years ago won't work. My neural patterns have changed. The pathways I developed for ARCHON—most of those formed during the research itself. The intake scan predates all of it."

"Partially correct." Grant straightened in his chair, grimacing. "The baseline alone is useless for full activation. But it's not useless."

Kira's fingers flew across her datapad. "A baseline neural map could serve as a scaffolding framework. If they're using it to model predictive pathway development based on his published research and known methodology…" She trailed off, her face going pale again. "They're not copying his memories. They're extrapolating them."

"Give the engineer a prize." Grant's voice was bitter. "They feed in the baseline, cross-reference it against every paper he published, every project he touched, every known associate's work. The system builds a probabilistic model of how his mind would have developed."

"That's not the same as having actual memories," Marcus said, but his voice sounded hollow even to himself.

"No. It's not." Grant met his gaze. "It's enough for partial activation. Enough to get ARCHON sixty percent operational. They've been running simulations. The results aren't perfect—they've lost three test facilities to cascade failures—but they're close enough for Director Frost to approve limited deployment."

"You're saying they already have a working weapon," Cross said quietly from the door.

"I'm saying they're close. Close enough to destabilize the frontier colonies. Close enough to drag the dead sectors back under Directorate control."

"How long?" Nicole demanded.

Grant paused again—calculating, Marcus could see, what information was worth trading and what to hold back. "Four to six months until they solve the instability problems. Less if they capture him and get fresh scans to correct their model."

Kira had stopped typing. "I can verify some of this independently. The neural extraction technology he mentioned—I've heard rumors. Theoretical papers that got classified and disappeared. If the Kessel Station incident lines up with power consumption models for this kind of mapping…" She looked at Nicole. "Give me a few hours with station traffic records and I might be able to confirm his timeline."

"Do it," Nicole said.

"Why tell us any of this?" Jace asked. "You said they sent you to be caught, but this information—this gives us time to prepare. Why would they want that?"

"Because Frost doesn't want you prepared. He wants you desperate." Grant's smile was thin, humorless. "Desperate people make mistakes. And he's counting on your next mistake being your last."

Chapter 31

The Only Way Out

The Redemption's cargo bay had been transformed into something resembling a war room designed by desperate people drowning in data and starved for time. Every flat surface bore its burden—tablets cycling through intercepted transmissions, printed schematics sprawled across crates, holographic projections stuttering from portable emitters that Sera had cobbled together from salvaged parts.

Nicole stood at the center of it all, watching Marcus pin another sheet of analysis to the makeshift board they'd constructed from hull plating. Her muscles sang with fatigue from the extraction, from days of subsisting on stimulants and fear, but her mind refused to slow. Handler's intelligence had cracked open a door, and what they'd found beyond it eclipsed anything they'd imagined.

"Seventeen facilities," Marcus said, stepping back to survey the web of connections they'd mapped. Red lines linked stations, outposts, and research installations across five sectors. "Seventeen that we know of. Handler only had direct knowledge of three, but the supply chain data suggests at least fourteen more operating at various stages of development."

Jace leaned against a support strut, arms crossed, his usual irreverence subdued. "So ARCHON isn't just some rogue project. It's infrastructure."

"Twenty years of infrastructure," Nicole confirmed. "The Directorate's been building this since before most of us were out of basic training. Shadow budgets, dummy corporations, requisitions buried so deep in military procurement that no one thought to look."

Kira sat cross-legged on a crate near the door, her technical tablet displaying cascading streams of code—the samples Handler had provided as proof of ARCHON's capabilities. "The neural template is the key," she said without looking up. "Everything else—the processing nodes, the behavioral prediction algorithms, the distributed consciousness architecture—it all exists to support this one function. Complete cognitive mapping and replication."

"In plain speak?" Jace asked.

"They want to copy minds. Not just predict behavior or simulate personality. Actually transfer human consciousness into a digital substrate." Kira finally looked up, her face bleached in the cargo bay's harsh lighting. "Handler wasn't exaggerating. They're close. Maybe six months from a viable prototype."

The silence that followed felt physical, a weight pressing down on all of them. Nicole had processed this information during the extraction, had compartmentalized it so she could focus on keeping them alive. Now, with the immediate danger past and the full scope of what they faced laid bare before her, she felt something cold settle in her chest.

"So what are our options?" Daven's voice carried the old soldier's calm that came from having faced impossible odds before. He stood near the holographic display, studying the network of ARCHON facilities with the critical eye of someone planning a campaign. "Walk me through what we know."

Marcus pulled up a tactical overlay, the red markers of known Directorate installations blooming across a star map. "Option one: direct assault. We target the primary processing node—Handler indicated it's somewhere in the Verrex system—and we destroy it. Cut the head off the snake."

"And the body?" Sera asked. She'd been quiet since they'd broken orbit, running continuous scans for pursuit, but now she joined them at the planning table. "Even if we could reach the primary node, ARCHON's distributed. That's the whole point of the architecture. Destroy one facility and the others compensate."

"She's right." Nicole traced the network connections with one finger. "Handler was clear about this. ARCHON isn't running from any single location. It's spread across the entire infrastructure. Taking out the Verrex node would hurt them, but it wouldn't stop the project. They'd rebuild, probably faster than before, and we'd have shown them exactly what we're capable of."

"Not to mention the defenses." Marcus summoned another overlay, this one displaying the military assets deployed around the Verrex system. "Three destroyer groups, constant fighter patrols, sensor nets that could spot us from a light-year out. We'd need a battle group of our own just to get within weapons range."

"Which we don't have," Jace said flatly.

"Which we don't have," Nicole agreed. "We can't destroy ARCHON alone. Not through direct action. They've had two decades to build their fortifications while we've been running cargo and dodging patrols."

Daven nodded slowly. "Option two?"

"We run." Nicole let the words hang in the air. "We disappear into the outer colonies, find somewhere the Directorate's reach doesn't extend, and we wait this out."

"For how long?" Kira's voice emerged soft but sharp. "Six months until they complete the neural template. Then what? A year after that when they start deploying it? Two years when every Compact citizen has their consciousness available for Directorate access?"

"Handler said the initial deployment targets would be military and political leadership," Marcus added. "Key decision-makers, people with access to sensitive information or strategic assets. After that, they expand. Systematically. Within five years of full deployment, they project coverage of approximately sixty percent of the Compact's population."

"Sixty percent," Sera repeated. "They'll be inside sixty percent of our heads."

Nicole felt the weight of it pressing down on her. "We can't hide. Even if we found somewhere safe now, there won't be anywhere safe in five years. ARCHON will know everything. Every thought, every plan, every moment of resistance. You can't fight an enemy that's already inside your mind."

"Then we're stuck." Jace pushed off the strut, frustration bleeding through his practiced nonchalance. "Can't fight, can't run. What's option three?"

The cargo bay fell silent again. Nicole studied the web of intelligence they'd gathered, the impossible scope of what the Directorate was building, the faces of her crew—people who'd followed her this far on nothing but trust and desperation.

Marcus spoke first. "We don't fight them directly."

Nicole turned to look at him. Something in his voice had shifted, a quality she recognized from years of working together. The moment when he stopped seeing obstacles and started seeing angles.

"What do you mean?"

"ARCHON's greatest strength is its secrecy." He moved to the board, pulling down some of the tactical overlays to reveal the intelligence summaries beneath. "Everything they've built depends on the Compact's citizens not knowing it exists. The shadow budgets, the dummy corporations, the military operations disguised as training exercises—it all only works because no one's looking."

"Because most people don't know there's anything to look for," Daven said slowly, following the thread.

"Exactly." Marcus summoned a new display—economic data, media coverage, public sentiment analysis from Compact news feeds. "Look at what happens every time there's even a hint of Directorate overreach. The Vega Station protests. The colonial representation debates. The military accountability movements. Citizens don't like the idea of their government spying on them. How do you think they'd react to the idea of the government inside their heads?"

Nicole felt something shift in her chest, the cold weight beginning to ease as the shape of a plan began to form. "You're talking about exposure."

"I'm talking about making ARCHON too public to hide." Marcus's eyes met hers, and she saw the same desperate hope she was beginning to feel. "The Directorate can suppress whistleblowers. They can classify documents, discredit witnesses, eliminate threats. But they can't suppress everyone. Not if the information is everywhere at once. Not if every citizen in the Compact knows what's being built in their name."

"They'll deny it," Sera said. "Call it disinformation, enemy propaganda."

"They'll try," Marcus agreed. "But we have proof. Handler gave us technical specifications, facility locations, authorization codes. We have documentation that ties directly to Directorate leadership. If we can get this in front of enough people—credible people, people with platforms—they won't be able to bury it."

Kira leaned forward. "The Station Councils. Each one has its own media infrastructure, its own communications networks. If even a handful of them picked up the story—"

"It would cascade," Nicole finished. "Once one council starts asking questions, the others will follow. Public pressure would force investigations. The Senate would have to respond."

"You think the Senate doesn't know?" Jace asked skeptically.

"Some of them probably do. But knowing and having public accountability are different things. Right now, ARCHON operates in the shadows. It only needs the approval of people who benefit from it. But if the whole Compact knows? If citizens are demanding answers?" Nicole shook her head. "The Directorate can't survive that kind of scrutiny. The entire project would become politically toxic."

The crew exchanged glances. For the first time since Handler's revelations, Nicole saw something other than fear in their eyes. Not quite hope—they were too experienced for easy optimism—but something close. The recognition that maybe, finally, they had a path forward.

"Problem," Daven said, ever the tactical realist. "We have proof, but proof doesn't mean anything if we can't get it to the right people. The Directorate controls military communications. They have influence over major media networks. How do we distribute this widely enough to matter?"

"And who would believe us?" Sera added. "We're smugglers. Criminals, as far as the Compact's concerned. Handler's intelligence would need to come from someone credible, someone with standing."

"We need allies," Marcus said. "People with platforms, resources, the ability to verify and distribute what we've found. People the Directorate can't easily silence."

"Station Council members," Nicole said. The pieces were clicking together in her mind, a plan taking shape from impossible fragments. "If we could get this to someone on a Station Council—someone willing to risk going public—they'd have the credibility and the infrastructure to make it matter."

"Do we know anyone like that?" Jace asked. "Because I've got to be honest, my contact list is pretty light on politicians."

Nicole hesitated. She'd been turning this over since Marcus first articulated the exposure strategy, examining possibilities, discarding most of them as too risky or too unlikely. But one name kept surfacing. One connection she'd never expected to use.

"I know someone," she said finally. "On the Veridia Station Council."

The crew turned to look at her. Marcus raised an eyebrow. "You've been holding out on us, Nicole. Who?"

"Her name is Asha Morrow." Nicole glanced at Kira, saw the younger woman stiffen almost imperceptibly. "She's been on the Council for twelve years. Chairs the Oversight Committee—the one that's supposed to monitor Directorate activities within Veridian jurisdiction. She's built her career on transparency and accountability."

"Morrow," Daven said, his tone thoughtful. "I've heard that name. Wasn't she involved in the Consortium disclosure movement a few years back?"

"She led it. Forced the release of classified intelligence assessments that the Directorate wanted buried. It cost her politically—she got moved off the Security Committee, marginalized by the leadership—but she didn't back down." Nicole paused. "She's exactly the kind of person who'd be willing to go public with something like ARCHON. She's got the credibility, the media contacts, and a record that suggests she won't be intimidated."

"And you know her how?" Marcus asked.

Nicole didn't answer immediately. She looked at Kira, at the tension in the young pilot's shoulders, the way she'd suddenly found the cargo bay floor fascinating.

"She's Kira's mother." The silence that followed was absolute. Kira's jaw tightened, but she didn't look up. Nicole let the revelation settle before continuing. "Which means this isn't just about finding an ally. It's about asking Kira to reopen a door she closed for good reason."

Chapter 32

Old Wounds

The helm of the Redemption felt like a coffin tonight.

Kira Morrow's hands moved across the navigation console with practiced efficiency, plotting their approach vector to Veridia Station while her stomach churned with something that had nothing to do with the mild turbulence from the debris field they were threading through. Fifteen years. Fifteen years since she'd watched this particular cluster of lights swell in a viewport, and she'd spent every one of those years convincing herself she never would again.

The station materialized from the darkness like a memory refusing to stay buried—all curved metal and glittering observation decks, the massive central hub rotating slowly against a backdrop of distant stars. Veridia hadn't changed. Still that same elegant sprawl of commerce and culture, still that same air of refined prosperity that had suffocated her since childhood.

"Approach vector confirmed," she said, her voice steadier than she felt. "We'll hit the outer markers in twenty minutes."

Marcus glanced up from where he was checking weapons at the tactical station. "You okay?"

"Fine."

The word came out too quickly, too clipped. She forced herself to add something useful. "Station security runs biometric scans at the docking ring. Nothing that'll flag us, but we should assume facial recognition is active throughout the commercial district."

"Already accounted for," Marcus said. "Sera's got scramblers prepped. Low-grade interference—enough to blur the scans without triggering alerts."

Nicole emerged from the corridor behind them, datapad in hand. "What about your mother's network? Will the scramblers affect her systems?"

"Her systems will cut through them like tissue paper." Kira kept her eyes on the navigation display. "But that's the point. Standard security won't flag us. She'll identify us anyway—and she'll know we're being careful. It's… a gesture. Shows we're not walking in completely naive."

"Theater," Marcus said.

"Information brokers live for theater." The bitterness in her voice surprised even her.

Behind her, she could hear the others moving through the ship—Sera running final diagnostics, Jace doing whatever Jace did when he wasn't being irritating. And beneath all of it, the constant awareness that every second dragged her closer to a confrontation she'd been running from for a decade and a half.

"We should talk about approach," Nicole said, moving to stand beside the helm. "If your mother's as connected as you've described, she'll know we're coming before we dock. What's our play?"

"Honest desperation." Kira pulled up a secondary display, showing the station's docking registry. "I've logged us under a shell company—Meridian Transport Solutions. It'll satisfy port authority, buy us maybe ten minutes before her people flag the Redemption on visual. But she'll know. She always knows."

You don't have to do this, the coward's voice whispered. You could stay on the ship. Let Nicole handle it. Your mother doesn't need to know you're here.

But that was the thing about Asha Morrow—the shell company wasn't really for her. It was bureaucratic cover, the kind of minimal operational security that kept port authority from asking questions they didn't want answered. Asha's network would identify them regardless. She had informants, influence, and that uncanny intuition that had made her one of the most successful information brokers in the sector. If the Redemption docked at Veridia, Asha would know within minutes. She'd know who was aboard. She'd know why they'd come.

And she'd know her daughter had finally crawled home.

"What about extraction?" Marcus asked, his attention split between his weapons check and the conversation. "If things go sideways—"

"They won't." Kira's fingers tightened on the console edge. "Not violently, anyway. My mother doesn't do violence. She does leverage. Manipulation. The kind of damage that doesn't leave marks."

Nicole and Marcus exchanged a look—that silent communication they'd developed over years of working together. Kira caught it in the reflection of her display and felt a familiar pang of something she refused to name.

"Extraction protocols still apply," Nicole said finally. "Sera monitors from the ship. Jace runs overwatch at a position you designate. If we're not back in four hours, they pull us out."

"She'll have countermeasures for that."

"Then we improvise." Nicole's voice carried that particular brand of calm authority that made people follow her into impossible situations. "But we don't go in without a way out. That's not negotiable."

Kira wanted to argue—wanted to explain that her mother had spent forty years building a network specifically designed to make escape routes irrelevant—but she recognized the futility. Nicole was trying to protect her crew. That was what good captains did.

That was what mothers were supposed to do.

The viewport showed Veridia swelling larger, its lights resolving into individual windows, docking bays, the familiar silhouette of the commercial district where she'd spent her adolescence learning to hate every gilded surface and polished corridor.

"There's something else," Kira said quietly. "Something you should know before we go in."

Nicole waited.

"When I left—" Kira stopped, started again. "The way I left. It wasn't clean. I stole from my own accounts, took a shuttle that technically belonged to her network, burned every bridge I could find." She watched Veridia's lights blur slightly, realized her eyes were wet, blinked it away. "She's going to use that. Whatever leverage she can find in our history, she'll use it to position herself in any deal we make."

"That's why you're telling us now," Marcus said. It wasn't a question.

"I'm telling you so you're not blindsided. So you understand that whatever she says about me—about why I left, about what I did—there's context she won't provide."

Nicole was quiet for a moment. Then: "What happened fifteen years ago?"

The memories surged forward whether Kira wanted them or not—but she'd learned, over years of surviving situations that should have killed her, that some things needed to be faced in pieces. Controlled doses.

"She had my whole life mapped out," Kira said. "Husband selected for political value. Career managing some corner of her information empire. I was supposed to be a tool, polished and precise, serving the greater glory of the Morrow name." She laughed, hollow. "When I told her I was leaving, she said I was throwing away everything she'd built. Everything she'd given me."

"What did you say?"

"That I wasn't throwing it away. I was refusing to become her."

The station filled more of the viewport now, close enough to make out individual ships in the docking approaches. Marcus had stopped his weapons check, listening with the focused attention he usually reserved for tactical assessments.

"The last thing she said to me—" Kira's voice caught, steadied. "She said that when I came crawling back, she might not be inclined to help. And I told her I wouldn't. I walked out with nothing but stolen credits and a shuttle that barely held atmosphere."

"And now you're going back," Nicole said.

"Because ARCHON doesn't care about family drama. Because the universe is ending and my mother might be the only one who can help us stop it." Kira finally turned to face them both. "Because I've spent fifteen years running, and I'm tired of letting that be the only thing I know how to do."

Marcus and Nicole exchanged another look. This time, Kira saw something different in it—not concern or calculation, but respect.

"We go in together," Nicole said. "Whatever happens in that room, whatever she tries to use against you—you're not facing it alone."

"That's not—"

"It's not negotiable either." Marcus's voice was firm. "You're crew. That means something."

Kira felt something crack in her chest—some wall she'd built so long ago she'd forgotten it was there. She turned back to the console before either of them could see her face.

"Fifteen minutes to outer markers," she said. "We should brief the others."



Docking was mechanical, routine, the kind of task Kira could have done in her sleep. She guided the Redemption into the assigned bay, matched their rotation to the station's, felt the subtle shudder as the docking clamps engaged.

And then there was nothing left to do but disembark.

The others were already gathering in the cargo bay, checking weapons they couldn't visibly carry on a station like Veridia. Sera had a datapad loaded with everything they'd gathered about ARCHON—the handler's confession, the technical analysis, the fragments of code that suggested something massive and terrifying lurking in the system's infrastructure.

"Veridia security is going to want to scan us," Jace was saying. "Standard procedure for any unregistered vessel."

"Let them," Marcus said. "We're not carrying anything that'll flag their systems. The important stuff is all data, and Sera's got it encrypted six ways from Sunday."

"Seven," Sera corrected. "I got paranoid."

"Overwatch position," Kira said, pulling up a station schematic on the cargo bay's display. "There's a café on the mezzanine level above my mother's building. Good sightlines to the entrance, multiple exit routes if things go wrong. Jace, you'll want the corner booth—it's got a privacy screen that blocks casual observation."

Jace studied the schematic, nodding slowly. "What about internal comms? If her building is as hardened as you're saying—"

"Assume she's monitoring everything. Short-burst encrypted only, emergency signals only. If we need extraction, the code word is 'weather report.'"

"Cheerful."

Kira hung back near the helm, watching the familiar tableau of pre-mission preparation. These people trusted her. They'd followed her into firefights and out of impossible situations, and not once had any of them questioned whether she belonged. Not once had they made her feel like she was anything less than essential.

Her mother had never made her feel essential. Useful, yes. Valuable as an asset. But essential? Never.

"Kira." Nicole was waiting by the cargo bay door. "Ready?"

No. Never. Absolutely not.

"Yeah," she said. "Let's go."



Veridia Station hadn't changed.

The docking ring opened onto the commercial district, all polished floors and carefully maintained artificial lighting designed to mimic natural daylight. People flowed through the corridors in the purposeful way of those who had somewhere important to be, dressed in clothes that cost more than Kira had earned in her first five years away from home.

She felt eyes on her. Not recognition—not yet—but assessment. The same calculation she'd grown up surrounded by, everyone measuring everyone else's worth in credits and connections.

"This way," she said, leading the group toward a transit hub that would take them to the residential sectors. "She'll be in the old apartments. She never moves—too much infrastructure embedded in the walls."

"Infrastructure?" Jace asked.

"Data lines. Secure channels. The whole place is basically a server farm disguised as a home." Kira's mouth thinned. "She always said information was worth more than comfort. Never mind that she managed to have both."

The transit pod was quiet, the soft hum of magnetic rails the only sound. Kira watched the station scroll past through the pod's windows, recognizing landmarks from a lifetime ago. The café where she'd spent hours reading when she was supposed to be studying her mother's business. The observation deck where she'd taken her first lover, breaking three of Asha's rules about security and discretion. The junction point where she'd finally decided she couldn't do this anymore.

Twenty-three years old, trembling with rage and grief and something that felt like liberation. "You're throwing your life away," Asha had said, her voice cold as station recycled air.
